The children of William and Martha Jane Browder were Louis (b. c1876), Bessie (b. Aug. 1878), Frederick J. (b. c1881), Minnie Gwendolyn (Mar. 1883-Jan. 14, 1963), Nina Winona (Sept. 29, 1885-Dec. 2, 1975), Ralph Waldo “Willie” E. (b. c1888), Earl Russell Browder (May 20, 1891-June 27, 1973), and Anna Margurite M. (b. c1893). All children were born in Wichita, Sedgwick County, Kansas.

Bessie Browder, daughter of William and Martha Jane Browder, married John C. Lowry (b. Sept. 1874).

Minnie Gwendolyn Browder, daughter of William and Martha Jane Browder, married (1) Joseph Webber (b. c1879), and (2) Frank Earl Long (Sept. 19, 1882-June 21, 1942).

Nina Winona Browder, daughter of William and Martha Jane Browder, married Bert Alvin Turner (Feb. 10, 1886-June 7, 1959).

Anna Margurite M. Browder, daughter of William and Martha Jane Browder, married unknown Sullivan.

Earl Russell Browder (the son of a teacher and the grandson of a Methodist minister) was born in Wichita, Sedgwick County Kansas and died at Princeton, Mercer County, New Jersey, having moved late in his life to Yonkers, Westchester County, New York. 

Earl left school before the age of ten (at the third grade) due to the illness of his father.  Earl then worked to help support the family with his brother.  Earl’s father, a Populist and later a Socialist (like many Kansans at that time), encouraged political discussions within the family (Olson, 625).  This was formative for young Earl. 

Earl moved to Kansas City, Kansas while in his twenties, where, in 1907, he joined the Socialist Party and, in 1914, completed a correspondence course in law.  He left the Socialist Party in 1912, thereafter associating himself with the Syndicalist League of North America, led by William Z. Foster, at which time he served as associate editor of the Toiler, a syndicalist magazine (ibid, 625).  He was later elected president of the local Bookkeepers, Stenographers and Accountants Union (Whitman, 3).

Earl Browder was a political activist, who was imprisoned for his opposition to United States participation in WWI.  He was first arrested in 1917 for “conspiring to defeat operation of the draft law and for non-registration.”  For the second charge he spent a year in prison.  He rejoined the Socialist Party when he was released from prison and became the first editor of “The Worker’s World”, a pro-Communist paper which circulated in Kansas, in 1919 (ibid, 3).  

He was later imprisoned at Leavenworth Federal Prison for a total of sixteen months, from 1919-1920, on a charge of “conspiracy” (but later pardoned in 1933).  He was made an honorary charter member of the new Communist Party while in prison and became active in the party in 1921 upon moving to New York City (Olson, 625). 

In his book, “The Communist Party of the U.S.A., Its History, Role and Organization”, Earl Browder wrote: “The Communist Party [in America] was born in Chicago on September 1, 1919” (Whitman, 8).  This nostalgic reminiscence well illustrates Browder’s fondness for the Communist Party in America at that time.  

Browder later also served 14 months in a prison in Atlanta for certain passport irregularities (sentence commuted in 1940).  Having joined the American Communist Party, he served as director of the Pan-Pacific Trade Union Secretariat in Shanghai, China.  There, he helped to organize the Communist drive in China. 

He and Mr. Foster, whom he worked with extensively during his early years in the party, met Lenin for the first time in 1921 at the first Congress of the Red International of Labor Unions (the Profintern) in Moscow.  In 1926 the Profintern sent Earl Browder, as one member of a delegation, to China.  There he helped form the Pan Pacific Trade Union Secretariat.  Mr. Browder then served as Secretary of this organization.  After this stint was completed, he returned to the United States in 1929 (Whitman, 3).

Later, due to factional strife among two divergent groups, the Trotskyites and the Lovestones, a special Comintern Commission, under Stalin, was called in Moscow to put it to an end.  During this meeting, Mr. Lovestone was dismissed, and the party leadership passed to Earl Browder due to the fact that Mr. Lovestone and William Z. Foster had had greater roles in the previous factional strife than had Browder.  However, Browder said he never really trusted Stalin, whom he was only acquainted with via this one meeting (ibid, 4).  

Earl Russell Browder married Raissa Borisbova Berkman (1897-Jan. 8, 1955), of Russia, in 1926.  Afterward he served as General Secretary of the Communist Party of the United States from 1930-1946.  In 1930, he was the Communist Party candidate for the U.S. House of Representatives from New York, 6th District. 

Earl Russell Browder and Raissa Borisbova Berkman became divorced, after which he married Gladys Grove (1891-1981). They were also divorced and Earl later married Catherine “Kitty” Harris (Mar. 25, 1899-Oct. 6, 1966).

Earl Browder was the Communist Party candidate for the Presidency of the United States in 1936, and again in 1940.  He lost all of these political contests.  Even so, in 1932, he had been the first U.S. Presidential candidate in history to select an African-American, James W. Ford, as his running-mate.  Ford later continued to work as a Communist Party organizer from 1936 to 1940.

Earl Browder served as General Secretary of the Communist Party in the United States during the years of its largest membership and greatest influence.  However, by 1935, he began backing down from the party’s former revolutionary line in the interest of world peace, and urged cooperation with the socialists and other groups, who were demanding more radical change.  Browder and the Communist Party backed the “New Deal” and Roosevelt, beginning in 1935, until the Soviet-German peace pact was signed in 1939, and the outbreak of World War II ensued (ibid, 4).

As Communist Party candidate for president in 1936, Browder had visited 26 states, traveled 26,000 miles, and received 80,160 votes.  This was the most votes he would ever receive.  It was in 1941 that Browder was later convicted of unlawful use of his passport, and sentenced to four years in prison, following a series of appeals (Olson, 625).

By June of 1941, the Communist Party changed course and began to support the war effort because of the invasion of Hitler’s forces into the Soviet Union.  In December of 1941, President Roosevelt commuted Mr. Browder’s sentence, and he was released from prison.  “I was released by President Roosevelt personally,” Mr. Browder said in a 1971 interview.  He called it a “political pardon” (Whitman, 4).

In 1943, the Communist Party in the United States was officially dissolved, due to Browder’s influence, after which the Communist Political Association was formed in 1944, with Browder as its leader.  This, however, proved unsatisfactory to many American Communists.  One in particular, Jacues Duclos, a French columnist, was infuriated!  In 1945, he published an open letter in which he criticized Browder, calling him a “right deviationist” because Browder had dissolved the American Communist Party.  What followed was a fierce campaign to unseat Browder from within the ranks of the American party. 

Browder, however, continued to believe that the letter was a hoax, which had actually been written in Moscow.  Of this incident, Earl Browder stated in the 1970 interview: “To be a dictator, Stalin required a respectable opponent.  Hitler was gone, and he had to be replaced.  It had to be the United States, the leading capitalist country.  Stalin picked a quarrel, and I was the victim of it.  Of course, he had plenty of provocation…” (ibid, 4-5).

Because of these events, Earl Browder was expelled from the Communist Party in 1946.  In 1949 he was named in “treason trials” in Budapest and Prague as the originator of “the heresy of Browderism” (Britannica, “Browderism”, 556).  This “heresy” is defined as the belief, held by Browder, that Communism and Capitalism could coexist in the same world peacefully without the one destroying the other.  This was an idea which Moscow could not tolerate.  

However, even after this devastating blow, Browder was appointed Soviet Publishing Representative in the United States, where he continued to serve until 1950, when he resigned because, he said, “to the embarrassment of the American Communist Party, he could not make enough money” from this job (Whitman, 5).  Thus, it would seem that Earl Browder fist rose from a youth of little education, to the highest position in the Communist Party in America, and later fell from that position due to his multiple imprisonments and, in the end, due to one man, Joseph Stalin. 

Earl Russell Browder died in Princeton, Mercer County, New Jersey on June 27, 1973.  His burial place is undisclosed. 

The children of Earl Browder and Raissa Berkman were Felix Earl (Jul 31, 1927-Dec. 10, 2016), and Andrew (Jan. 8, 1931-Mar. 24, 2019). The child of Earl Browder and Gladys Grove was William E. (Jan. 6, 1934-2011).

David Browder (c1755-c1813), son of Richard and Mary Browder, married (1) Mary Pegram, who died only about two years after their marriage, and later (2) Mary Cousins (daughter of Henry Cousins [believed to have been a native of England] and “Ms. Thompson”).  Mary Cousins (d. c1818) is said to have, in fact, been David’s cousin (Browder, Thomas E., p. 1).   

The booklet by Thomas E. Browder, “Genealogy of David Browder, of Virginia and His Descendants”, is referring directly to this David Browder in the title.  The addition to this book, added by David Parrish Browder in 1913, provides the names of Richard and Mary Browder’s known children, as far as David P. Browder could ascertain at that time. 

David Browder paid taxes in Dinwiddie County, Virginia in 1782.  He also owned four slaves in 1782.  In 1787 he was taxed on 100 acres and 46 acres of land located in Dinwiddie County, Virginia, conveyed to him by Peter Pritchett.  In 1788 and 1789 he was taxed on 95 acres and 46 acres of land there.  In 1790 he was taxed on 95, 46 and 60 acres of land there, the latter conveyed to him by Daniel Mayes.  In 1791 he was taxed on 95, 46, 60¾ and 204 acres of land there, the latter conveyed to him by William Trebue. 

Also, in 1791 he was taxed on 84 acres of land there conveyed to him by Martha Hardy.  In 1792 he was taxed on 95, 46, 60¾ and 84 acres of land there.  In 1793 he was taxed on 54 acres of land there.  From 1794 to 1804 he was taxed on 95, 46, 60¾, 84 and 54 acres of land there.  In 1805 he was also taxed on an additional 168 acres and 47¼ acres of land there, conveyed to him by William Elder, Jr.  In 1806 he was taxed on an additional 100 acres of land there conveyed to him by Hezekiel Spain.  In 1807 he was taxed on 54 acres of land missing and an additional 200 acres of land there. 

From 1809 to 1812 he was taxed on 96, 46, 60¾, 84, 200, 168, 47¼ and 100 acres of land there.  In 1813 he was taxed on 285¾, 247¼ and 366 acres of land there.  In 1814 his estate began to be taxed on 533 and 386 acres of land there.  From 1815-1817 his estate was taxed on 533 and 464 acres of land there.  In 1818 his estate was taxed on 533 acres as well as 264 acres of land, the latter being conveyed to his son, Richard Browder.  In 1819 his estate was taxed on the same 533 and 264 acres of land.  And, finally, in 1820 his estate was taxed on 481 acres of land, all located on Hatcher’s Run (Hughes, p. 51).  His estate continued to be listed in the same records until 1819 while his sons continued to pay taxes on his property there, although they had already moved to Kentucky. 

By the time of his death, about 1813, David Browder owned nearly 1,000 acres of land on Hatchers Run and Whipponock Creeks in Dinwiddie County, having started out with only about 146 acres (Penland Browder, p. 31).  This made David one of the wealthiest members of the Browder family at that time.

The 1810 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists David Browder (45 and over) along with his wife (45 and over) and children; one male (16-25), one male (10-15), one male (under 10), one female (26-44), two females (16-25), and two females (10-15).  No slaves are listed.  Other families listed on the same page in this census included those of Nancy Booth, Jones Browder, John Booth, William Booth and Mary Bolling.  Several other names are unreadable. 

In 1818 the estate of Mary Browder, David’s widow, was taxed for 66½ acres of land on Hatcher’s Run in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  This land had been conveyed by John Birchett (Hughes, p. 52).

David Browder and Mary Pegram had one daughter, Mary Chapman (Feb. 21, 1781-July 21, 1829).  She married Anthony Sydnor (b. c1772). 

The known children of Anthony and Mary C. Sydnor were Joseph (b. c1801), David B. (b. c1803), Edward (b. c1805), Martha (b. c1807), Sarah Ann (b. c1809), Thomas A. (b. c1811), Baker (c1813-1833) and John T.

The known children of David Browder and Mary Cousins were Nancy Ann Wilmuth (c1783-June 17, 1847), Pvt. Richard (April 1, 1789-January 2, 1878), Jane (June 17, 1793-Oct. 28, 1836), Almeda (c1797-c1869), William Cousins (c1798-Feb. 9, 1847), and Robert W. (February 20, 1804-August 17, 1890).

Nancy Ann Wilmuth Browder, daughter of David and Mary Browder, married John Peay Moore, Sr. Their children were David B. (Nov. 16, 1816-1866), Mary J. (Nov. 10, 1818-Feb. 28, 1844), John Peay, Jr. (b. Sept. 9, 1821), Martha Ann (May 1, 1824-Nov. 22, 1886), and George N., Sr. (b. Jan. 6, 1827).

Pvt. Richard Browder (April 1, 1789-January 2, 1878), son of David Browder and Mary Cousins, served in the 83rd Regiment (Scott’s) Virginia Militia during the War of 1812 as a Private.  He married (1) Elizabeth Anderson on February 24, 1813 in Chesterfield County, Virginia, and later (2) Sarah.

From 1816-1817 Richard Browder was taxed on 140 acres of land, located in Dinwiddie County, Virginia, conveyed to him by Robert M. Smith.  From 1818 to 1820 he was again taxed on this 140 acres of land, located on Whipponock Creek as well as 200 acres of land, also located on Whipponock Creek, conveyed to him by the estate of his father, David Browder, through David’s will (Hughes, 52).  Richard Browder is also listed in the 1820 Dinwiddie County, Virginia tax list as owning five slaves.

The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists this Richard Browder (26-44) as well as his presumed wife (26-44) and presumed children; two males (under 10), and one female (under 10).  Also listed are nine male slaves and eight female slaves.  Also, in a separate listing of the 1820 census showing “persons engaged in manufacturing”, it notes that two persons within Richard’s household were blacksmiths.  Richard is listed on the same census page as William Booth, Williamson Browder and Isham Browder, Jr., among others. 

On April 9, 1821 Gustavus A. Muir, administrator of Francis Muir, deceased, sued John P. Moore (husband of Richard’s sister, Nancy) and Richard Browder in Dinwiddie County Circuit Superior Court over debts and won the lawsuit.  It is interesting to note that Richard Browder’s attorney was John W. Jones and his security was Kennon Jones (DCCSCR&J 1819-1841, p. 41). 

Also, on September 8, 1821, Gustavus A. Muir, administrator of Francis Muir, deceased, sued Kennon Jones and Richard Browder in Dinwiddie County Circuit Superior Court over debts and won the suit.  This time Richard Browder’s attorney was John F. May.  Within the text, Robert Hutchings (husband of Richard’s sister, Almeda) is also named as a co-defendant (ibid, p. 47).

Richard, William C., and Robert W. Browder all moved with their respective families to Green County, Kentucky about 1821, migrating from there at a later date to Logan County, Kentucky.

The 1830 census of Russellville, Logan County, Kentucky lists Richard Browder (40-49) along with his wife (40-49) and presumed children; one male (30-39), one male (10-14), one male (under 5), two females (10-14), one female (5-9), and one female (under 5).  Also listed are five male slaves and six female slaves.

The 1840 census of Logan County lists Richard Browder (50-59) along with his wife (40-49) and presumed children; one male (20-29), one male (10-14), one male (5-9), two males (under 5), one female (15-19), one female (10-14), and one female (under 5).  Also listed are eleven male slaves and six female slaves.

The 1850 census of Logan County (District 2) lists Richard Browder (62) along with Sarah Browder (45), and two certain sons, William (18), and Thomas (21), as well as James Boyer (20).  This census confirms that Richard was born in Virginia.  Obviously, Richard’s wife died prior to 1850. 

The 1860 census of Logan County (District 2) lists Richd. Browder (71), farmer, along with Sarah Browder (55), and children; Caro. E. (31), and Wm. R. (28), and William’s wife, Helen P. (24).  Also listed are the following members of the Walker family; James V. (8), Richd. B. (7) and Jno. T. (5).  Also listed are Fannie E. Brender (4), and Jas. W. Brender (10).  This census states that Richard was born in Virginia.  Richard died in Logan County, Kentucky. 

The known children of Richard and Elizabeth Browder were David (November 11, 1818-c1870), Mary S. (b. September 24, 1824), Thomas E. (b. September 1, 1826), Caroline “Caro” E. (September 26, 1828-September 30, 1895), and William R. (b. May 26, 1832).

Mary S. Browder, daughter of Richard and Elizabeth Browder, married Edward W. Hughes.  Their known children were Sarah F. (b. Oct. 15, 1840), Mary E. (b. Jul. 31, 1843), David B. (Dec. 18, 1845-July 1901), Richard B. (b. Mar. 29, 1849), Emma (b. Aug. 29, 1850), Ida H. (Dec. 21, 1852-Aug. 5, 1854), Helen P. (Apr. 25, 1855-Feb. 28, 1860), Edward Lee (Jan. 1, 1859-Aug. 8, 1865), and Morgan O. (b. June 29, 1862).

Caroline E. Browder, daughter of Richard and Elizabeth Browder, married J. A. Walker. Their known children were James V. (b. June 7, 1851) and John T., Sr. (b. Mar. 19, 1855).

David Browder, son of Richard and Elizabeth Browder, married (1) Mary Evans, and later (2) Elizabeth E. Irvine. David and Mary had no children.

The 1840 census of Logan County, Kentucky lists David Browder (40-49), along with his presumed children; two males (15-19), one male (10-14), one male (under 5), one female (15-19), one female (10-14), and two females (5-9).  No slaves are listed.  David died in Montgomery County, Alabama.

The known children of David and Elizabeth E. Browder were Robert Irvine (August 31, 1846-July 19, 1933), Wilbur F. (b. Nov. 12, 1848), Richard D. (b. June 15, 1856), Edward M (b. September 11, 1859), and Fannie I. (Browder, Thomas, pp. 2-3). 

Robert I. Browder married Alice M. Noble.  They had one known son, David (b. Sept. 13, 1871).

Wilbur F. Browder married Bettie B. Wills. Their known children were Wilbur F. (b. Nov. 24, 1872), Marion C. (b. June 6, 1874), John C. (b. Mar. 27, 1876), Lucien M. (b. Feb. 6, 1878), and Eugene J. (b. July 4, 1882).

Richard D. Browder married Mary Arnold.  Their known children were Richard (b. c1886), Sterra A. (b. c1889), Walter R. (b. c1890), and Elizabeth (b. c1892).

Edward M. Browder married Judith Rhea.

Thomas E. Browder, son of Richard and Elizabeth Browder, married Susan Virginia Warfield on December 23, 1856.

The known children of Thomas E. and Susan V. Browder were Lizzie D. (b. September 1, 1864), Susan “Susie” H. (b. August 2, 1866), Nannie G. (b. January 23, 1868), Katie E. (b. June 19, 1869), and Dora W. (b. February 28, 1872).

Lizzie D. Browder married J. C. Mitchell.  Susan H. Browder married W. E. Barnes. 

Katie E. Browder married S. E. Robertson. Their known children were Browder Robertson (b. c1894) and Virginia Robertson (b. c1899).

William R. Browder, son of Richard and Elizabeth Browder, was born in Logan County, Kentucky.  He married (1) Helen P. Jones (c1836-bef. 1870) on January 12, 1854 in Logan County, Kentucky.  He later married (2) Mary “Mollie” E. Sydnor (Penland Browder, p. 33/Browder, Thomas, p. 2-5).  Helen was born in Logan County, Kentucky. 

This couple were residing with William’s parents when the 1860 census was taken.  The 1870 census of Logan County, Kentucky (Hogan District) lists William R. Browder (38), farmer, along with his wife, Mary E. (31), and daughter, Fannie (14).  Also listed within this household are a family of black servants; Bob Grimes (56), Dollie Grimes (50), and Edward Grimes (10).  This census states that all household members were born in Kentucky except for Bob Grimes, who was born in Virginia.

Next door is listed the family of Paschal Browder (49), who was black and who worked as a farm laborer.  His family included his wife, Lucinda (48), and a presumed relative, Edmund (50).  Paschal and Lucinda are listed as having been born in Kentucky and Edmund is listed as having been born in Virginia.

The 1880 census of Logan County (Hogan District) lists William R. Browder (48), farmer, along with his wife, Mollie E. (41), and daughter, Fannie J. (24).  Also listed is Laura Sydnor (10).  This census states that       William and both of his parents were born in Kentucky.  Mollie and her mother are listed as having been born in Kentucky while her father is listed as having been born in Virginia.  Fannie, Laura and both of their parents are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

The 1900 census of Logan County, Kentucky (Magisterial District 3) lists William R. Browder (68/May 1832), farmer, along with his daughter, Fannie J. Bailey (44/March 1856), and grand-daughter, Anna Bailey (13/December 1886).  All family members and their parents are listed as having been born in Kentucky except for William’s parents, who are listed as having been born in Virginia.

Next to this household is that of Thomas B. Sydnor (65/March 1835), farmer, which included his wife, Mary M. (54/February 1846), and their children; Robert H. (29/June 1870), and Catherine G. (18/May 1882).  Thomas and his mother are listed as having been born in Kentucky.  His father is listed as having been born in Virginia.  Mary is listed as having been born in Kentucky, her father in Virginia and her mother in Georgia.  Robert and Catherine are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

Lucy Frances “Fannie” J. Browder (b. c1856), daughter of William R. Browder and Helen P. Jones, married Dr. Daniel Bailey. They had one known child, Anna (b. Dec. 1886).

Jane Browder, daughter of David Browder and Mary Cousins, married Rev. Caleb Norris Bell, Jr. (June 5, 1788-Sept. 21, 1872). Their known children were Mary Susan, Mary, William, and Eliza.

Almeda Browder, daughter of David Browder and Mary Cousins, married Robert Hutchings. Their known children were George H. (Jan. 21, 1822-Nov. 16, 1893), Mary E. (c1828-Oct. 1899), and David B. (Oct. 15, 1833-Mar. 6, 1895).

William Cousins Browder, son of David Browder and Mary Cousins, married Sarah B. Hazelwood.

The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists William C. Browder (16-25), along with his presumed wife (16-25).  Also listed are six male slaves and three female slaves.  Also, in 1820 William C. Browder was taxed on 361 acres of land, located on Whipponock Creek in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.

This family moved to Logan County, Kentucky prior to 1830.  The 1830 census of Russellville, Logan County, Kentucky lists William Browder (40-49) along with his presumed wife (20-29) and presumed children, one male (30-39), two males (under 5), one female (5-9) and one female (under 5).  It also lists five male slaves and three female slaves.

The 1840 census of Logan County lists William C. Browder (30-39) along with his presumed wife (30-39) and presumed children; one male (10-14), one female (15-19), one female (10-14), and two females (under 5).  Also listed are four male slaves and three female slaves.

The 1850 census of Logan County, Kentucky (District 2) lists Sarah B. Browder (42), along with her children; Richard C. (20), Martha (14), and Ermine (11).  This census shows that Sarah was born in Virginia.

The known children of William C. and Sarah Browder were Mary E. (b. June 2, 1824), Richard Cousins, Sr. (Nov. 7, 1830-Jan. 2, 1879), Martha H. (b. c1836), and Ermine (b. c1839), and Sarah. 

William C. Browder’s will is recorded in Logan County, Kentucky (Penland Browder, p. 33). 

Mary E. Browder married William B. Hughes.  Their known children were Virginia B. (Jan. 20, 1846-Feb. 1, 1897), Mary George (Mar. 7, 1848-June 28, 1876), Sarah (b. Nov. 1, 1848), Martha B. (b. June 22, 1850), Lizzie B. (b. Mar. 3, 1854), and Lula (b. Sept. 5, 1859).

Martha H. Browder married Dr. J. S. Baker. Their known children were Bettie, William E., Mary, Sallie, and Ella.

Ermine Browder married Richard A. Webster. Their known children were Henry, Sallie, and John.

Sarah Browder married John Bell.  Their known children were Sarah, John, and Caleb.

Richard Cousins Browder married Amanda J. Small (Apr. 3, 1837-Feb. 2, 1920). Their known children were W. B. (b. Mar. 15, 1858), J. S. (b. Jan. 18, 1860), Mattie G. (b. Dec. 13, 1861), Mary H. 9b. Oct. 16, 1863), Martha H. (b. June 1, 1866), Sallie B. (b. Aug. 22, 1871), Richard Cousins, Jr. (b. Dec. 13, 1873), Arthur Cousins (Mar. 5, 1875-Dec. 29, 1954), and Walter W. (b. Aug. 13, 1877).

Mattie G. Browder married Joseph J. Bell. Their known children were Richard and Mary Helen.

Mary H. Browder married James L. Young. Their known children were J. L., Richard B., and Mattie L.

Sallie B. Browder married E. W. Sydnor. Their known child was Wallace B. Sydnor.

Arthur Cousins Browder married Ruby Byron Miller (c1887-1923).

Robert W. Browder (February 20, 1804-August 17, 1890), son of David Browder and Mary Cousins, married (1) Helen Walker (d. 1827), daughter of the Honorable David Walker, in Kentucky on December 30, 1825, and later, (2) Sarah L. Gilmer, daughter of Nicholas M. and Amelia Gilmer, on December 25, 1828.

Robert Browder served as a member of the Kentucky Legislature for many years.  However, prior to the move to Kentucky he was listed in the Dinwiddie County, Virginia tax list from 1819 to 1820 in which he was taxed on 66½ acres of land there, located on Hatcher’s Run, which was conveyed to him by the estate of Mary Browder, his mother, through her will (Hughes, p. 52).

The 1830 census of Russellville, Logan County, Kentucky lists Robert Browder (20-29), along with his wife (20-29), and presumed children; one male (under 5), and one female (under 5).  Also listed are four male slaves and three female slaves.

The 1840 census of Logan County lists Robert Browder (30-39), along with his wife (20-29), and presumed children; one male (10-14), two males (5-9), and two males (under 5).  Also listed are five male slaves and four female slaves.

The 1850 census of Logan County (District 2) lists Robert Browder (46), farmer, along with his wife, Sarah (42), and children; William (11), Robert (10), Francis (f. 7), Mary (4), and David (1) as well as A. Bagwell (18).  This census confirms that Robert was born in Virginia.  Sarah is listed as having been born in Georgia and all of the children are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

Listed next to this family is that of David Sydnor (47), farmer, which included his wife, Catherine (45), and children; Washington (18), David E. (16), Thomas B. (14), Mary E. (11), and Sarah (9).  This census states that David was born in Virginia and that all other family members were born in Kentucky.

The 1860 census of Logan County (District 2) lists Robert Browder (56), farmer, along with his wife, Sarah L. (52), and son, William M. (23).  This census states that Robert was born in Virginia, that Sarah was born in Georgia and that William was born in Kentucky.

The 1870 census of Logan County (Hogan district) lists Robert Browder (66), farmer, along with his wife, Sarah L. (62) and children; David (21), James (18), and Ann E. (16).  Also listed are Mary Gillmore [probably Gilmer] (68) and Eliza Rutherford (21), both listed as boarders.  This census states that Robert was born in Virginia, that Sarah was born in Georgia, that David, James, Ann and Eliza were born in Kentucky, and that Mary was born in Georgia.

The 1880 census of Hogan, Logan County lists Robert W. Browder (76), farmer, along with his wife, Sallie B. (72) and children; James W. (27), and Anna E. (25).  Also listed is Lizzie Rutherford (30).  This census states that Robert was born in Kentucky and that both of his parents were born in Virginia.  Sallie, as well as her parents, are all listed as having been born in Virginia.  All children are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

The child of Robert and Helen Browder was Rev. George Richard, Sr. (January 10, 1827-September 3, 1886). 

The children of Robert and Sarah Browder were Helen (November 2, 1829-June 14, 1833), Amelia M. (February 14, 1831-July 17, 1831), John N. (February 17, 1833-Sept. 22, 1912), William M. (June 18, 1837-January 25, 1919), Robert M. (January 19, 1840-1879), Francis “Frank” Gilmer (June 26, 1843-Oct. 20, 1926), Mary M. (b. February 11, 1846), David Parrish (October 19, 1848-Mar. 7, 1913), James W. (May 19, 1851-January 20, 1916), and Ann “Annie” Elizabeth (June 27, 1853-Sept. 21, 1884).

The Rev. George Richard Browder, Sr., son of Robert and Helen Browder, married Ann Elizabeth Warfield (Jan. 26, 1830-Oct. 2, 1897) on September 5, 1850.

The 1880 census of Logan County, Kentucky (Hogan District) lists George R. Browder (53), minister, along with his wife, Elizabeth (50) and children; Hansen (24), a professor, George R. (22), Ludur (19), and Waller (15).  This census shows that George and his mother were born in Kentucky and that his father was born in Virginia.  Elizabeth and her parents are listed as having been born in Maryland.  All children are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

The children of Rev. George R. and Ann E. Browder were Helen S. (August 5, 1851-Mar. 12, 1920), Dr. Rev. Robert W. (April 23, 1853-Mar. 23, 1916), Hanson W. (b. July 8, 1855), Dr. George Richard, Jr. (June 17, 1857-February 25, 1889), Sarah Virginia (November 19, 1858-November 21, 1860), Rev. Luther W. (Apr. 6, 1861-June 4, 1925), Wallace “Waller” Warfield (October 12, 1865-Apr. 15, 1949), Lizzie Warfield (May 20, 1870-Sept. 17, 1870), and Ann Elizabeth.

John N. Browder, Sr., son of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married (1) Olivia Susan Blue (Sept. 15, 1834-Mar. 27, 1888) and later (2) Sarah Frances Barton (Nov. 27, 1848-June 12, 1922), widow of Leroy H. Fogleman. 

The known children of John N. and Olivia S. Browder were Robert Neil (June 13, 1858-Aug. 1, 1860), Francis “Frank” Gilmer (Dec. 14, 1860-Oct. 19, 1942), Lillian (b. Sept. 21, 1863), Eugenia (Jan. 9, 1867-Feb. 27, 1872), George Richard (b. July 7, 1869), John N., Jr. (Oct. 28, 1871-July 24, 1873), Lucy R. (b. Nov. 11, 1875), and “Son” (Aug. 15, 1877-Aug. 15, 1877).

William M. Browder, son of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married Emma Lynn Jones (June 10, 1841-May 27, 1899).  Their known children were Sarah “Sallie” Frances (Sept. 16, 1859-Jan. 31, 1948), and Robert Fletcher (Dec. 10, 1861-1937).

Francis G. Browder, son of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married Alice F. Barton (Jul. 23, 1847-Jan. 20, 1920).  Their known children were George M. (b. Sept. 18, 1868), Carrie L. (b. Oct. 26, 1870), Mary Ella (b. Nov. 24, 1872), Robert Barton (b. Nov. 6, 1875), Alice E. (Aug. 13, 1879-Dec. 27, 1882), Frank N. (b. Jul. 8, 1882), James W. (b. Oct. 27, 1884), John Morgan (b. Apr. 16, 1887), and William Leroy (June 4, 1890-Aug. 24, 1891).

Mary M. Browder, daughter of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married Thomas B. Sydnor.  Their known children were Robert H. (b. June 1870), and Katie G. (b. May 20, 1882).

Ann Elizabeth Browder, daughter of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married Dr. Daniel Bailey.

James W. Browder, son of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married Margaret N. Walker (Sept. 19, 1860-Sept. 4, 1934).

The 1900 census of Logan County, Kentucky (Magisterial District 3) lists James W. Browder (49/May 1851), along with his wife, Margaret W. (40/September 1859).  This census states that James was born in Kentucky, that his father was born in Virginia and that his mother was born in Georgia.

The death certificate for James W. Browder shows his death date and that he died where he was born, in Logan County, Kentucky.

David Parrish Browder, son of Robert W. and Sarah L. Browder, married (1) Nellie May unknown, and later, (2) Florence A. Bailey. The known children of David P. Browder were Jonathan Bailey (Jul. 27, 1872-Apr. 5, 1958), Annie Gilmer (July 12, 1876-Nov. 23, 1879), David Philip (Mar. 8, 1882-Jan. 17, 1953), and Robert Marvin (Mar. 8, 1882-Feb. 25, 1949).

The 1840 census of Russellville, Logan County, Kentucky, lists David Browder (20-29), along with his wife (20-29), and one son (15-19).  Also listed are two male slaves and two female slaves.

The 1900 census of Logan County lists David P. Browder (51/October 1848), farmer, along with his wife, Florence (47), and children; Robert M. (23) and Philip (18).

The 1910 census of Logan County lists David P. Browder (61), farmer, along with his wife, Florence B. (57), as well as David’s brother, James W. (59), and sister-in-law, Margretta N. (55).  This census states that David and James were born in Kentucky and that both of their parents were born in Virginia.  All other family members and their parents are listed as having been born in Kentucky.

The death certificate of Robert Marvin Browder states that his parents were David P. Browder and Nellie May.  Robert died at the age of 71 at Crassville, Cumberland County, Tennessee.

Thompson Browder (c1758-c1804), son of Richard and Mary Browder, married Tabitha Johnson on December 29, 1786 in Brunswick County, Virginia (Brunswick County Marriage Records/Penland Browder, p. 29).  One can presume that he likely had the first name “James” after James Thompson, but I have found nothing to actually prove this.

Thompson Browder is listed in the 1782-1787 Dinwiddie County tax lists as owning one slave.  He is also listed in the Dinwiddie County, Virginia tax list from 1787-1790 as being taxed on 80 acres of land there.

The known children of Thompson and Tabitha Browder were Jonathan (c1787-Dec. 13, 1876), Benjamin A. (c1788-1849), Elizabeth “Betsy” (c1790-p. 1870), Jesse (c1795-p. 1860), Pvt. Vines “Venus” C. (b. c1798), Tabitha T. (Mar. 17, 1800-Sept. 23, 1880), and Jane (c1803-October 11, 1879). 

Jonathan Browder, son of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, became a medical doctor. He married Nancy Ligon (d. bef. 1850) (Halifax County Deed Book [HCDB] 45, p. 324). Their known children were Samuel (b. c1829), Joseph A. (b. c1832). William P. (b. c1834), and Sarah E. (b. c1843). After Nancy died Jonathan married Amelia unknown (b. c1803).

The 1850 census of Jefferson County, Kentucky (Louisville, Ward 7) lists Jonathan Browder (63), along with family members; Amelia (47), Samuel (21), Joseph A. (18), William P. (16), and Sarah E. (7).  This census states that Jonathan was born in Virginia.

Joseph A. Browder, son of Jonathan and Nancy Browder, married Elizabeth J. Kilby.  The 1870 census of Lebanon, Cooper County, Missouri lists Joseph A. Browder (38), auctioneer, along with his wife, Elizabeth J. (30), and children; Henry W. (13), Walter F. (8), Amelia L. (6), Fannie A. (2), and Blanche (3 months).  This census shows that Joseph was born in Kentucky.  All other family members are listed as having been born in Missouri. 

Joseph died sometime between 1871 and 1880.  The 1880 census of California, Moniteau County, Missouri, lists Elizabeth Browder (40), along with her children; Walter (20), Amelia (16), Fannie (12), Blanche (10), and Cora (6).  This census shows that Elizabeth was born in Missouri, that her father was born in Connecticut and that her mother was born in Virginia.  All children are listed as having been born in Missouri as is their mother and their father is listed as having been born in Kentucky.

The 1900 census of Central, Perry County, Missouri lists Elizabeth Browder (58/May 1842) as residing within the household of T. E. Brown (24/November 1860), along with his wife, Cora (24/July 1875), and their children; Gladys Elma (2/June 1898), and Browder (f., 1 month/March 1900).  This census shows that Elizabeth, listed as T. E.’s mother-in-law, was born in Kentucky, that her father was also born in Kentucky and that her mother was born in New York State.  It also shows that T. E. was born in Canada as were both of his parents.  Cora is shown as having been born in Missouri with both of her parents listed as having been born in Kentucky.  Both children are listed as having been born in Missouri.

The 1910 census of De Queen, Sevier County, Arkansas (Ward 1) lists Thomas E. Brown (44), along with his wife, Cora H. (34), and children; Gladys E. (11) and Harriett F. (10).  Also included is Denard Riggin (24), and Elizabeth J. Browder (66).  This census shows Thomas and his parents as having been born in English Canada.  It shows Cora as having been born in Missouri, her father as having been born in Kentucky and her mother as having been born in Virginia.  Both children are shown as having been born in Missouri.  Denard, a white boarder, is shown as having been born in Arkansas, his father in Maryland and his mother in Alabama.  Elizabeth is shown as having been born in Missouri, her father as having been born in Connecticut and her mother as having been born in Virginia.

The known children of Joseph A. and Elizabeth J. Browder were Henry W. (b. c1857), Walter F. (b. c1862), Amelia L. (b. c1864), Fannie A. (b. c1868), and Blanche (b. c1870), and Cora Hermans (July 6, 1874-August 26, 1953).

Cora H. Browder (b. July 1875), daughter of Joseph A. and Elizabeth J. Browder, married Thomas E. Brown (b. Nov. 1860).  Their known children were Gladys Elma (b. June 1898), Browder (f., b. March 1900), and Harriett F. (b. c1871).

The death certificate of Cora Hermans Brown provides her birth and death dates and shows that she was born in California County, Missouri, dying at the age of 79 in Knoxville, Knox County (District 9), Tennessee of coronary occlusion/arteriosclerosis.  This certificate shows that her parents were Joseph W. Browder and Elizabeth J. Kilby.  It also shows that she was widowed at the time of her death.  She was buried in Woodlawn Cemetery in Knox County.

Benjamin A. Browder, son of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, married Elizabeth Thompson (HCDB 45, p. 324). 

The 1820 census of Person County, North Carolina lists Benjamin A. Browder (26-44), along with his wife (26-44), and children; two males (10-15), one male (under 10), and one female (under 10).  No slaves were listed.  This family lived by farming.

Benjamin Browder still resided in Person County, North Carolina in 1836 (HCDB 45, p. 324).  Later deeds show that he had moved to Halifax County, Virginia by 1839.  Book 52, p. 385, records a deed from the year 1848 which shows that his wife was named Elizabeth.  Book 53, p. 304 shows that he had died by 1849.

Elizabeth Browder, daughter of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, married Lt. William Phelps Pulliam (Sept. 7, 1786-1871). Their known children were Simeon L. (Aug. 30, 1832-Dec. 13, 1862), Richard Thompson, Benjamin W., Elizabeth Ann “Eliza/Louisa”, Tabitha Jane, and Martha P.

Simeon L. Pulliam married Martha E. Vawter. Richard Thompson Pulliam married Martha W. Elam. Benjamin W. Pulliam married (1) Louisa W. Miller and (2) Elizabeth Goff. Elizabeth Ann Pulliam married William “Billy” A. Wall (Mar. 4, 1818-May 24, 1903). Tabatha Jane Pulliam married John Ellis Smith. And Martha P. Pulliam married (1) Henry Fergusson and (2) Obediah D. Ligon.

Jesse Browder, son of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, married Nancy Susannah Owens (c1804-p. 1860) (HCDB 45, p. 324). Their known children were Nancy Gregory (Nov. 10, 1831-Aug. 12, 1914), John N. (c1832-Sept. 19, 1864), David A. (b. c1834), Susan Ann (b. c1839), and Martha J. (b. c1841).

John N. Browder, son of Jesse and Nancy Browder, married Sarah “Sallie” Tuttle (Aug. 2, 1839-June 16, 1909). Their known children were Martha Adeline (Mar. 19, 1859-May 3, 1924), William “Billy” David (Oct. 28, 1860-Mar. 17, 1940), and John T. (June 15, 1862-Feb. 16, 1931).

Martha Adeline Browder married John M. Grubbs (Dec. 12, 1855-Aug. 4, 1919). William David Browder married Annie Marie Westmoreland (July 19, 1870-May 27, 1930). John T. Browder married (1) Louisa Harriet Boles (Aug. 2, 1864-Jan. 31, 1892), and (2) Louisa “Lula” E. Ledford (Aug. 2, 1878-Feb. 6, 1925).

Pvt. Vines Browder married Sarah Dunn. In court documents his name is consistently misspelled as “Venis”.

Tabitha Browder, daughter of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, married Pvt. Drury Benjamin “Ben” Wall (Apr. 21, 1788-July 24, 1857). Their known children were Elizabeth Jane (Jan. 20, 1826-Dec. 31, 1913), John (b. c1833), Martha (b. c1837), Malinda “Malindie” Ann (Apr. 1, 1838-May 27, 1915), James (b. c1841), and Mary Johnson (June 8, 1844-May 8, 1915).

Elizabeth J. Wall married (1) Israel Sides and (2) James William “JW” Shields (Aug. 11, 1824-Mar. 29, 1905). Malinda A. Wall married John H. Moser (Feb. 28, 1837-Jan. 6, 1884). Mary J. Wall married Solomon H. Bowen (Oct. 8. 1841-Oct. 7, 1902).

Jane Browder, daughter of Thompson and Tabitha Browder, married William Rand Bostick (c1803-p. 1880), son of Don Ferdinand Bostick (March 9, 1772-1824) and grandson of Col. Absolom Bostick (c1740-1803), sometime prior to 1833.  She and William Bostick both died in Forsyth County, North Carolina. 

The 1870 census of Forsythe County, North Carolina (Bethania Township) listed William Bostick (66), farmer, along with his wife, Jane (66), and their children, James (28), and Louise (26).  All family members are listed as having been born in North Carolina except for Jane, who is listed as having been born in Virginia. 

This same 1870 census also lists the John W. Bostick (32), farmer, family, which included his wife, Millia (30), and children, Lucy A. (7), Mary F. (5), John W. (3), and Martha E. (2 months).  All family members are listed as having been born in North Carolina.

The 1880 census of Forsythe County, North Carolina (Bethania Township) again listed Wm. R. Bostick (76), farmer, along with his son, John W. (41), his daughter, Jan M. (37), his granddaughters, Lucy A. (18), Mary F. (16), and L. E. (5), and his grandsons, John W. (15), Wm. C. (4), and J. H. (4).  All family members are listed as having been born in North Carolina.

So, obviously, Jane had died by 1880, and we already knew that she died in 1879 in any case, presumably in Forsythe County, North Carolina.  It is also known that William R. Bostick died later in Forsyth County, North Carolina.  So, they are, presumably, buried together somewhere in Forsythe County.

The known children of William R. and Jane Bostick (in part from Jerome, “Bostick Family Ancestors” webpage and Jerome, “The Jarvis Family and Other Relatives” webpage) were Mary Jane “Jan M.” (b. c1833), Angelina “Anne” (Jan. 9, 1836-Jan. 29, 1892), John W. (May 2, 1838-Mar. 3, 1924), James Abner (c1842-1913), Jesse (m. Nancy Green), and Louise (Jul. 31, 1844-Feb. 2, 1920). 

In fact, the eldest daughter of William R. Bostick was Mary Jane (Jan M. from the 1880 census).  She is listed in the 1850 census of Stokes County, North Carolina, along with her siblings and parents.  Tucker and others state that she married Ed Vaughn, but marriage records for Stokes County only list the marriage of a Mary Ann Bostick to a Beverly C. Vaughn (d. 1862), on November 8, 1856. They had one known daughter, Mary Jane (1860-Mar. 7, 1931). She married Newton Spears.

Angelina Bostick married James McCoy Tuttle (Apr. 12, 1835-Oct. 9, 1905).

John W. Bostick married Milly M. Tuttle (Sept. 1, 1840-Sept. 1, 1925). Their children were Lucy A. (b. c1863), Mary F. (b. c1865), John W. (b. c1867), Martha E. (b. c1870), L. E. (b. c1875), J. H. (b. c1876), and William C. (b. c1876).

James Abner Bostick married Nannie Jane May. Louise Bostick married James McCoy Tuttle after her sister died. Jesse Bostick married Nancy E. Green.

John Browder, son of Richard and Mary Browder, was said in family tradition to have died as a boy of an accidental gun shot. However, it seems obvious that he was the John Browder who moved, along with Isham and Talitha Browder, to Guilford County, North Carolina. That John Browder is known to have served during the Revolutionary War. He would have died about 1800 by my best estimate. There is no indication that he ever married or had children.

Mary “Polly” Thompson Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, married (1) the above Jesse Browder on December 15, 1780.  Jesse Browder was born on November 19, 1758 in Dinwiddie County, Virginia, but his parentage is still uncertain.  He died on February 7, 1783 during the American Revolutionary War, but he had returned home by that time.  Jesse had enlisted into the Southern Continental Army in 1776 for three years and apparently re-enlisted after that time period was completed (Penland Browder, p. 78-79).

Jesse Browder paid taxes in Dinwiddie County, Virginia in 1782-1783.  He is listed as owning one slave in said tax records.  His estate of 50 acres of land, located in Dinwiddie County, continued to be taxed as late as 1793 (Hughes, p. 51).  Jesse Browder also paid taxes on property in Brunswick County, Virginia in 1782.  What happened to his property following his death is unknown.

It seems to me that he was one and the same as the Jesse Browder who was granted land on Wolf Island Creek in Guilford County (later Rockingham County), North Carolina.  This Jesse Browder grant was close to Capt. Isham Browder (c1732-1784) in Guilford/Rockingham County. 

It is interesting to note that both Isham C. Browder and Jesse Browder married daughters of Richard Browder and Mary, and that they were both cousins of these daughters. 

One might like to conclude that Jesse and Isham Browder (c1732-1784) might have been brothers.  The problem with this is that Jesse would have been too young to have been a brother of Isham Browder, since Jesse was born in 1758, and Isham’s father, Arthur, died about 1738.  Regardless, Jesse Browder was a cousin of Mary Browder and, therefore, would have been a descendant of one of Richard’s brothers. 

Jesse and Mary Browder had no children. This is clear from the records. Mary remarried after the death of Jesse Browder to (2) Thomas Pentecost, Sr. (Nov. 19, 1758-February 7, 1830), son of William Pentecost (c1732-c1804) and Lucy Johns (c1732-c1804), on September 23, 1784 in Dinwiddie County, Virginia. 

The known children of Mary Browder and Thomas Pentecost were Thomas, Jr. (b. c1795), Scarborough (c1796-c1838), and an unknown male. 

Some years after Thomas Pentecost’s death, Mary applied for a military pension based upon her previous marriage to Jesse Browder.

Nothing further is known of Wilmuth Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder.

Nancy A. Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, married (1) Thomas Williams (c1763-c1791), and later, (2) John William Pettypool (c1762-c1839), the latter being a son of Henry Pettypool and Anne Browder (daughter of John and Elizabeth Browder).

The known children of Thomas and Nancy Williams were Hartwell W. (b. c1783), Mary Thompson (b. c1787), Nathan (b. c1789), Jones (b. c1791), Lewis (b. c1792), and Edward.

It is not known who Lewis Williams married. But he had at least one son, Thomas (b. c1824). He married Mary E. unknown (b. c1834). Their known children were Virginia A. (b. c1854), Henry H. (b. c1856), A. A. (a female, b. c1859), and Thomas (b. c1860).

The known children of Nancy A. Browder and John William Pettypool were Perkinson (c1793-June 11, 1836), Henry (b. c1794), Elizabeth (b. c1795), David 9b. c1795), Stephen, John, and Peterson.

Perkinson Pettypool married Nancy S. Sullivant (b. c1794). Their known children were Perkinson, Jr. (Aug. 29, 1828-Mar. 12, 1890), Nancy, and John.

Perkinson Pettypool, Jr. married Frances Bevis. Nancy Pettypool married Coleman Gordon.

Henry Pettypool, son of John W. Pettypool and Nancy A. Browder, married Margaret Tarbutton. Their known children were Elizabeth and Peterson.

Elizabeth Pettypool, daughter of John W. Pettypool and Nancy A. Browder, married Pettipool Massie (b. c1794).

David Pettypool, son of John W. and Nancy A. Browder, married Frances Winfield.

Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, married her cousin and my ancestor, Isham C. Browder (c1774-1832).  This fact is first stated in the addendum to the previously referenced book, “Genealogy of David Browder, of Virginia and His Descendants”, in which David P. Browder refers to research he had recently obtained through his nephew, the Rev. Robert W. Browder, which Robert Browder, in turn, obtained from his father, the Rev. George R. Browder, who had, in turn, obtained the information from his father, Robert W. Browder (b. 1804), son of David Browder and Mary Cousins.  In the addendum to this book by Thomas E. Browder, David states this fact in the following manner:

“One BROWDER, an Englishman, married a WELSH WOMAN, and emigrated to America early in the 18th century, and settled in Virginia, where he lived and died.  His name has not been retained in the family.  The children of these progenitors of the Browder family were Richard, Joseph, ARTHUR, George and Nancy as far as known. . . .  MARTHA BROWDER, daughter of RICHARD and MARY THOMPSON BROWDER, married a cousin ISHAM BROWDER.   Their children were Mary and RICHARD so far as is known” (Browder, Thomas, pp. 13-14).

This is the first written mention in a book of the marriage of the cousins Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder.  But I still wound up having to prove it, as many wanted to dismiss it. 

Finally, “By 1800, despite all the moving around, Dinwiddie County, Va. still contained the largest number of BROWDERS.  The Personal Property Tax Records show the following Browder males 16 and above:  Thomas Browder; John Browder Jr.; Harmon [actually, Herman] Browder; William Browder; Thomas Browder; John Browder Sr.; Joshua Browder; Richard Browder; Jones Browder; Isham Browder; William Browder; Jeremiah Browder; George Browder; and Littleberry Browder” (Sides-Dye, p. 22). 

I should add in the end that I have come to believe with near certainty that Jesse Browder (Nov. 19, 1753-Feb. 7, 1783) was more than likely another son of William Browder, Sr. (Jul. 7, 1723-c1763) and Margaret (c1723-p. 1763). Jesse had no known living children.

What we know for sure about Jesse Browder begins with the fact that, according to later sworn testimony, Jesse Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, having recently enlisted in the southern army (unusual for someone from Virginia), takes part in the Battle of Sullivan’s Island or the Battle of Fort Sullivan near Charleston, South Carolina on June 28, 1776. The British received significant casualties and were repelled, not to return until 1780.

Also, according to the same sworn testimony, Jesse Browder took part in the First battle of Savannah on Dec. 29, 1778. The British achieve victory and capture the city, occupying it and opening up their southern campaign. Jesse Browder, along with two others, is forced to flee and escape in order not to be captured by British forces, they making a raft and going across the Savannah River by this means.

After Jesse returned home to Dinwiddie County, Virginia, he married his cousin, Mary Thompson “Polly” Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, on Dec. 15, 1780.

It is known that he paid property taxes in Dinwiddie County and Brunswick County in 1782 and 1783. He is also undoubtedly the Jesse Browder who was granted lands in Guilford County, North Carolina, but there is no indication that he and Mary ever moved there. On Nov. 8, 1784, Jesse Browder is posthumously granted 500 acres and 400 acres of land in Guilford County, North Carolina, located at the head of Rocky Branch Creek and Little Troublesome Creek in Guilford County, NC along the lines of Benjamin Grainger, Hugh Martin, and Andrew Martin. It also bordered the property of Mr. Bankson (RCDB A, p. 58; Guilford County Land Grants Patent Book 56, pp. 203; File #991).

St. of N. Car. Grant 500 A, head of Rocky branch Ck. Guilford Co. To Jesse Browder:

For consideration of 50 shillings for every 100 acres.  Tract of land containing 400 acres at the head of Rocky branch & little Troublesome Cks. Guilford Co. a description follows – lies along Benjamin Granger’s line, also Andrew Martin’s line. . . . Alexander Martin Gov. at time of transaction Nov. 8th 1784.

Jesse Browder died on Feb. 7, 1783 in Dinwiddie County, Virginia, leaving his widow, Mary. There were no known children from this union. Following his death, Mary married again on Sept. 23, 1784 to Thomas Pentecost.

The sworn testimony is now provided, dated Apr. 2, 1844, from the Pension Application of Jesse Browder, R8100: originally transcribed and annotated by C. Leon Harris:

Isham Browder, Sr. [son of George Browder, Sr. and Martha F. Parham], of Dinwiddie County, Virginia 78 years of age, testified in a deposition before James H. Boisseau, Justice of the Peace, Dinwiddie County, Virginia, concerning the pension application of “Polly” Browder that he had been “well acquainted with Jesse Browder when he was young” and that Jesse enlisted in the regular service for a period of three years. That he could not give particulars other than that Jesse had often said he had been in an engagement near the Savannah River when American forces were defeated, that he and two others went to the Savannah River, but only two could swim, so they made a raft and crossed. Soon after his return Jesse married Polly Browder, now Polly Pentecost and that they lived as man and wife until Jesse’s death. Afterward she married Thomas Pentecost, and that Polly has remained a widow since, etc.

“State of Virginia } Ss Dinwiddie County }

Be it known that on the second day of April 1844 personally appeared before me James H. Boisseau a Justice of the Peace in and for the county and state aforesaid Isham Browder aged 78 years of said county who being first duly sworn according to law doth on his oath make the following statement:

That he was well acquainted with Jesse Browder when he was young and that the aforesaid Jesse enlisted in the regular service as he always believed and that he was in the service three years, but who he the said Jesse served under or the particulars of his service he cannot say more than that he heard him the said Jesse say that in an engagement near the Savannah river where the american forces were defeated and that he the said Jesse and two more in the retreat came to the savannah river that two of the three could swim that they made a raft and all three crossed in safety That soon after his return he the said Jesse married to one Polly Browder now Polly Pentycost, as I always understood and I do know that they the said Jesse and Polly did live together as man and wife untill his the said Jesse Browders death that she the the said Polly Browder arfter her husbands death the aforesaid Jesse Browder she married one Thomas Pentycost as I heard and I do know that they the said Thomas and Polly lived together as manand wife and were considered to be lawfully man and wife by all who knew them and that he the said Thomas Pentycost died about fourteen years ago that she the said Polly is still his widow.

Isham Browder”

In addition, in other sworn testimony, on Apr. 2, 1844, Susan Browder [wife of Isham Browder who was a son of George Browder and Martha F. Parham], 71, stated that she had often heard Jesse Browder’s mother speak of his having been a soldier in the southern army, and that Polly Browder and Thomas Pentecost were married some years before 1791, when she, herself, was married.

In addition, on Apr. 11, 1844, Peter Eppes of Dinwiddie County, Virginia 85 years of age, testified in a deposition before James H. Boisseau, Justice of the Peace, Dinwiddie County, Virginia, also concerning the pension application of “Polly” Browder that he did not know Jesse Browder while he was in the army but did know him before the Revolutionary War and after he (Peter Eppes) had been in the northern army for some time he learned from Jesse’s “brother”, Charles Browder, who served in the same company with Peter Eppes (Capt. Alexander Rose’s company of infantry) that Jesse had enlisted in the southern army (so we know that both Charles Browder and Peter Eppes enlisted before Jesse Browder did). Peter Eppes stated that he also knew Jesse Browder after the war and had joked with him that he would rather have served two years in the northern army than six months in the southern army. Eppes stated that he did not see Jesse in the service but had no doubt whatsoever of his faithful service. He then stated (as if there is any real doubt) that “Jesse Browder married Polly Browder now Polly Pentycost of this county”. He corroborated by stating that he himself had been married in 1787 and that Jesse and Polly had been married before he had been, etc.

“State of Virginia, Dinwiddie County} Ss

Be it known that on the 11th day of April 1844 personally appeared before me Jas. Epes a Justice of the Peace in and for the county and State aforesaid Peter Eppes [pension application S8424] aged eighty five years of said county who being first duly sworn according to law doth on his oath make the following statement

That I did not know Jesse Browder in the army but that I knew Jesse Browder before the war of the revolution and after I had been some time in the northern army I learned from his brother Charles Browder who served in the same company with me (viz Capt Alexander Rose company of infantry) that his brother Jesse Browder had enlisted into the regular service and joined the southern army how long he the said Jesse Browder served or in what capacity I do not know, but I knew him after the close of the war and reccollect to have had but one conversation with him in relation to the service when I reccollect to have said to him the aforesaid Jesse that I had rather serve two years in the northern army than six months in the southern army Although I did not see him in service I have not the least shadow of doubt but that he did enlist and serve out the term of his enlistment faithfully I would also state that some time after the war the aforesaid Jesse Browder married one Polly Browder now Polly Pentycost of this county I cannot say as to the precise time of the marriage but this I do know that I was married in the year 1787 and that they the aforesaid Jesse & Polly Browder were married before myself.

Peter Eppes”

The above Peter Eppes (c1759-Jul. 21, 1851) (death date according to Revolutionary War Pension Records) was a son of Peter Eppes d. c1773) and Ann Hardyman and grandson of Llewellyn Eppes (c1731-c1759) and Angelica. Peter served in the same unit along with Charles Browder. Both entered the service as Privates. Peter rose in rank to Sergeant by November of 1777. Charles was a Corporal by that time.

Both Peter Eppes and Charles Browder were listed in the same unit from at least April of 1777. They were already in Pennsylvania by June of 1777. Interestingly, both served in the same unit as an Isham Coleman.

Apr. 13, 1844 – Mary Williams. (daughter of Richard Browder and Mary Eppes Randolph), of Dinwiddie County, Virginia 83 years of age, testified in a deposition before James H. Boisseau, Justice of the Peace, Dinwiddie County, Virginia, concerning the pension application of “Polly” Browder that she had been brought up in the same neighborhood with Jesse Browder and Polly “Pentycost” and that she knew them both long before they got married “they being cousins” (clearly meaning that Jesse and Polly were cousins). She stated that she remembered well that during the Revolution Jesse Browder had “left the neighborhood” and that word got around in the neighborhood that he had joined the southern army; that she did not know this as a fact directly but always knew it to be true. She stated that he had been gone about three years she knew of him returning and that everyone had understood that he had been a regular in the southern army. After Jesse’s return, he courted and married Polly Browder, “now Polly Pentycost”. She further states that, “I was present at the wedding and saw them married”, etc.

“State of Virginia } SS.

Dinwiddie County }

On this 13th day of April 1844 personally appeared before the subscriber a Justice of the Peace Mrs Polly Pentycost a resident of the County and State aforesaid aged eighty one years, who being first duly sworn according to law doth on her oath make the following declaration in order to obtain the benefits of the provision made by the Acts of Congress passed 7th d July 1838 and the 23 August 1842 granting pensions to widows of persons who served during the Revolutionary War. That she is the widow of Jesse Browder who was first searjjnt in Capt Rowley P Dowlman [Rawleigh Downman, BLWt1917-300] company, that he enlisted under the aforesaid Dowlman about the year 1776 for the term of three years, which term he served in the southern army, that she has often heard him speak of having been in the battle on Sullivans island [28-29 Jun 1776], also of his being in an engagement, on or near, the Savannah river (the name of the place not now reccollected,) where the Americans were defeated, and he in order to save his life, and not be taken a prisoner, was obliged to swim the Savannah river with many others, and she does know that he always did attribute his bad health and early death to cold taken at that time. That she has often heard him speak of other battles the names of which she cannot now reccollect, that she does not of her own knowledge know, that he was a searjent. That he enlisted under Capt. Dowlman. That he was in the battles above mentions, but she has often heard him say that they were facts. And she does now, and has always believed them to be such as much as she ever believed any thing that she ever heard, but did not see. That she has often seen his discharge, which is now destroyd or cannot be found, does not reccollect by whom it was signed, but she has often seem him roll it up with other papers and heard him say at such times, that he must keep it, for he had to serve three years before he could get it.

She also declares that she was married to the said Jesse Browder, on the 15th day of December in the year seventeen hundred and eighty, that her husband the aforesaid Jesse Browder died on the 7th February 1783. That she was not married to him prior to his leaving the service, but the marriage took place previous to the first of January, seventeen hundred and ninety-four, viz; at the time above states. She further declares, that after the death of the husband aforesaid she was married to Thomas Pentycost, on the 23rd of September 1784, That he died on the 7th day of February 1830, and that she is still a widow.

Polly her X mark Pentecost

Family Record

 Marriages

Thos. Pentecost Born 19th November 1758

Was Married To Mary Browder 23 Sept’r 1784

She was Born 7th Decb’r 1762”

[Certified copy from a family Bible, the original in the handwriting of Thomas Pentecost.]

Apr. 13, 1844 – “Polly” Pentecost of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, aged 81 years, appeared before James H. Boisseau, Justice of the Peace, Dinwiddie County, Virginia to file a claim for benefits from the service of her first husband, Jesse Browder. In her deposition she stated that Jesse Browder had been First Sergeant in Capt. Rumby P. Dowlman’s Company, enlisting about 1776 for a term of 3 years. She stated that Jesse served this term in the southern army (which is in line with him receiving land grants in Guilford County, NC). She further stated that he often spoke of being in the Battle of Sullivan’s Island and in a battle (name forgotten) near the Savannah River in which the Continentals were defeated. She further stated that in order to save his life and avoid capture he and several others had to swim the Savannah River to get away, which he attributed his bad health and early death to. She further stated that she did not know of these instances as direct facts, but had heard him speak of them often and did believe what he said. Also that she had seen his discharge many times, but that it had since been lost or destroyed. She further stated that they had been married on Dec. 15, 1780, and that he died on Feb. 7, 1783. She married Thomas Pentecost later on Sept. 23, 1784, and that he died on Feb. 7, 1830, and that she had been a widow ever since, etc. Some corroborating dates from a family Bible are also included in this record.

NOTE: Even though some of the deposition material above is a bit confusing, a few things are made clear within said deposition statements. (1) Certain dates and ages are provided that would not otherwise have been known to us, even if they may be a little off as memories had faded. (2) Jesse Browder had served in the southern army as a regular while Charles Browder and Peter Eppes served at about the same time in the northern army in the regular army as infantrymen. That Both Jesse and Polly had been Browders and cousins, and both brought up in Dinwiddie County, Virginia. Several more things are made clear too. But one might have thought that Mary Williams might have stated that Polly Pentecost, formerly Browder, was her own sister, but she did not. Yet, this becomes all too clear in the texts of court cases concerning the division of Jones Browder’s estate. It seems odd, but one of the sisters was referred to as Mary and the other Polly most of the time, but sometimes both were referred to as Mary. Yet one is sometimes referred to as Nancy also. It is JUST possible that these two were not sisters. But if they were not, then one has to figure out the parentage of one or the other.

I have also not detailed Isham Browder (1762-1830), his possible brother, here because that is done in another section.
