John Browder I and Elizabeth Jones (the “Welsh Lady”)

John Browder I and Elizabeth Jones (the “Welsh Lady”)

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated and corrected, Dec. 27, 2024)

John Browder I (c1686-c1757), son of Edmund Browder I (c1651-c1739) and Elizabeth (c1653-1721/22), whom I believe to have been a Parsons, was born in Charles City (later Prince George) County, Virginia.  

The name of John’s wife was Elizabeth (c1685-p. 1741), and family tradition has always held that she was a “Welsh lady.”  The fact that she has been remembered as a “lady” is telling, since that would signify that she was, perhaps, descended from one of the Welsh noble families.  And it is known that Welsh noble families traced their ancestry to King Arthur, and tended to name their first sons Arthur. 

It is my belief that Elizabeth’s maiden name was Jones, both because this is a Welsh ancestral name of noble origins, and also because one of their descendants, a grandson of Richard Browder and Mary of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, was named Jones Browder.  Another of their descendants, son of their granddaughter Nancy Browder, who married Thomas Williams, was named Jones Williams. 

In further support of the belief that Elizabeth was of Welsh descent, one needs only to look at the name of her eldest son, Arthur (1715-c1738).  Again, during this time period, it was very common for the Welsh, Cornish, and those from Brittany in Northern France, to name their first sons after the mythical/ historical figure, King Arthur.  This appears to be the first time that the name Arthur can be found within the Browder family line anywhere.  And, no doubt due to his mother’s influence, the story of King Arthur had some impact upon young Arthur Browder himself as he was growing up.  It is possible that he even went so far as to try to emulate King Arthur to some extent, having been influenced by his mother and the story of King Arthur and the Knights of the Round Table.  It should not be considered at all far-fetched to suggest such a thing as popular entertainment having great influence upon people.  That is, possibly, an even greater common occurrence today.  There can be little doubt, Elizabeth instilled the tale of King Arthur and the Round Table into the minds of all of her children.

So, again, Elizabeth may perhaps also have been of noble descent herself, a true “Welsh lady”.  And, although little is actually known of John and Elizabeth Browder, their impact upon future generations of their descendants cannot be overestimated.  Indeed, Elizabeth must have made a very strong impression on at least the next two Browder generations descended from her in order for her to be remembered simply as a “Welsh lady” even while they, apparently, actually forgot her maiden name.  So, it is perfectly obvious that, whatever her maiden name might really have been, the influence of her Welsh heritage was remembered and passed down to later generations of her descendants, probably due, in part, to the imparting of the story of King Arthur. 

John Browder I and Elizabeth were married, almost certainly, in 1715, since that is the year Arthur is supposed to have been born and because Elizabeth is still referred to as “Eliz Jones” in her (presumed, but virtually certain) father’s will, which was written early in that year.  This date proves important in determining Elizabeth’s parentage, as will be shown. 

The idea that Elizabeth may have been a member of the Jones family is entirely my theory.  I had never encountered it elsewhere prior to coming to this conclusion, and had, in fact, encountered other theories, which I found to be without merit because they were based on little or no evidence.  Still, I myself did look into the possibility of her descent from other families, including the Scoggan and Mumford  families (since association with these families is obvious), for example, finding nothing of substance. 

It is said that three Jones brothers, Peter, William and Richard, emigrated from Wales to the American Colonies together.  Although it is more complicated than this, it is known that the first Peter Jones settled in Dinwiddie County, Virginia on or near the location of present day Petersburg, but he was not the founder of Petersburg.  Still, mainly due to his influence, the area was first called “Peter’s House”, then “Peter’s Town”, and finally “Petersburg.”  His brother, William Jones, died unmarried (Duda, p. 166).  She is, unfortunately, also not descended from this Jones line. 

But, before we solve this mystery, we must continue with John and Elizabeth themselves.  Extant records concerning or involving John Browder I, follow: 

John Browder I is first mentioned (as in, for the first time) in the official records of Prince George (later Dinwiddie) County, Virginia, in 1712, at which time he had some land surveyed by Robert Bolling, son of Col. John Bolling and Mary V. Kennon (PGCDB III, 1718-1728, p. 750).  This land was located on Gravelly Run near old Petersburg.  The fact that he first appears in such a record in 1712 shows that he had turned twenty-one years of age and was, therefore, considered to be an adult.  

The next record we have took place when, on August 11, 1719, John Browder, along with several others, was summoned to answer a petition and complaint of William Davis, Overseer of Highways,

For their contempt in refusing to assist in clearing the highway, and these persons (except JOHN BROWDER), failing to show just cause, it was ordered that each of them, except BROWDER, pay Davis 5 shillings.  Present: Maj. Robert Munford (Va. Gen. Soc. Qtly., Nov. 1986, p. 58). 

The timing of this alleged incident is interesting to me as it must have been around the time of Elizabeth’s pregnancy with their son, Richard Browder, and this birth may have had something to do with his refusal in this situation, as he probably needed to be home.  Obviously, John Browder had already made his reasoning clear, and it was accepted, whatever it was. 

Later, on June 22, 1722 (on the same day his brother, George Andrew Browder, was granted 264 acres of land in the same general area) John Browder I received a grant of 100 acres on both sides of Gravelly Run.  This land was probably that which had already been surveyed during 1712.  Also, on June 22, 1722, John Browder I had even more land surveyed (p. 482). 

Later, the Bristol Parish Vestry paid John Browder 100 pounds of tobacco per month for allowing Robert Glidewell to board with the family, since Glidewell was unable to work.  The Bristol Parish Vestry Book, in a notation dated April 14, 1728, (pp. 88-89) mentions this situation in the following manner:

Rob Glidewell Being afflicted With a Cancur in his face which hath Made him unable to labour for his livelihood it is ord’red that the Church Warthen find him necessary board and he to be allow’d one hundred pounds of tob* Pr month.

             And, on “Octbr 1728”:

To JOHN BROWDER for Glidewell . . . . . . . 00,700   0,056

1730 South Side Bristol Parish Dr. (pp. 52-53)

To JOHN BROWDER . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 1,000  80

This Robert Glidewell, by the way, is probably the one who married Elizabeth Nash, daughter of Robert Nash, about 1720 in Henrico County, Virginia.  Robert Glidewell, born about 1701 in London, England, son of William Gladwell and Alice Bryant, was transported to Virginia in 1716, and died of cancer in 1729. 

One of Robert Glidewell’s sons, Robert (October 23, 1722-c1809), married Elizabeth Byrd (c1732-c1782), daughter of William Byrd (c1704-bef. 1777) and Jean (Bell?/c1705-aft. 1765), about 1756.  This William Byrd was not the famous William Byrd of Westover, but was, instead, his uncle.  But the connection must not be lost to the reader. 

John and Elizabeth Browder were obviously, therefore, members of the Church of England, residing within the boundaries of Bristol Parish, Virginia, just as Edmund and Elizabeth Browder, John’s parents, had been.  They obviously attended services within the very brick church which still exists today.  However, during 1742, Bath Parish was formed from part of Bristol Parish.  It seems that this family resided within the boundaries of the new parish, as records of them in Bristol Parish cease about 1741/42. 

On July 20, 1736, John Browder was granted 300 more acres of land, located on Gravelly Run, by King George II.  This land joined that which he had previously been granted.  It also bordered on the land of his brothers George Andrew Browder and Edmund W. Browder (Prince George County Land Grant Records Book [PGCLGRB] 17, p. 125/Penland Browder p. 116).  This grant reads as follows:

GEORGE the Second by the Grace of God of Great Britain, France, and Ireland-Defender of the Faith to ALL TO WHOM these Presents shall come Greetings KNOW YE that for diverse good causes and considerations by more especially for and in consideration of the sum of THIRTY SHILLINGS of good and lawful money for our use Paid to our Receiver general of our Revenues in this our Colony and Dominion of Virginia WE HAVE given granted and confirmed and by these Presents for us our heirs and successors do give grant and confirm unto JOHN BROWDER one certain Parcel of Land

containing three hundred acres lying and being in the County of Prince George on the Branches of Gravelly Run adjoining his own and his Brothers George and Edmund Browder their lines and bounded as follows to wit:  Beginning in George Browder’s line at a . . .  In a Branch the corner of Edmund Browder’s Tract of one hundred acres thence along the said George Browder’s Line for East two hundred and thirty-four poles to his corner maple in a branch Thence north forty-four Degrees East one hundred poles along his own line to his corner Thence North West two hundred and sixteen poles to a corner Thence West forty Degrees South five hundred and nine poles to Edmund Browder’s Line Thence East twelve poles along the same to his corner and Thence South one hundred poles to the  beginning WITH ALL Woods underwoods swamps marshes . . . meadows Feedings and his due share of all . . . miles  quarried as discovered as not discovered within the bounds aforesaid and being part of the said Quantity of three hundred acres of Land and the Rivers, Waters and water courses therein  contained together with the Privilege of hunting, Hawking, Fishing, Fowling and all other Profits comodities & . . . whatsoever  to the same or any part thereof belonging or in any wise appertaining  TO HAVE HOLD possess and enjoy the said Tract or Parcel of land and all other the before granted Premises and every part thereof with their . . . and every of their appurtenances unto the said JOHN BROWDER and to his heirs and assigns forever TO BE HELD of us our heirs and successors as of our Mannor of East Greenwich in the County of Kent in fee and common . . . and not in . . . for every fifty acres of Land and so proportionable for a lesser or greater Quantity than fifty acres the Fee Rent of one shilling yearly to be paid upon the Feast of Saint Michael the Arch Angel and also cultivating and improving three acres part of every fifty of the Tract above mentioned within three years after the Date of these Presents PROVIDED always that of three years of the said Fee.  Fee Rent shall at any time be in arrears and unpaid or if the said JOHN BROWDER his heirs or assigns do not within the space of three years next coming after the Date of these Presents Cultivate and improve three acres part of every fifty of the Tract above mentioned then the Estate hereby granted shall cease and be utterly Determined and thereafter it shall and may be lawful to and for us our Heirs and Successors to grant the same Lands and Premises with the appurtenances unto such other Person or Persons as we our heirs and  Successors shall think fit.  IN WITNESS whereof we have caused these our Letters Patent to be made.  WITNESS our Trusty and well beloved William Gooch Esq.  Our Lieutenant Governor and Commander in Chief of our said Colony and Dominion at . . . Williamsburg under the seal of our said colony the Twentieth Day of July one thousand seven hundred and thirty-six in the Tenth year of our Reign.

             William Gooch

Further, on October 15, 1741, John Browder I was granted 325 additional acres of land.  At that point, then, his property holdings totaled at least 725 acres.  On this deed, John signed only with a mark, “B”, so he was likely unable to write or sign his name (but that did not necessarily mean that he was illiterate, as some misunderstand).  Two of his closest neighbors were John Eppes and Isham Eppes, and some of John’s land bordered land owned by Isham Eppes. 

Also, on September 20, 1745, John Hailes sold 74 acres of land located “on the heads of the branches of Gravelly Run and Hatcher’s Run” to William Sanders.  This property was bordered by the properties of John Browder, William Sanders, and Jeffrey Mumford (PGCDB, p. ?). 

And, on September 3, 1750, John and Elizabeth Browder together sold some of the land they had acquired on June 2, 1737 from John Scoggan [(c1697-July 1760), son of George Scoggan (b. c1665)], to Joseph Crook [son of George Crook and his wife, Elizabeth] of St. Andrew’s Parish (Brunswick County Deed Book [BCDB] 5, 1751-1755, pp. 35-37).  This particular tract was located on the south side of Waqua Creek, which was located in Brunswick County at that time, because some of John Browder’s land was in that part of Prince George County which was later formed into Brunswick County just across the Nottoway River.  This deed transaction follows verbatim:

             BROWDER and ux to Crook:

This Indenture made the third day of September in the year of our lord one thousand seven hundred and fifty by and between JOHN BROWDER of the parish of bath in the County of Prince George of the one part and Joseph Crook of the Parish of saint Andrews in the County of Brunswick of the other part Witnesseth that the said JOHN BROWDER for and in Consideration of the sum of Twenty Shillings of Lawfull money to him in hand paid or soon to be paid before the sealing and delivering of these presents the receipt whereof the said JOHN BROWDER doth hereby acknowledge therof and Every part and Parcel therof hath given granted bargained and sold and doth by these presents give grant bargain and sell unto the said Joseph Crook one hundred Acres of Land lying in brunswick County on the south Tide of waqua Creek formerly sold and Conveyed by John Scoggan deceased to JOHN BROWDER by deed bearing date the second day of June in the year of our Lord one thousand seven hundred thirty and seven parcel of Two hundred forty four acres of Land to be taken from the Lower side of the Tract in proportion to the breadth with all Properties Comodities advantages woods ways Easments houses orchards water and water Courses mines minerals and all advantages and appurtenances whatsoever to the same belonging or in any wise appertaining and the reversion and reversions  Remainder and remainders thereof and of Every part and Parcell therof and all the Estate Right title interest claim and demand of him the said JOHN BROWDER or any other Person or persons whatsoever of in or to the said one hundred Acres of Land to have and to hold all and singular the one hundred acres of Land parcel of two hundred and forty and four acres with all the appurtenances unto the said Joseph Crook his heirs and assigns to the only use and behoof him the said Joseph Crook his heirs and assigns forever and the said JOHN BROWDER for himself and his heirs doth hereby Covenant promise grant and agree to and with the said Joseph Crook his heirs and assigns that he the said JOHN BROWDER the said Parcel of Land with the appurtenances to the Joseph Crook his heirs and assigns forever shall and will warrant and forever defend the said one hundred acres of Land unto the said Joseph Crook his heirs and assigns forever and the said JOHN BROWDER doth hereby further grant and agree to and with the said Joseph Crook his heirs and Executors admin~ors that the said JOHN BROWDER now is and stands seized and indefeasible Estate in fee Simple of and in the said tract of Land and that he the said JOHN BROWDER hath good right and lawfull Authority to sell and Convey the same in manner and form aforesaid in witness wherof the said JOHN BROWDER hath hereunto Set his hand and fixed his Seal the day and year aforesaid

Signed sealed and delivered

     his mark

In Presence of Us                                               JOHN B BROWDER (seal)

             Test William Scogin Junr.                  her

             John Scogin                        ELIZABETH X BROWDER (seal)

                 His                                      mark

                             James ƚ Love

                                   Mark

             At a Court held for Brunswick County The 26th day of March 1751

This Indenture was proved by the oaths of William Scoggin Junr. John Scoggin and James Love the witnesses therto and ordered to be Recorded and ELIZABETH the wife of JOHN BROWDER Party therto Personally appeared in Court and having been first Privately Examined as the Law directs freely and voluntarily Relinquished her Right of Dower in the Lands within mentioned

                                                                             Teste

                                                                             Litt Tazewell ct Cur

Here it must be emphasized that, contrary to the prevailing belief among some researchers, making a mark, rather than signing one’s own name, is NOT necessarily a sign of illiteracy.  There are any number of reasons why a person either may not have been able to write their own name (such as arthritis, etc.), or may not have wanted to do so.  Or a person may have only been used to printing, but unused to, or unable to, actually sign their name, having never been taught to write cursive.  In these early records, it is actually fairly common for persons that we know to have been literate to simply make their mark rather than signing their name.  I have seen many, many of them. 

But what is of even more interest here is the known fact that, most of the time, people who did not sign their own names simply made an “X” mark.  That was the usual way practically everyone did it.  However, in this document alone, we see that John Browder’s mark was a “B”, and that James Love’s mark was an “I”.  Some may state that I am taking this a little too far, but it seems to me that, since Elizabeth did make her mark with an “X”, that shows us something. 

It is my opinion that this indicates that Elizabeth had no middle name, or initial, while both John Browder and James Love DID.  Therefore, they both wrote their middle initials as their marks.  I won’t go so far as to guess the middle name of James Love, although one great candidate comes to mind (Isham).  But I will state here that it seems more than logical and reasonable to me that, if “B” truly did stand for John Browder’s middle initial, that his middle name was very likely BOLLING  and that, therefore, his full name would have been John Bolling Browder.  This is more than plausible, although it can’t be proven with this document alone.  So, I don’t call him John Bolling Browder within this work, although I am fairly certain that this should be his full name. 

I must emphasize here that, although John Browder wrote a “B” as his mark, which someone could say stands for the first letter in Browder, James Love wrote neither a “J” nor an “L”.  So, his mark stands for the first letter of neither James nor Love.  All of this, to me, clearly indicates that the marks made, except for Elizabeth’s “X”, stood for the first letters of their middle names.  So, there can be little doubt, at least, that John Browder’s middle initial was, in fact, “B”, whatever it may have stood for.  And the most likely, and logical, possibility, according to my own research, is that it stood for Bolling. 

It is also important to note the slim possibility here that Elizabeth might, instead, have been a Scoggan, rather than a Jones.  It is said that Scoggan is a Gaelic name, and can be Welsh in origin, as well as Scottish and Irish.  It is possible that Elizabeth could have been a sister of the John Scoggan, son of George Scoggan, and grandson of the George Scoggan (b. c1630, England), mentioned above.  However, it is unknown whether George Scoggan (b. c1665, England) had a daughter named Elizabeth or not.  I simply suggest it as another possibility while maintaining that, based on available evidence, Arthur Jones (as I will show) is much more likely to have been her father.  And, I must add, those who research this branch of the Scoggan family maintain that they were of Scottish origin, not Welsh, in any case.  Furthermore, the surname Scoggan is actually an old Scandinavian name (as also is “Crook”, by the way), meaning “the bearded one”.  So, Scoggan is actually a poor candidate for Elizabeth’s maiden name.  From BCDB 5, p. 42:

Indenture made 8 October 1750, between William Scogin Senr. of Brunswick County, St. Andrews Parish, and William Scogin Junr. of same, 50 Pounds, 300a, on North side of Sturgeon Run on the North side of the Reedy branch, it being part of a tract of Land containing 383a granted to the said William Scogin by Patent dated 28 September 1728.  Wit:  William Scogin, Martha Scogin.  Wit:  John Wall, James Love, Joseph Crook.  Court 26 March 1751, Indenture acknowledged by William Scogin Senr., and Martha the wife of the said William personally appeared and relinquished her right of Dower. 

It is also interesting to note the following deed transaction excerpt from BCDB 6, p. 463:

Indenture made the 24th day of March, 1760, between Samuel Craft and William Scogin, for 12 pounds and 10 shillings, conveying 100 acres, on both sides of Reedy Branch, adjoining lands of William Wall, Matthew Evans, Theopheleus Field, and William Scogin, and being part of a tract granted to Thomas Williams by Patent dated November 3, 1750.  Witnesses were William Cryer, John Jones, and William Epes.  Indenture and Memorandum of Livery of Seizin were acknowledged in Court on April 28, 1760, by Samuel Craft, and [wife’s name not given], his wife, appeared and relinquished her right of dower.

And, it is also of interest to note that, on October 25, 1762, James Love and James Crook [son of George and Elizabeth Crook] of the one part, and James Fisher of the other part, took part in a deed transaction in which Fisher received 280 acres of land in Brunswick County, Virginia which adjoined that of Henry Wilson and James Vaughn.  James Love’s wife, Elizabeth, relinquished her right of dower in this land (BCDB 7, p. 190).  

Now follows a copy (courtesy of Carol A. Morrison) of the deed between John Browder and Joseph Crook, showing everyone’s marks: 

It is also interesting to note that the original home of John and Elizabeth Browder is said to have still been standing as late as 1963, at that time being owned by Mrs. George R. Slate.  They both died, and are buried, where they resided, in Dinwiddie (previously Prince George) County, Virginia.  They very well could have been buried in or near the old Blanford brick Church and Cemetery, but their gravesites, like those of the other early Browders, appear to have been lost to time. 

Now, getting to the possible ancestry of Elizabeth, the difficulty in finding her parentage should actually go without saying.  Which Welsh family should one look into?  Also, even in colonial times, the Jones family was already quite numerous, even within the borders of the American Colonies, from an early date.  But, based on available evidence, the Jones family is, and always has been, the best candidate for Elizabeth’s ancestry. 

So, with that as a working hypothesis, I engaged in a process of elimination in order to ascertain the most likely candidate(s).  It should be noted, however, that information on the early Jones families is incredibly mixed up on the internet, and full of obvious errors.  I did rely heavily on said internet information, but believe I have come to a good picture of the early Jones families in certain areas of Virginia overall by utilizing extant records, where available, that were useful for this purpose.  Thankfully, most Jones families are completely eliminated without difficulty. 

To begin with, the surname Jones is first found in Denbighshire, Wales, where Llanerchrugog served as their ancient family seat.  There are three main original sources for the surname Jones; from (1) Gwaithvoed, Lord Cardigan, Chief of one of the fifteen noble tribes of northern Wales in 921 CE; from (2) Bleddyn Ap Cynfyn, King of Powys; and from (3) Dyffryn Clwyd, a Chieftain of Denbighshire about the eleventh century.  Although originating in Wales, some members of this family early moved to Ireland (houseofnames.com/Jones Family Crest and History). 

Regardless, all of this elimination, both of Jones and other families that could have been candidates, leaves the very best possibility of Elizabeth’s parentage as being Lt. Arthur Jones of Isle of Wight County, Virginia and his wife, Susanna King.  They are known to have had a daughter named Elizabeth (b. c1685), but little more seems to be known of her.  Some posit that she died by 1716, but this is an obvious error, based on the fact that no record is found of her after her mention in her mother’s Will of 1691 and a misreading of her father’s Will.  It is nothing more than an assumption, based on lack of evidence for her existence past a certain date.  I happen to disagree with that assumption, for reasons I will show.  

Lt. Arthur Jones (c1663-1715), son of Edmund/Edward Jones, was born in Oldland, Hanham, County Gloucestershire, England and died in Surry County, Virginia, where he had been a miller.  He married (1) Susannah King (c1665-1691), daughter of Henry King (proven by the Will of John Newman, Isle of Wight County Will Book 1, p. 192, as well as the marriage record in IOWCW&DB 2, p. 192 & 565/Chapman, p. 26), in 1679 in Isle of Wight County, Virginia, and later, (2) Prudence Baldwin (c1660-aft. January 22, 1715), about 1695.  Susannah had previously been married to the said (1) John Newman (d. 1679), briefly, prior to her marriage to (2) Arthur Jones.  In his Will, Newman gave Arthur Jones the estate due his wife, Susanna. 

Susannah died in Isle of Wight County, Virginia, very likely due to complications from childbirth, since that was extremely common in those days and she was still of childbearing age.  Prudence died in Surry County, Virginia. 

Susannah wrote her own Will, dated December 19, 1691, so, sadly, even at her young age, she somehow knew she would soon die.  In her Will, she named her husband, Arthur Jones, her father, Henry King, and her daughters, Mary and Elizabeth, so they were still living at least as late as December of 1691.  This Will was witnessed by John Person, Richard Bennett, Jr., John Person, Jr., Richard Jones [brother of her husband, Arthur Jones], and Thomas Proud (IOWCWB I or II, p. 565). 

Still, one has to ask here why Susannah did not name her soon-to-be-born son, Arthur, in her Will, since he was obviously still living.  The reason for this is probably also obvious.  There was no way for Susanna to really know whether the child she carried would live or not and, frankly, she could not give him a name because she did not know that he would be a boy yet. 

This Will was not proven until May 26, 1713, which presents a problem.  If Elizabeth and Mary had been deceased by 1713 (as some researchers state), there would surely have been some notation of this in the record when said Will was proven.  But no such mention is made, so it becomes obvious that they were still alive.  In addition to this, Susanna’s son, Arthur, would have reached the age of twenty-one by this time.  So, although he did not stand to inherit anything, that is a reasonable explanation for why it was not until 1713 that her Will was finally probated.  All of her children had become adults by the standard of the time.  A copy of the actual Will of Susannah Jones (microfilm courtesy of the Virginia State Library), follows: 

From IOWCWB 2, p. 565, follows her Will verbatim:

In the Name of God amen I SUSANA JONES being in health of Body and in perfect memory blessed be to God for to appoint and ordaine to the Consent of my loveing husband ARTHER JONES that to be my last Will and testament in Manner and form as followeth –

Imp          the Land which my Father HENRY KING gave me by Will I give part unto my daughter ELIZA JONES that plantation that Thomas A…. formerly lived upon and a hundred acres which be in all a hundred and fifty acres to her and her heirs Lawfully begotton –

Imp          I give unto my Daughter Mary Jones a hundred and fifty acres of land lying on the other side of the Meadow Branch Eastward to her and her heirs Lawfully begotton Witness our hands and seals this nineteenth Day of December One thousand Six hundred Ninety and One –

Signed and Delivered in the

presence of us . . .                               ARTHER JONES (seal)

John Person                          SUSANNAH {} JONES (seal)

      The marke of

Richd. R Bennett Junr.                       At a Court held for Isle of Wight County

John Person Junr.                                the 26th day of May 1713

The RJ marke of The . . . will was proved in Court by the Oaths of –           Richd. Jones Richd. Bennett and Richd. Jones and admitted to –

Thomas Proud                                    Record

                                                             Test H Lightfoot C C

In any case, Arthur lived several years past Susanna’s death and, in the meantime, remarried.  One interesting incident which took place in the interim is mentioned thusly.  Among those who signed the following address were Lt. Arthur Jones and Cornet Thomas Clarke (Boddie, p. 169). 

In 1702 the Magistrates and militia of Isle of Wight county prepared an address of loyalty to Queen Anne. They stated that they “resented the attempt made upon our Religion, Laws and Liberty by the late unaccountable action of the French King in owning and declaring the pretended Prince of Wales to be the King of England.”

The Will of Arthur Jones was written on January 22, 1715, and probated on May 16, 1716 in Surry County, Virginia.  In it, Arthur names his daughter, “Eliz. Jones”, commonly accepted by some other researchers to be the daughter of Prudence, rather than Susanna.  However, that is not proven and, frankly, it is mere guesswork to suggest that he had two separate daughters named Elizabeth by two separate wives.  I suggest that Elizabeth had married John Browder I in 1715, the year Arthur Jones wrote his Will, but soon after said Will was written.  That is absolutely perfect to fit marrying John Browder I, and having her first child, Arthur, later in 1715. 

Plus, Arthur specifically uses her maiden name of “Jones” here, while the only other daughter he calls by a surname is Mary Cook, all the others simply being referred to by their given names.  This could signify that Elizabeth was about to be married, but had not yet been married.  At the very least, the mention of Elizabeth in her father’s Will proves she was still alive, contrary to what other researchers think. 

Overseers of said Will were James Sammon and Giles Underhill.  Witnesses were Jones Williams, William Bridges, and William Beech.  From Surry County Will Book 7, p. 16:

In the Name of God, Amen.  I ARTHUR JONES being very ill but in perfect sence being Desirous to make this my last Will and Testament.  First I give my Sole to God Almighty, my body to the Ground to be Decently buried.

Item  I give to my Son Arthur Jones my wearing clothes and my Shoemaker’s tools and my two guns and a bill of Thomas Lisbons for Six hundred pounds of Tobacco and a bill of Phillip Lupes for Six hundred pounds of Tobacco and Coopers tools.

Item  I give to my Daughter Mary Cook one Shilling.

Item  I give to my Daughter Sarah a parcel of Land lying between Joseph’s Swamp and Nottoway River Beginning at Doby’s Lower Corner tree so running on Doby’s Line to a red oak Corner tree thence Southerlyward to the River and down the River to a marked tree so running down the River to the fishing place and down the bottom to a mark and then Southerlyward to the River and down the River to the mouth of Joseph’s and a Cow and Calf to her and her heirs forever.

Item  I give to my Daughter Jane a parcel of Land beginning at Doby’s Corner pine to Southerly to the River and a Cow and Calf and two pewter Dishes to her and her heirs forever.

Item   I give to my Daughter Mary all the rest of my Land after her Mother’s Decease to her and her heirs forever.

Item  I give to my Daughter Prudence my Negro boy Dick provided that old John Freeman will acknowledge the land to his son Henry where he now Liveth to him and his heirs forever after so done I give the Negro to them and their heirs forever.

Item  I give to my Daughter ELIZ JONES my Negro boy Darby to her and her heirs forever and a Cow and Calf and two Pewter Dishes.

Item  I give to my Loving Wife Prudence all the rest of my Estate real and Personal with two Negroes named Lew and Robert to her and her heirs forever.

Now after all I make my Loving Wife Prudence my Executrix first to pay the Debts then the Legacys of this my Last Will and Testament. . . .

The known children of Arthur and Susannah Jones were Mary (b. c1682), Elizabeth (b. c1685), and Arthur (c1691-aft. 1762).  All children were born in Isle of Wight County, Virginia. 

The known children of Arthur and Prudence Jones were Prudence (c1698-c1760/m. 1717), and Sarah (b. c1699).  There is no basis for positing any other children for Prudence, including one named Elizabeth, as far as I can tell.  All children were probably born in Surry County, Virginia. 

After going over as many records as reasonably possible, I have no doubt left that Elizabeth did indeed marry John Browder I, and that this is the very reason that one finds no further record of Elizabeth Jones after 1715.  Following her marriage to John Browder, she, naturally, would have always been referred to as Elizabeth Browder.  And, as Arthur’s Will was written in January of 1715, Elizabeth Jones and John Browder I could easily have been married shortly afterward and still have had their first child, Arthur, later that same year. 

Elizabeth’s death prior to 1715 is simply conjecture, mainly based on a misreading of Arthur’s Will.  The fact that Arthur refers to Prudence as Mary’s “mother” is meaningless, since she was, effectively, Mary’s mother since Susanna had died.  Who else would be referred to as Mary’s “mother” in such a Will?  Arthur was not calling Prudence Mary’s natural mother, but her effective mother.  And, besides that, we, again, have proof that Mary, daughter of Susanna, still lived, regardless of what some seem to think. 

Mary Jones (b. c1682), daughter of Arthur and Susannah Jones, married Thomas Cooke, Sr. (c1677-November 22, 1736), son of William Cooke and Joane Roper, on February 17, 1702 in Isle of Wight County, Virginia (Isle of Wight Will & Deed Book I, p. 383/Chapman, p. 11).  Thomas was born in, and died in, Isle of Wight County. 

Boddie (p. 649) also mentions a deed made between “Thomas Cooke and Mary his wife, dau. of Arthur Jones” and Robert Sherrer, for 1200 pounds of tobacco, in which 50 acres of land where Robert Sherrer resided, located on the main Blackwater, was purchased.  This transaction took place on February 17, 1702, and was witnessed by John Brett, Richard Holliman and Thomas Harris (IOWCDB 1/no page reference provided).  So, with this land deed, the union between Thomas Cooke and Mary Jones, daughter of Arthur Jones, is proven. 

The known children of Thomas and Mary Cooke were Thomas, Jones, Sarah, Susanna, Benjamin, John, Rebecca, and Arthur.  Thomas Cooke, Sr. names these children in his Isle of Wight County Will, dated January 21, 1735. 

Arthur Jones, son of Arthur and Susannah Jones, married Mary Jordan (c1700-aft. 1762) about 1720.  Their known children were Arthur (b. 1722), and James (1725-c1780). 

Prudence Jones, daughter of Arthur and Prudence Jones, married Henry Freeman (c1690-1753), son of John Freeman, Sr.  The known children of Henry and Prudence Freeman were Josiah (c1720-1774), Henry (c1722-1776), Joel (b. c1724), Jones, Arthur (c1718-1752), and Amy (b. c1729). 

Now, to trace the Jones family descent, it begins with an unknown Jones who was born prior to 1506 and resided at Britton, Glouchestershire, England.  He had three known sons, John (bef. 1523-1584), unknown (b. bef. 1523), and Jones (bef. 1523-cMay, 1584).

John Jones (d. 1584), son of unknown Jones, was born at and died at Britton, Gloucestershire, England.  He wrote a Will in May of 1584 and refers in it to a lease of land he had in Oldland.  He married Maryann prior to 1540.  Their known children were Edmund (bef. 1540-aft. July 2, 1575), Anne, Thomas “the older” (b. bef. 1565), Thomas “the younger”, Joan/Jane, William, Anthony, Elizabeth, Julius, and John.

Nothing really seems to be known of Thomas Jones “the younger” except that he is the father of Robert Jones (bef. 1557-cNovember 1604) of Epikings Hall, according to John Jones’ Will.  Robert and his brother, John, would have inherited in the stead of their father, Thomas, if he died before his father, John. 

Robert Jones, son of Thomas Jones “the younger”, married Marjory (d. aft. November 1, 1604).  The known children of Robert and Marjory Jones were Arthur I (b. bef. 1574), and Rowland (bef. 1574-bef. November 1604). 

The Will of Robert Jones, written in November of 1604, provided mainly to his grandchildren, the children of Arthur Jones I, which was to be divided between them after Robert’s widow died. Apparently, Arthur was Robert’s only living child at the time of the writing of his Will, because Rowland had died.  Robert is buried at Oldland, England. 

Arthur Jones I married Eleanor Pitman on July 4, 1591 at Hanham, England.  Their known children were Martha, Mary (d. 1607), Edmund (b. c1594), Arthur II (b. c July 1597), Ffrydeswede (a female/b. c April 1602), Sara (b. c June 1604), and Henry (b. c November 1606).  All children were born at Hanham, England. 

Arthur Jones II married Joren Pychar on April 7, 1619 at Hanham, England.  The children of Arthur Jones II and Joren Pychar were Arthur III (c1624-1692), Edmund/Edward (b. c1626), Samuel (b. c1628-September 1631), Samuel (c1632-bef. November 16, 1699), and Richard (c1633-bef. August 29, 1692). 

Edmund/Edward Jones, son of Arthur Jones II and Joren Pychar, owned land in Isle of Wight County, Virginia by October 12, 1653.  It is interesting to note an instance in which Quakers were meeting, in 1663, at William Yarett’s home, located on Pagan River in Isle of Wight County.  It was illegal, according to Virginia law, to practice the Quaker religion or for Quakers to meet in that time period.  The meeting had been found out, and William Yarrett, along with Edward Jones [Sr.], were arrested and held for some time afterward in the house of the Sheriff because they attended this meeting (Boddie, p. 115/Weeks, p. 26). 

So, Edward Jones appears to have at least dabbled in this religion.  It is also known that Edward Jones, Sr., as well as Edward Jones, Jr., both took part in Bacon’s Rebellion, along with others such as Robert Flake (misspelled as Flacke), Samuel Cooke, John Harris, and John Clarke (Boddie, pp. 160-61). 

It is unknown, however, who Edmund Jones married.  Still, besides Lt. Arthur Jones he certainly had two known sons, Edward (bef. 1665-1722), Richard (c1666-c1721), as well as one daughter, Sara (c1667-c1726).  Edward Jones, Jr. married Deborah Exum (Boddie, p. 452). 

Henry King (d. c1669) was a son of John King (d. bef. May 14, 1655) and Jane.  Some say that Henry’s name was actually William, but it was more likely William Henry King. 

Henry King married Gulian Ruth Connor (c1636, England-c1678) about 1656 in Surry County, Virginia.  Gulian was transported to Virginia on January 24, 1656 by William “Caulfill” (Caulfield). 

Gulian died in Isle of Wight County, Virginia at only about forty-two years of age.  Prior to her death, since Mr. King had died early, she married again to (2) Darby Stantlin, and later to (3) Mathew Waikley. 

Boddie (p. 172) mentions several persons called “viewers of tobacco” for Isle of Wight County during the years 1639-40, which included Henry King, covering the area “from Castle Creek to the Alps”, along with William Lawson, Justinian Cooper, and William Ellis.  He further mentions (p. 539) a deed in which Maj. John Bond sold Thomas Harris a corn mill standing at the head of Pagan Creek on January 13, 1663.  This deed was witnessed by Henry King and Alice Phillips.  And Henry King, along with Alice Phillips, also witnessed a deed, dated October 20, 1664, in which Thomas Harris and Alice, his wife, sold Maj. John Bond 190 acres of land, located at the head of Pagan Creek (Boddie, p. 539-40/IOWCW&DB 1). 

Also, in 1665, Henry King agreed with Phil. Hanniford that, after Thomas Woodward measured 500 acres from the creek to the property of Mr. Ayers, that Phil. Hanniford could run a plot between Woodward and Henry King above the said branch.  This was witnessed by Robert Flake and John Harris.  And, on September 2, 1665, Henry King had 100 acres of land which he allowed Frances England to have in exchange for 100 of England’s land.  Robert Flake and Thomas Woodward witnessed this deed (Boddie, p. 543/IOWCW&DB 1). 

And on June 4, 1666, Robert Flake gave John Proctor, son of Ambrose Proctor, Sr. some property.  This was witnessed by Martin Luther and Henry King (Boddie, pp. 544-45/IOWCW&DB 1).  Finally, on April 18, 1668, Henry King was granted 1,000 acres of land located on the first swamp of the Blackwater, 750 acres of this having formerly been granted to John Sweet on September 25, 1643 (Boddie, p. 688). 

In his Will, dated March 2, 1668, and probated on May 3, 1669, Henry King left legacies to his daughters, Susanna and Elizabeth, of 900 acres of land, which had been given to him by his deceased father, and which had been confirmed by his mother, Elizabeth, since deceased.  He also names his wife, Ruth, to whom he gave 100 acres of land, located next to the property of Mr. England.  And he gave legacies to his Parish for the maintenance of a free school; to his servant, John Russell; and to his two daughters “to be brought up to learning.”  Witnesses were Edward Poynter and John Holly (IOWCW&AB 1, p. 61/Chapman, p. 10). 

The Will of Darby Stantlin, dated April 25, 1670, and recorded on October 25, 1670, left legacies to his wife, Gulian, whom he states was “sick at the present time.”  He stated that, should she die, that his friend Mathew Waikley was to remain on his plantation until Henry King [her son] “becomes of age.”  Also, “[i]n case of wife’s death and of her son Henry King” his plantation was to go to Mathew Waikley.  His Will was witnessed by Nicolas Cobb, William Cole, and Richard Sharpe. 

Bequests to his son, Henry King, were acknowledged in open court by Gulian Stantlin, and confirmed by Mathew Waikley, “her new husband” (IOWCW&AB 1, p. 90/Chapman, p. 14). 

Henry and Gulian King, as can be seen here, had at least one son, who was probably their only son, Henry (b. c1656/58).  Since he was orphaned as a child, he chose Robert Flake to be his guardian on February 10, 1672.  He later married (1) Katherine Clarke, daughter of John Clarke (d. 1717) and Mary Flake of Surry County, Virginia, in 1684 (Surry County [small] Deed Book 2, p. 13/Chapman, p. 29).  Henry King later married (2) Sarah Relick (or Williams, as some believe).  Boddie (p. 521) mentions the following:

Whereas pat. was granted 19 April 1648 to John King and Lawrence Ward for 500 acres at head [of] Pagan Creek, John King being dead said LAWRENCE WARD conveys unto King’s wife MRS. ELIZABETH KING, widow.  14 May 1655.  Teste, Thomas Davis. 

So, we know that John King, father of Henry King, lived to at least April 19, 1648, and that he was deceased by May 14, 1655.  We know, then, that, at the time of his death, he had a wife named Elizabeth.  Whether she was a second wife, or other researchers are incorrect, or her name was, perhaps, Elizabeth Jane, we may never know.

The known children of John and Elizabeth Browder, several of whom are known to have been baptized by the Rev. George Robertson, were Arthur (c1715-c1738), Edmund (b. c1716), John II (1717-c1814), Richard (c1719-c1807), William (July 7, 1723-c1763), George (Feb. 5, 1731-c1819), Anne (b. Feb. 13, 1733), and Joseph (February 2, 1741-March 1801), and possibly others. 

It is interesting to note that, among these children, for the first time, as far as is known in the Browder family, we have the names Arthur, Richard, Joseph, and Susanna.  These names correlate to names of children of Lt. Arthur Jones.  The other names, John, William, Edmund and George were already in the Browder family.  

As we will see, the names Arthur, Richard, Joseph, Frederick, Margaret, and Jones (as a given name) appear EXCLUSIVELY within the family lines descended directly from John and Elizabeth Browder.  This shows a direct correlation to the Jones family from which they likely descend.  It should also be noted that, had they been descended from Peter Jones, as famous as he was, one would expect some descendants to have been named Peter.  But I have never yet found a Peter Browder, for example. 

One should also note that NO descendant, as far as is known, of John and Elizabeth Browder was named Prudence, so the likelihood is increased that Elizabeth was the daughter of Susanna King rather than Prudence Baldwin.  That is another reason that I favor the belief that the first Elizabeth Jones lived past childhood, despite what some researchers suggest.

Now, it should also be noted that there are some gaps between these children, most notably between the years 1719 and 1723, 1723 and 1731, as well as 1733 and 1741.  It is entirely possible that this couple had more children during these year gaps, and might even have had more after 1741, even though Elizabeth would have been about 54 years of age by that time.  I would find the latter unlikely, however.  

Finally, it seems obvious to me that John Browder I died about 1757. The reason for this is that on Dec. 3, 1757, a John Browder, a Richard Browder, and a “Wm. Browder” are all listed in the Dublin County, Ireland Court of Chancery Bill Books 1757-1759, Vol. 35, p. 119. It is a good possibility that it might pertain to our John Browder, Richard Browder, and William Browder, all sons of John Browder I. In fact, it is highly unlikely that the references could be to anyone else other than the three brothers because, even though other possible relatives are listed in said records, (1) these three have the correct surname spelling for our family and (2) are all referenced in the very same record on the same page. The question here is just what said record pertained to and whether the brothers had to return to Ireland in order to deal with it, afterward returning back home.

An additional question would be what might have taken place around this year for them to be referenced in such records. I can only suggest that it might have taken that long for any part of their father’s estate, if any existed, to finally be settled in Ireland. It is entirely possible that only the three brothers made the trip but represented all of the other siblings in so doing. Could it possibly be that it was because of such a trip that later generations remembered three Browder brothers arriving in Virginia from Ireland? In such a case, they simply forgot what the trip was about and relegated it to immigration.

Please recall that Arthur Browder was deceased and his brother, Edmund Browder, died about this time also, so he was probably too ill to make the trip with the other three. George and Anne were living, but George may not have wanted to make the trip, and Anne would have been represented by someone else in any case. That left Joseph, who was likely considered too young to make the trip.

Regardless of the circumstances, it is clear from the record title that it concerned land. This seems to be a clear indication that John B. Browder I died about this time, leaving some land in Ireland as part of his estate which had to be dealt with.

Select Bibliography:

Boddie, John Bennett.  Seventeenth Century Isle of Wight County, Virginia, A History of the County of Isle of Wight, Virginia, During the Seventeenth Century, Including Abstracts of the County Records [by Eliza Timberlake Davis].  Chicago Law Printing Co., 1938.

Browder, Blanche Penland.  Notes On the Browder Family of Tidewater Virginia, 1695-1850, Second Edition, Copyright: Blanche Penland Browder, printed in USA by Blanche Penland Browder.

Chamberlayne, Churchill Gibson, trans.  Births from the Bristol Parish Register of Henrico, Prince George, and Dinwiddie Counties, Virginia, 1720-1798.  Genealogical Pub. Co., Inc., 1974.

Chamberlayne, Churchill Gibson, trans.  The Vestry Book and Register of Bristol Parish, Virginia, 1720-1789, Richmond, Virginia:  Pub. by: Churchill Gibson Chamberlayne, privately printed, 1898.

Chapman, Blanche Adams.  Wills and Administrations of Isle of Wight County, Virginia, 1674-1800. Books I-III, 1938.

Duda, Thomas Walter.  “Genealogical Notes Of The Jones Family.”

Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia

Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated Dec. 28, 2025)

Richard Browder (c1719-c1807), son of John Browder (c1686/87-p. 1741) and Elizabeth Jones (c1685-p. 1741), the brother of Arthur Browder (1715-c1738) and the father of Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), the latter being the wife of my ancestor Isham C. Browder (c1774-1832), was born in Prince George (later Dinwiddie) County, Virginia, where he resided all of his life.  He and his family resided in the tidewater region near the waters of Gravelly Run at old Petersburg.  This family appears to have been members of the Church of England, and no known record indicates that they ever converted to any other denomination, even after the Revolution. 

Richard Browder is said to have married “Mary Thompson, daughter of Dr. James Thompson”, but instead, I am convinced, actually married Mary Epes Randolph, daughter of William Randolph (c1690-c1726) and Mary Epes (c1700-c1753/Will in Chesterfield County Will Book [CCWB] 1, p. 85/Order Book 1, p. 353), about 1741.  Mary Epes Randolph was actually the step-daughter of Dr. James Thompson, MD. 

Mary Epes Randolph was born about 1720 in Henrico County, Virginia, and surely lived past at least 1778, if she was the wife of Richard Browder.  William Randolph, her father, was a son of Capt. Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann. 

One will recall that David Parrish Browder, Sr. stated that Richard Browder had taken in the child of his brother, Arthur Browder, upon Arthur’s death.  And it should conversely be remembered that family tradition holds that Richard Browder brought up the child of one of his brothers.  Arthur is the only one of Richard’s brethren to have died within the right time frame to fit.  And the child who is named in family tradition is my ancestor, Isham Browder (c1732-1784).  The sources who named Isham as this child are good sources because they are great-grandchildren of Richard and Mary Browder, thus, not very far removed generationally.  But, that does not mean that they got everything right.  They simply did the best they could with the information available to them. 

In addition to this, there is one other presumed link.  Mary Epes, daughter of Col Francis Epes III (c1659-Jan. 1718), not only married William Randolph, but was also a descendant of the Isham family herself through her mother, Anne Elizabeth Isham (1665-1718), just as Mary Isham Randolph (c1716-Dec. 1772) was. 

Knowing that Richard Browder also married a Randolph, who was also descended from the Isham family, just as his brother Arthur had done, it becomes obvious that after the death of Arthur Browder (1715-c1738), it would only have been logical for the Richard Browder family to be chosen to bring Isham Browder up, since Richard and Mary genetically represented both Isham’s paternal and maternal relatives themselves.  Why else would it have been Richard Browder, rather than either of his two older brothers, Edmund or John Browder II, for example, who took in young Isham Browder? 

Richard Browder himself appears for the first time in the land tax records of Prince George and Dinwiddie Counties, 1782-1787 (the first to be done after the American Revolutionary War).  However, he certainly owned land there well prior to 1782.  Richard continues to appear in said records until 1807/08, so we know he lived to at least 1807 (Penland Browder, p. 27). 

Between 1782 and 1787, Richard Browder is listed in said records as owning 196 acres of land in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  He also owned six slaves, three horses and eight cows.  So, the family made their living primarily by farming and apparently did not produce tobacco.  In fact, by this time tobacco had lost favor as an economically suitable crop, so most people had turned to simply farming other crops, mainly corn. 

Again, Richard Browder is known to have died about 1807.  His death at this time is mainly evidenced by the Power of Attorney initiated by Isham C. Browder and his wife, Martha Browder, in 1808, which is discussed elsewhere. 

The known children of Richard Browder and Mary Epes Randolph “Mary Thompson”, were Arthur (c1742-c1786), Susanna (c1745-p. Apr. 1834), William (c1748-c1802), Littlebury “Littleberry” (c1754-1846), David (c1755-c1813), Thompson (c1758-c1804), John (c1759-c1800), Mary “Polly” Thompson (Dec. 7, 1762-p. 1844), Wilmuth (c1763-bef. Apr. 1834), Nancy A. (c1764-p. 1844), and Martha (c1768-c1811). 

 Sadly, we really do not know much more about Richard and Mary Browder directly.  They lived, brought up children, and passed on.  Their burial places are also not known.  One might think that there must have been a Browder family cemetery near the main family home.  But, to my knowledge, no such cemetery has ever been found.  Of course, it is always possible that both Richard and Mary, as well as any children and grandchildren who did not move away, might have been buried in the Blanford Church Cemetery. 

Select Bibliography:

Browder, Blanche Penland. Notes On the Browder Family of Tidewater Virginia, 1695-1850, Second Edition, Copyright: Blanche Penland Browder, printed in USA by Blanche Penland Browder.

Posted byjmarkbrowderSeptember 4, 2019December 28, 2025Posted inUncategorizedLeave a comment on Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia

Mary Epes Randolph, Step-Daughter of Dr. James Thompson and wife of Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated and corrected, Dec. 28, 2025)

Mary Epes (1700-c1753), daughter of Col. Francis Epes III (1657/59-Jan. 1718) and Anne Elizabeth Isham (1665-1718), married (1) William Randolph, Gent. (c1690-c1726), son of Capt. Henry Randolph II (Jan. 16, 1665-Feb. 26, 1693) and Sarah Swann (c1672-1714) about 1718, and later, (2) Dr. James Thompson.  She married James on February 8, 1724/25.  These are facts, as can be found in the records. 

Mary Epes was born about 1700 at Bermuda Hundred, Henrico County, Virginia, and was deceased by June 1, 1753.  According to the “Epes Book”, Mary Epes may have been “Mistress Mary Epes a pretty girl” mentioned in the diary of William Byrd of Westover on February 17, 1709. 

Dr. James Thompson was a physician.  He was born about 1696, also at Bermuda Hundred in Henrico County, Virginia.  James died in 1746/47. 

Mary is believed by some to have remarried after his death to (3) George Robertson, but I have been unable to find evidence to support this. 

The children of William Randolph and Mary Epes were Isham (before August, 1720-August 1751), Mary Epes (b. c1720), and an unknown female (b. c1722).  The daughter of Dr. James Thompson and Mary Epes was Anne (c1730-c1793).  James did not have a daughter named Mary.

Anne Thompson, daughter of Dr. James Thompson and Mary Epes (1700-c1753), married John Sturdivant (c1719-c1793).  The known children of John and Ann Sturdivant were; Mary Epes (b. Jan. 18, 1750), Thompson, a son (b. Sept. 11, 1752), Ann Isham (b. Oct. 10, 1754), John (b. May 20, 1756), Sally (b. July 20, 1758), and Joel (b. Jan. 15, 1764).  These are taken from the Bristol Parish Register. 

Isham Randolph, son of William Randolph and Mary Epes, never married.  He died in Henrico (now Chesterfield) County, Virginia.  On October 25, 1751, the inventory of Isham Randolph’s estate was presented by Burwell Green (Chesterfield County Will Book [CCWB]1, 1749-1753, p. 207).  The account of Isham’s estate was sworn to in court by Burwell Green, administrator, in May of 1756 (Chesterfield County Will Book [CCWB] 1, 1749-1753, p. 206). 

It is of interest to note that, on May 1, 1748, “Henry Randolph, Jr. of Dale Parish”, deeded to George Cousins, for £40, 447 acres of land located in Dale Parish, next to the properties of Field Archer, George Cousins, Gills, Widow Franklin, Col. Poythress, and Isham Randolph, this land being part of 1,790 acres which was patented to Col. Francis Epes, grandfather of Isham Randolph, but which had lapsed (because Isham died), and was then granted to Henry Randolph, Jr.  There were no witnesses, but it was signed by Henry Randolph and recorded on May 1, 1749 (Arlandson, Randolphs of Early Virginia; Chesterfield County Deed Book, 1748-1750, p. 99). 

This was Henry Randolph IV, son of Henry Randolph III and Elizabeth Eppes, sister of Mary Eppes. This deed alone proves that Mary Epes married William Randolph because their son, Isham, was patented these lands by his own grandfather, Francis Epes. But after the death of Isham Randolph, this property was transferred to Henry Randolph IV, son of Henry Randolph III and Elizabeth Epes.

Mary Epes Randolph (b. c1720), daughter of William Randolph (d. c1723) and Mary Epes (1700-c1753), married Richard Browder (c1719-c1807), about 1741.

The unknown female (b. c1722) married (1) Burwell Green (d. aft. 1751).  They had at least two daughters, Mary Isham Green and Betty Green, who are named in their grandmother’s Will. 

It is important here to provide some detail about William Randolph, Gent. The documentation is as follows: First, on December 1, 1707, Giles Webb and his wife, Sarah, brought suit against John Stuart due to a deed, dated Oct. 29, 1695, in which Giles and his wife, Sarah, allowed for fifteen years Stuart to dwell upon said plantation which was on Swift Creek where Henry Randolph, deceased, formerly lived, and Stuart was to keep the dwelling house there in good repair. However, a fire burnt said house on Dec. 30, 1705, and Stuart refused to repair it. Therefore, Giles and his wife, Sarah, were asking for 10,000 lbs. of tobacco, which was the bond.

Please understand that there is no indication that the Col. William Randolph I side of the family ever owned any lands at Swift Creek. It was the Henry Randolph line who owned lands there.

On August 5, 1709, Giles Webb of Henrico County, Virginia wrote his will. To his loving wife, Sarah, he bequeathed all of his lands during her lifetime, etc. To “son-in-law” (step-son), Henry Randolph (III), the other half of his goods, etc. After wife’s death, his lands were to go to his brother, Thomas Webb, of Gloucester, England. Will was proven, June 1, 1713.

In the meantime, on February 5, 1710, “Henry Randolph, Jun’r., son and heir at law of Henry Randolph of the parish of Bristol, dec’d” deeded to John and Thomas Pleasants, Gents., 175 acres of land in Henrico County, Virginia upon which two mills and a dam stood, on Swift Creek, “which land and mills descended to me as heir of my father, who was son and heir of Henry Randolph Sen’r the patentee and builder of said Mills, Land, &c. . . . Beginning at a tree on the North side of Swift Creek, parting this land and the land now of John Archer, being part of the Saw Mill divident, (so called). . . .”

The above is Henry Randolph III. It is not certain which John Pleasants is being referred to here, but he was a relative in any case. Thomas Pleasants was, presumably, a brother of John.

A distinct pattern begins to emerge here. Careful attention to detail suggests that Henry Randolph III “inherited” directly from his father, Henry Randolph II, even though Henry Randolph II did not leave a will. There is no indication of William “inheriting” anything from him directly. But apparently, there was a plan for him in any case.

Note that John Archer I above married Martha Feild, daughter of Maj. Peter Feild and Judith Soane, widow of Henry Randolph I. John Archer II married Sarah Randolph, daughter of the above Henry Randolph III and Elizabeth Eppes.

That Henry Randolph III calls himself, “son and heir at law of Henry Randolph of the parish of Bristol, dec’d” does not mean that he was the only son and heir. But he may have been the only direct heir, for whatever reason.

Later, on March 7, 1716, Thomas Webb of Gloucester, England, deeded to William Randolph, all lands in Virginia left him by his brother, Giles Webb.

There can be no real mistake here. Thomas Webb is deeding to William Randolph lands that had been passed to him by his brother through Sarah Swann, who married his brother, Giles, to a son of Henry Randolph II, first husband of Sarah Swann. There is NO reason why this would be any other William Randolph than the son of Henry Randolph II. If William were not a son of Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann, then these lands would surely have been deeded to Henry Randolph III. If Henry Randolph II had left William Randolph out of his inheritance, his mother, Sarah, cleverly, did not.

Aside from this, William Randolph first appears in the extant records in August of 1711 in a deed in which John Archer and his wife, Martha, deed to William Randolph, 961 acres of land left by Peter Feild to said Martha, his daughter. This John Archer was John Archer I who married Martha Feild, daughter of Peter Feild and Judith Soane, widow of Capt. Henry Randolph I.

Again, the above William Randolph, it seems obvious, is a son of Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann and he had just turned 21 years of age. This William Randolph would be the brother of Henry Randolph III, who married Mary Eppes, daughter of Col. Francis Eppes III and Anne Elizabeth Isham. Henry Randolph III married Elizabeth Eppes, sister of Mary Eppes.

Again, there can be no real mistake here. Thomas Webb is deeding to William Randolph lands that had been passed to him by his brother through Sarah Swann, who married his brother, Giles, to a son of Henry Randolph II, first husband of Sarah Swann.

Further, on September 28, 1716, Thomas Webb, Gent. of the city of Gloucester, England deeded to William Randolph, Gent., of Virginia, several other tracts of land in Henrico County, Virginia “which Giles Webb late of the said Colony deceased devised to the said Thomas Webb in fee simple. . . .” John Bolling was attorney for Thomas Webb for the Prince George County section. Other attorneys appointed in his stead by Thomas Webb for Henrico County property were Joseph Royall, Jr., Francis Epes, Jr., Thomas Randolph, and Richard Randolph, all of Henrico County, Virginia. Signed: Thomas Randolph, Isham Randolph, John Randolph, Edward Hill, E. Randolph, Thomas Posford, etc. (Henrico County, Virginia Wills and Deeds Book, 1714-1718, pp. 157-58).

Again, the above William Randolph is obviously a son of Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann. On first reading of this document, one might think that the William Randolph in question must have been William Randolph II, son of Col. William Randolph I and Mary Isham, who married Elizabeth Beverly since almost all of his brothers are named or signed said document. However, one must remember that all of these persons were Justices and commonly selected as attorneys by many people. Plus, they were relatives.

Further, on June 28, 1717, William Randolph of Henrico County, Virginia deeded to William Byrd of Charles City County, Virginia, 418 acres of land on the south side of the James River near Falling Creek, “lately in possession of Giles Webb, deced”, bought by Webb from Andrew Martin, and 132 acres of land on the north side of the James River, bounded by the lands of the said William Byrd. Witnesses: Charles Griffin and Richard Randolph (Henrico County, Virginia Wills and Deeds Book, 1714-1718, pp. 181-82).

Then, on July 1, 1717, William Randolph of Henrico County, Virginia deeds to Thomas Wood, Jr., of same, 277 acres of land on the north side of the James River in Henrico Parish and County, sold by Joshua Stepp, Jr. to Giles Webb, Gent., and from him to his brother, Thomas Webb, and by Thomas Webb to William Randolph. Witnesses: Joseph Pleasants and John Redford (Henrico County, Virginia Wills and Deeds Book, 1714-1718, p. 183).

And on July 1, 1717, William Randolph of Henrico County, Virginia deeds to John Bolling, Gent., of same, 150 acres of land in Henrico Parish and County on the south side of the James River, bounded by the land formerly owned by Henry Lound and of John Bowman, Jr., land which Henry Gee conveyed to Giles Webb and by Giles Webb to his brother, Thomas Webb, and by Thomas Webb to William Randolph, Witnesses: William Kennon and Joseph Royall, Jr.

“At a Court held for Henrico County the first day of July 1717 William Randolph acknowledged this Deed with the Livery and Seizen acknowledged to be his Act and Ded, and the Land with the … admitted to Record. Then Elizabeth his wife being first personally examined … her right of Dower in the land hereby conveyed. . . .” (Henrico County, Virginia Wills and Deeds Book, 1714-1718, pp. 186-87).

This is William Randolph, Gent., son of Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann, who married Mary Eppes, in both of the above deeds. It is apparent from this document, however, it appears obvious now, that William Randolph had first been married to someone named Elizabeth. It is easy here to jump to the conclusion that these deeds are really referring to William Randolph II who married Elizabeth Beverly. But that simply would not make sense given the preponderance of the evidence.

Finally, on June 3, 1726 (dated 1727, but should be 1726), John Stewart, planter, of Henrico County, Virginia deeded to “William Randolph of the same county, Gent.”, 400 acres of land in Henrico County, Virginia near Fighting Creek lately surveyed by Francis Eppes, County Surveyor, part of a patent lately granted to Stewart on Nov. 24, 1725. Witnesses: John Redford, John Corn and Thomas Short, Jr. (Henrico County, Virginia Wills and Deeds Book, No. 1, Part 1, 1725-1737, pp. 103-04).

Again, the reason we know that this has to do with William Randolph, son of Henry Randolph II and Sarah Swann, is that there was a lawsuit against John Stuart, dated Dec. 1, 1707, in which Giles Webb and his wife, Sarah [Swann], brought suit due to a deed dated Oct. 29, 1695 in which Giles and his wife, Sarah, allowed for fifteen years Stuart to dwell upon their plantation on Swift Creek. Stuart must have purchased this land in the meantime and then sold it to William Randolph. But William, sadly, died soon thereafter and his wife, Mary Eppes, married Dr. James Thomoson the next year.

To conclude the proof documentation concerning William Randolph, it must be noted that in 1753, during the May session of the Chesterfield County, Virginia Court, the following order was made. “Ordered that the petition of Samuel Gordon admer de canis non of James Thompson decd against Elizabeth Randolph decd. be dismissed.” Following this entry on the same page is the same order concerning Benjamin Ratcliff (Chesterfield County, Virginia County Court Order Book 1, 1749-1754, p. 341).

The questions here are, (1) who was Samuel Gordon, (2) who was Elizabeth Randolph, and (3) who was Benjamin Ratcliff?

First, John Pleasants, son of John Pleasants and Dorothea Cary, purchased land from Samuel Gordon according to his 1771 will. The elder John Pleasant’s first wife was Mary Woodson. This doesn’t mean much in and of itself except that the Woodsons were related to the Randolphs and the Gordons appear to have been related to the Pleasants’ and the Parsons’. But how did he become administrator of the estate of Dr. James Thompson? Since he was a Justice in Henrico County, it is possible that he was simply appointed to said task by the Court, although he was a relative too, as was usually the case in such matters. That John Pleasants had a son named Samuel may be a further clue. Of course, the records show that John Pleasants sued Samuel Gordon too, but the cause is not known. I have actually found very little else to go on concerning Samuel Gordon.

As for the Ratcliffs, the family seems mainly to have been Quakers, as the Woodsons also were. So, this may be the main connection between them. I found absolutely nothing else about Benjamin Ratcliff.

Now to Elizabeth Randolph. Elizabeth Beverly-Randolph, wife of William Randolph II, was deceased by this time. But why would her estate be sued? It is understood that she died about 1723 (although this is a guess since the death year on her tombstone is unreadable), several years prior to her husband’s death. So, if an estate were to be sued between the two, it would have been his, not hers.

James Thompson was connected to the Henry Randolph line, not that of Col. William Randolph I. The preponderance of the evidence clearly shows this. That there are connections between the two Randolph families, however, is natural, especially since both lines married into the Eppes family. But if it is about the estate of Dr. James Thompson, and the suits are against (1) Elizabeth Randolph and (2) Benjamin Ratcliff, it would seem most likely that we are talking about a first wife of William Randolph, Gent., as the records seem to suggest. If indeed he had a first wife named Elizabeth, as the records appear to show, this is the Elizabeth Randolph (d. c1717) who is mentioned here. It is highly likely, then, that Benjamin Ratcliff was the executor of her estate and that it had not all been finalized even though she also died several years prior to this point. I suggest, but cannot prove, that, perhaps, she was an Elizabeth Ratcliffe. But there are several other names that could be proposed, including Woodson, for example.

Let’s be clear here. Siblings and other relatives don’t always get along. There is no indication that William Randolph, Gent. and Henry Randolph III had any real interaction with one another. But that does not prove that they were not siblings. That they married sisters indicates that they may have gotten along up to the point of these marriages, but that something might have transpired that changed their relationship. Perhaps we will never really know.

Regardless, it seems obvious that Henry was the older of the two, but only by about a year or so. One might like to think that it might have instead been Elizabeth who was the mother of William Randolph. That would make sense except that then one would have to answer why Sarah Swann was evidently so intent upon ensuring that William receive some sort of inheritance. It would make more sense to suggest that Henry was a son of the previously unknown Elizabeth. I am not stating this is a fact, of course, just a suggestion.

Anyway, for those who insist that Henry and William were not related due to supposed DNA evidence, I suggest that some further checking is in order now.

As for Dr. James Thompson, he was a witness to the Will of Peter Jones of Bristol Parish, Prince George County, Virginia, in 1721.  He later purchased land in Prince George County, Virginia on February 8, 1724/25.  He was a resident of Henrico County, Virginia by September 12, 1726, when he sold this land and his wife, Mary, relinquished her dower right to said land. 

In an indenture dated April 1, 1728, land was bought from Joseph Willson of Bristol Parish, County of Prince George, and sold to Theophilus Field of Elizabeth City County, of 169 acres on the south side of Appomattox River called “Boyling Springs.”  This tract of land adjoined lands of Richard Vaughn, Daniel Vaughn, John Vaughn and William Vaughn, and was “all the land Joseph Willson bought of Dr. James Thompson on that side of the Appomattox River.” 

It seems obvious that the land bought by Joseph Wilson is the land sold by Dr. James Thompson in 1726.  This deed (dated 1728) was witnessed by J. A. Mumford, George Willson, and Thomas Hardaway, and was signed by Joseph Willson.  It was recorded on April 9, 1728 in PGCW&DB 1713-1728, p. 1098. 

On August 17, 1731, James Thompson of Henrico County, Virginia gave power of attorney to his wife, Mary, to conduct all necessary business.  This was done in preparation for a move to Edgecombe Precinct, North Carolina, where they settled by November of 1731.  Isham Randolph, Mary’s son, also made this move along with them.  It is interesting to note that there is no mention of Mary Epes Randolph as having made the move along with the others. But perhaps she did.

Once relocated, the couple were witnesses to a land deed with William Epes.  In 1734 a deed between James Thompson of Edgecombe Precinct and Henry Goff of same was witnessed by Isham Randolph, Alexander Wright and William Epes, Sr.  The James Thompson family all returned to Henrico County, Virginia sometime later. 

Significantly, in October of 1740, Mary Epes Randolph, orphan of William Randolph, Gent., chose Dr. James Thompson as her guardian.  John Robertson, Gent, held security (Arlandson, “Randolphs of Early Virginia”; Court Orders 1737-1746, p. 126).  This demonstrates that Mary was unmarried at the time.  Why so many years had transpired between the marriage of Dr. James Thompson and Mary Epes and this guardianship selection is not certain. 

This is a key document that not only shows that she had chosen Thompson as her guardian, but also that she was not yet married to Richard Browder. It also demonstrates how Mary became known as Mary Thompson rather than Mary Randolph. Family tradition had always stated that Richard Browder married Mary Thompson, daughter of Dr. James Thompson. But, again, he had no daughter named Mary.

The documentation, verbatim, reads, “Thompson Guard – Mary Randolph one of the Orphants (sic) of William Randolph Gent Deced coms (sic) into Court and chooses Doctor James Thompson to be her Guardian who accept. Thereof and enters into Bond for the Same. John Robertson Gent [son of Rev. George Robertson, Sr. and Mary Worsham (daughter of Capt. John Worsham I)] Security. Present Richard Royall Gent.” (Henrico County, Virginia Court Orders 1737-1746, p. 126).

Later, on September 6, 1751, Mary Epes-Thompson, mother of Isham Randolph, dcsd., having relinquished her right of administration; Burwell Green, as the greatest creditor was appointed as Administrator of Isham’s estate.  William Pride and Edward Hill held security (Arlandson, Randolphs of Early Virginia; Chesterfield County Order Book [CCOB] 1, p. 138).  

Still later, on October 4, 1751, Mary Epes-Thompson, widow of James Thompson, was summoned to accept or refuse administration of her late husband’s estate (ibid, p. 147), an action which became necessary due to the death of her son, Isham Randolph, who had also previously been named as his Executor. She did not accept and administration of his estate fell to Samuel Gordon.

Isham Randolph, son of William Randolph and Mary Epes, had also been a witness to James Thompson’s (of Dale Parish, Chesterfield County, Virginia) Will, which was dated March 6, 1746, and proven and recorded on the first Monday in November,1747 in Henrico County, Virginia (Chesterfield County Deed Book 1744-1748, pp. 314-314a).  This Will names Thompson’s own daughter Anne, his wife, Mary, and his “son-in-law” (actually, step-son) Isham Randolph (again, Mary Epes-Thompson and Isham Randolph were both named as Executors).  Other witnesses were Field Archer (1711-1785), Elizabeth Archer, and Katherine Mail. 

The Will stipulated that half of James Thompson’s estate was to be given to his wife, Mary, and the other half to his daughter, Ann Sturdivant.  No mention is made of Mary Epes Randolph, daughter of his wife, Mary.  It is entirely possible that, since she was not actually his daughter, he did not wish to leave anything to her, or to her brother, Isham.  They might have, presumably, been expected to have inherited from their mother later, after all. 

As an aside, in 1749, after James Thompson died, James Randolph purchased land from (1) Joseph Kennedy of Mill Creek and, (2) 200 acres of land from Benjamin Bordon on the corner of Samuel McClure’s and James Thompson’s line, also on Mill Creek. 

Mary Thompson’s (of Dale Parish) Will was dated January 23, 1753 and probated in Chesterfield County, Virginia on June 1, 1753 (CCOB 1, 1749-1753, pp. 85-88/p. 353).  She had died by April 6, 1753 (ibid, p. 330). Mary left half of her property, as well as seven negroes, to her daughter, Anne Sturdivant (the daughter of James Thompson), and the other half, along with eight negroes, to her granddaughters, Mary Isham Green and Betty Green.  Mary also mentioned her grandchildren, Mary Epes Sturdivant and Thompson Sturdivant, in her Will.  Her daughter, Anne Sturdivant, and Ann’s husband, John Sturdivant, were named as Executors (Saunders, Early Settlers of North Alabama, p. 475).  Her own daughter, Mary, was not mentioned and received nothing. Perhaps, then, she had received her inheritance when she married Richard Browder. Things like this often happened.

The following is an excerpt of a judgment relating to the Will of Mary Epes-Randolph (note that she is referred to as Mary Randolph in said judgment, not Mary Thompson, even though she signed her will as “Mary Thompson”), from BCDB 7, 95:

Joseph King and William Thornton are held and firmly bound unto Burwell Green and Benjamin Hunt of Sussex County, in the sum of 500 pounds, that whereas the above Burwell Green and Benjamin Hunt became bound unto William Lightfoot, first Justice of the Commission of the Peace of the County of Sussex, for the due execution of the Last Will and Testament MARY RANDOLPH, deceased, to make or cause to be made a true and perfect inventory of all and singular the goods, chattel and credits of the said deceased which should come into the hands of Joseph King to fulfill his bond, dated the 22nd day of June, 1756. Signed by Joseph King (his mark) and William Thornton. Witnesses were Wm. Clack and James Maclin Junr. Bond proved by the oaths of William Clack and James Maclin Junr., in Court on April 26, 1762.
