It is clear that both Mary Isham Green and Betty Green were both minors, and still unmarried, when the Will was written.  And it is noted in said will that both were under 14 years of age at the time.  They also would have to have been born prior to October of 1740, due to the guardianship judgment.  So, they would both have been born between about 1738, at the earliest, and October of 1740.  Whatever happened to them, beyond this point in time, is unknown, however, because I have found no further record of either of them.  It is entirely possible that both reached adulthood and married.  But that is not a certainty. 

Again, one must keep in mind that family tradition has always held that Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, married “Mary Thompson, daughter of Dr. James Thompson”.  So, unless one wishes to posit that this is an error and that he must have married some other Mary Thompson (or even some other Mary), then one has to accept that Richard actually married this Mary Epes Randolph, step-daughter of Dr. James Thompson.  There literally is no other conclusion to be drawn.  The facts are clear.  Dr. James Thompson did not have a daughter named Mary, and Mary Epes Randolph, his step-daughter, requested that he become her guardian in 1740, prior to her marriage to Richard Browder.  Whatever the circumstances really were, this has to be the lady referred to as “Mary Thompson, daughter of Dr. James Thompson” in family tradition.

Posted byjmarkbrowderSeptember 4, 2019December 28, 2025Posted inUncategorized15 Comments on Mary Epes Randolph, Step-Daughter of Dr. James Thompson and wife of Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia

The Earliest Isham Browders and Other Related Persons:

The Earliest Isham Browders and Other Related Persons:

By. John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div.

The plain fact of the matter is that the Isham Browder (c1732-c1784), who married Talitha Cox (c1745-July 3, 1828), was the very first person in the Browder family to be given the name Isham.  This Isham Browder was a colonial planter and a politician.  He was very prominent in society, very wealthy, and even had a mansion built in which he and his family dwelt.  How could this be if he were the son of a plantation bailiff who was by no means wealthy?  And, how could this be if he were brought up by his uncle, Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, within a not so very prominent family household?  How did he acquire his wealth and prominence, both of which were decidedly more than any other Browder had during that time period? 

            Even though I have failed to locate any actual record, to date, which would prove my contention, it is, again, my belief that this Isham Browder (c1732-c1784) inherited property and wealth through the very wealthy and socially prominent Randolph family, but perhaps not directly.  One has to understand that no member of the Browder family of that time period possessed that kind of wealth other than this Isham Browder.  And we know where Jones Browder got his wealth, which was fairly considerable and came close to rivaling it.  So, from where else could Isham have obtained this wealth if not from some inheritance? 

            In addition, one must remember that Arthur Browder (c1715-c1739) did have at least one infant heir.  Arthur did possess property to be passed on.  In whatever way it was actually carried out, his property must have been passed down to this Isham Browder (c1732-c1784) by inheritance, perhaps by way of the Randolph family, who might have held it in trust.  And for this to have taken place, there must have been some familial relationship between them.  And the Browder and Randolph families of Old Virginia did often perpetuate at least a friendly relationship, evidently even after the death of Arthur Browder (c1715-c1739). 

            In any case, it is here that I will emphasize that the preponderance of the evidence (which is still extant) proves beyond a reasonable doubt that Isham Browder (c1732-c1784) was indeed the son of Arthur Browder and Mary Isham Randolph, and that, in fact, his name was actually Thomas Isham Browder, he, being named, in part, after his grandfather, Thomas Randolph.  I will present evidence for his name at a later point. 

            Still, the question remains as to exactly why so many Browders during this time period were given the name Isham.  They could not have all been directly descended from Mary Isham Randolph!  Indeed, as we shall see, they were not.  But they were all closely related, and all descend from John (c1690-p. 1760) and Elizabeth (1686/87-p. 1750) Browder, for sure. 

            The very first reason why any Browder would have been given the name “Isham” would certainly be due to descent from the Isham, or a related, family.  But that would not have to apply to all Browders named Isham.  Children were often named after family friends.  Direct descent would apply only to the prominent planter and politician, and his descendants, unless others were also descended somehow from the Isham or a related family. 

As for the rest, the primary reason could well have been a means by which the Browder family could collectively recall some major event in their own family history without actually talking about it.  In fact, this could have been a concerted effort by family members on their own to help protect the identity of the child of Arthur Browder (c1715-c1739) and Mary Isham Randolph (c1716-Dec. 1772).  They may well have feared further violence or retribution.  They may well have believed, unfounded as that belief may have been, that the anger of the Randolph family would not subside, and that the child might still be in danger even into young adulthood.  But with so many Browder children suddenly possessing the given name Isham, it would have been difficult for anyone to focus on any particular Isham Browder.  However, once the child grew to adulthood the danger would have been essentially over and the naming of Browder children with the given name Isham would simply have become traditional.  Thus, it continued to be common for almost 100 years. 

            Of course, this is all speculation on my part because no one knows the answer.  But it is a question that researchers have attempted to solve, obviously with little to no success.  It is just plain unusual for a given name to crop up in a family line like this and be perpetuated so prolifically.  For it to be perpetuated on one line – the line of direct descent – is not unusual.  But, for it to be perpetuated on several related lines, with no known direct link to the origin of the name is very unusual.  But, I submit, that is exactly what happened because there is no good reason to posit that either John Browder (c1690-p. 1760) or his father, Edmund, married anyone from the Isham family, although some have tried to posit exactly this, with no evidence to support their contention.  I have to state that at least I have presented evidence to support my contention, even if it cannot be classed as absolute proof. 

            In any case, it is time to move on to a delineation of all of the known earliest persons named Isham Browder.  Among the other known early Isham Browders are the following: 

Isham Browder, Sr. (c1766, Dinwiddie County, Virginia-1852), who was the brother of George, Jr. (b. c1771) and Uriah “Urias” Browder, and who married Susanna (d. after 1852), and who also had a son named Isham (d. bef. 1852), is the first to be mentioned here.  Isham, Sr., George, and Uriah were the sons of George Browder, Sr. (son of John and Elizabeth Browder), who gave aid to the American Revolution on April 23, 1782 (Brunswick County Persons, p. ?).  This Isham Browder, Sr. resided in Dinwiddie County, Virginia all of his life and owned lands (252 acres) on Gravelly Run (at old Petersburg) close to the lands of Jones Browder, which were also located on Gravelly Run. 

            The land tax records of Dinwiddie County, Virginia in the early 1800’s show that Richard Browder, Isham Browder, Sr., Isham Browder, Jr., Jeremiah Browder, Jones Browder, and Littleberry Browder, as well as other family members, resided within just a few miles of one another. 

            The three brothers, Isham, Sr., George, and Uriah Browder, are also all mentioned in the parish register of the old Blanford Church, Bristol Parish, Church of England.  So, these brothers also continued to reside in the same area, and attend church in the very same building, that the Browder family had worshiped in during the previous generations.  However, the three brothers together converted to the Methodist faith once that denomination was organized by John Wesley.  They likely converted as early as 1776, because it is known that,

“the first important acquisitions to the Methodist [Societies] ranks came following a quarterly meeting in May, 1776, at Boisseau’s Chapel, in Dinwiddie County. . . .  [S]everal hundred were converted, and hardly anything was talked of but the wonderful work of God.  The counties of Dinwiddie, Amelia, Brunswick, Sussex, Prince George, Lunenburg and Mecklenburg, all shared in the revival” (The Old Free State, pp. 388-89/Memorials of Methodism, p. 88).

            The home and farm where these three brothers were brought up was located about 23 miles south of Richmond, Virginia.  This was close to the place called Browder Crossing (where Browder’s Bridge was located) and was also located about ten miles west of present day Petersburg, Virginia.

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder, Sr. (45 and over), along with his wife (45 and over), and slaves; four males and two females.  He and his family are listed directly above the family of Jones Browder, showing that they were close neighbors.

            The 1830 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder [Sr.] (60-69), along with his wife (50-59), and slaves; four males and eight females.

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County lists Isham Browder, Snr. (70-79), along with his wife (60-69).  Also listed within this household is one free colored male (24-35), as well as seven male slaves and twelve female slaves. 

            And the 1850 census of the Northern District of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder, Sr. (83), farmer, along with his wife, Susanna (75).  Interestingly, also residing within this household were Sam Jones (40), and Ben Jones (41).  All household members are listed as having been born in Virginia.  The value of Isham’s real estate that year was $1,740.

            He is most probably the Isham Browder who paid taxes in Dinwiddie County in 1800 (the 1800 Dinwiddie County, Virginia, Tax List).  He and Isham Jr. are both listed in the 1820 Dinwiddie County Tax List, with Isham Sr. having three slaves, and Isham Jr. having five slaves.

            In his will, Isham Browder, Sr. mentions his brothers, George and Urias, and his father, George (DCWB. 5, 1852, p. 176).  In the same county, by the way, other Browders also left wills including Susanna (1818), Caleb P. (1826), and Uriah “Urias” (1849). 

            Isham Browder, Sr., of Dinwiddie County left all of his property to the children of George Browder, Jr., upon his death in 1852 (Penland Browder, p. 40).  Obviously Isham, Jr. (as he was called), son of George, Jr. and Elizabeth, was named after this Isham, Sr.  Isham, Jr. also administered the estate of his uncle, Isham, Sr. 

It also becomes obvious that Isham, Jr., son of Isham, Sr., was deceased by 1852 (and had no descendants), and this is why Isham, Sr. left his property to the children of George Browder, Jr. 

            The will of Isham Browder, Sr. contains the following stipulations referring, in part, to the children of Isham Browder, Jr:

Legatees– Wife Susannah; Isham Jr. son of George; Nancy Stell (formerly Browder), daughter of George, deceased; Edna E. E. Browder, dau. of Isham Jr; Martha A. M. Browder, dau. of Isham Jr.; Uriah Browder “my only [living] brother”, (of Brunswick County, Virginia); Charles L. Browder, son of George, deceased; Mary P. Chandler, dau of George; balance of estate to George Browder’s children, Uriah’s children, Nancy Young’s children and Edna E. E. and Mary (Martha) A. M. Browder.  Dated December 8, 1852.

Executor: Isham Browder, Jr., son of George.

            George Browder (c1771-c1820) may be the George Browder who married Martha Hawks in July of 1813 in Amelia County, Virginia, although he is also known to have married Elizabeth.  George Browder, Jr. (b. c1771), brother of the afore mentioned Isham, Sr. and Uriah, along with his wife, Elizabeth (b. c1775), are known to have resided with the family of David Browder (son of Richard and Mary Browder) in Dinwiddie County from 1787 to 1794.

            George Browder patented 299 acres of land in Dinwiddie County, Virginia on the south side of Hatcher’s Run on December 15, 1758.  John Roberts purchased a neighboring tract of land to this George Browder in 1769.  He also acquired 105 acres of land, some of which was in Brunswick County, probably through inheritance, which he paid taxes on even while residing with the David Browder family (Penland Browder, p. 40).

            The children of George, Jr. and Elizabeth Browder were Isham, Jr. (b. c1799), Charles L. (b. c1801), Nancy (m. Robert? Stell), Mary P. (Chandler), and Elizabeth (m. George Landifer).  George Browder died about 1820. 

            The 1820 census of St. Andrew’s Parish, Brunswick County, Virginia lists Mrs. Elisa Browder (45 and over), along with three females [presumably, her daughters] (16-25), as well as eight male slaves and eight female slaves.  On the same page is listed Uriah Browder. 

            Isham Browder, Jr., son of George, Jr. and Elizabeth Browder, had at least two daughters, Edna E. E. and Martha A. M.  Isham, Jr. inherited 62 acres of land in Dinwiddie County on August 26, 1821, after the death of his father, George, Jr. 

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder Junior (16-25), as well as another male (16-25), and two females (10-15).  Who all of these persons were, is unknown but, presumably, one of the females may have been Isham’s wife.  Also listed are four male slaves and one female slave. 

            The 1830 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder [Jr.] (30-39), along with two females (30-39), and two females (under 5).  Also listed are five male slaves and ten female slaves. 

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder, Jr. (40-49), along with another male (25-30), and two females (10-15).  Obviously, his wife had died by this time. 

            Interestingly, it seems that another Isham Browder (40-49) is also listed in the 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, along with two females (10-14), and five male slaves, as well as one female slave. 

            The 1850 census of the Northern District of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Isham Browder, Jr. (51), brick layer, as a single head of household.  That year the value of his real estate was $1,200.  This census shows that Isham was born in Virginia.

            In 1830 this family is listed directly above Charles L. Browder (30-39) and his presumed wife (20-29).  Also listed on the same page is Robert Stell (20-29), along with his wife (15-19), and one other female (50-59).

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia also, again, lists Chas. L. Browder (30-39), along with his presumed wife (20-29), and presumed children; one male (under 5), two females (5-9), and one female (under 5).  Also listed are two male slaves and one female slave. 

            The 1850 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia (Northern District) once again lists Charles L. Browder (49), along with his wife, Sally A. (39), and children; Roberta F. (15), Henrietta S. (13), John A. (2), Elvira (9), and Laura A. R. (7), as well as a boarder, Joseph Lock (12).  This census confirms that Charles was born in Virginia.

            Uriah “Urias” Browder, Sr. brother of George and Isham, Sr., married Mary Quarles (daughter of John Quarles?) on May 9, 1793 in Brunswick County, Virginia.  They resided in Brunswick County, Virginia from 1795 to 1848/49.

            The 1810 census of St. Andrews Parish, Brunswick County, Virginia lists Urias Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (26-44), and presumed children; one male (10-15), four males (under 10), and one female (under 10).  It also lists eleven slaves.

            The 1820 census of St. Andrews Parish, Brunswick County, Virginia lists Uriah Browder (45 and over), along with his presumed wife (45 and over), and presumed children; two males (16-25), one male (16-18), two males (10-15), one female (16-25), and two females (under 10).  Also listed are three male slaves and five female slaves.

            The 1830 census of St. Andrews Parish, Brunswick County, Virginia also lists Urias Browder (60-69), and presumed children; one male (20-29), one male (under 5), one female (30-39), two females (10-14), one female (5-9), and two females (under 5).  Also listed are five male slaves and eight female slaves.

            The 1840 census of Brunswick County, Virginia lists Urias Browder (60-69), along with two females (20-29), and one male (10-14).  Also listed are five male slaves and seven female slaves.

            The will of Uriah Browder is dated January 10, 1849, and is recorded in Brunswick County, Virginia records (BCWB, Vol. 15, 1849, p. 232). 

            The children of Uriah Browder and Mary Quarles were John (b. c1801), Catherine (m. Hartwell Ogburn, January 25, 1825), Isham (b. c1805), Uriah, Jr. (b. c1812), Ann R. (Nolley), Susanna, Mary Jane (m. Lewis Barrow, June 24, 1839), and George E. (b. c1823/m. Judy R. Hawthorne) (Penland-Browder, p. 71). 

            The 1810 census of St. Andrews Parish, Brunswick County, Virginia lists John Browder [son of Uriah Browder and Mary Quarles] (16-25) as a single head of household residing alone.

            John Browder, son of Uriah Browder and Mary Quarles, is probably the one who married Angelia F. Dameron on March 8, 1834 (Penland Browder, p. 71).

            The 1840 census of Brunswick County, Virginia lists John Browder (30-39), along with his wife (30-39), and one daughter (under 5).  Also listed are four male slaves and three female slaves.

            It is of interest to note that on June 28, 1762 John Quarles, Nathaniel Tatum, Jr. and Hugh Williams witnessed a deed between Robert Sayer and his wife, Mary on the one part and James Howeton of Culpepper County, Virginia of the other part (BCDB 7, p. 134).  

            Uriah Browder, Jr. married Susan “Sally” Elmore on April 10, 1817 in Brunswick County, Virginia. 

            The 1840 census of Lunenburg County, Virginia lists Urias Browder [Jr.] (30-39), along with his wife (20-29), and two female slaves.

            The 1850 census of the Northern Division of Brunswick County, Virginia lists Uriah Browder [Jr.] (38), along with his wife, Susan (30), and children; Mary (8), Sarah (7), John (6), William (5), and Harriet (2).  This census shows that Uriah was born in Virginia.

            Isham Browder (b. c1805), son of Uriah Browder and Mary Quarles, married Martha W. Burnett on December 8, 1836 in Lunenburg County, Virginia. 

            The 1840 census of Brunswick County, Virginia lists Isham Browder (30-39), along with his wife (20-29,) and children, one male (10-14), one male (under 5), and one female (under 5).  Also listed is one female slave.  This family is listed directly under the family of John Browder. 

            The 1850 census of the Northern Division of Brunswick County, Virginia lists Isham Browder (45), planter, along with his wife, Martha (35), and children, Allen (12), Lucy (11), Philip (10), Lois (9), George (8), Martha (7), Thomas (6), Miles (5), Marcus (4), and Marietta (3).  The value of Isham’s real estate that year was $94,500.  All family members are listed as having been born in Virginia.

            On April 15, 1851 (BCDB 35, pp 390-91), Freeman J. Gunn sold Thomas Lambert 200 acres of land, known as the Jordan Tract, which was partly in Lunenburg County and partly in Brunswick County.  This land is said to have been bounded by the lands of Robert B. Jones, Samuel Mattox, and Isham Browder.  This is also within the same area of residence as previous Browder generations. 

            Another Isham Browder married Frances Daniel on December 2, 1782 in Pittsylvania County, Virginia.  I actually believe that this Isham Browder is one and the same as the Isham Browder (June 12, 1762-Feb. 19, 1830) of the Revolutionary War, and that he first married this Frances Daniel, but that there were no living children of this union, and Frances soon died.

            William Browder, son of Richard and Mary Browder, married and had children; Jeremiah (b. c1774), Isham (b. c1775), Thomas (b. c1776), William (b. c1777), and James (b. c1780).  This is probably the William Browder whose wife was named Elizabeth.  This Elizabeth relinquished her dower rights in a certain tract of land about 1790 (Penland Browder, p. 189/DCOB 1, p. 27).

            The brothers, Jeremiah, Isham, and Thomas, all resided in Mecklenburg and Lunenburg Counties, Virginia prior to moving to Iredell County, North Carolina.

            This is the sum total of what is known about the known Isham Browders of this period, with the exception of those who were descended from either Thomas Isham Browder or the Isham Browder of the revolutionary War. 

            But, in addition to these Isham Browders, there were other, unaccounted for, members of the Browder family, as well as an Arthur Browder (b. c1771) who married Charlotte (b. 1771) about 1790.  He is known to have relocated from Bladen County, North Carolina to Williamsburg District, South Carolina. 

            The 1800 census of Williamsburg, Marion District, South Carolina lists this Arthur Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (26-44), and presumed children, two males (10-15), one male (under 5), one female (10-15), and one female (under 10).  No slaves are listed.  Interestingly, on the same page is listed the Jacob Coleman family. 

            The 1810 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina again lists Arthur Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (26-44), and presumed children, one male (16-25), one male (10-15), three males (under 10), one female (10-15), and two females (under 10).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1820 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina also lists Arthur Browder (45 and over), as well as his presumed wife (26-44), and presumed children; two males (16-18), one male (10-15), one male (under 10), two females (10-15), and one female (under 10).  No slaves are listed.  Interestingly, the families of H. Henry Bostwick and Jonathan Bostwick are also listed. 

            The 1830 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina lists Charlotte Browder (40-49), along with presumed children; one male (20-29), one male (10-14), one female (20-29), and one female (10-14).  No slaves are listed.  So, it is obvious that this Arthur Browder was deceased by this time. 

            Also listed in the same 1830 census is the family of John Browder (50-59), along with one male (20-29), one male (5-9), two males (under 5), one female (20-29), and one female (5-9).  Presumably, he is one and the same as John Browder who was earlier listed in Chatham County, North Carolina. 

            The 1790 census of Chatham County, North Carolina lists John Browder (16 and over), along with five females (no age range specified), as well as three slaves, all within the same household.  He is probably the John Browder who was born about 1755 in North Carolina, and who married Frances Richardson of Tennessee.  He does show up in the Bladen County, North Carolina tax list of 1763. 

            Also, on the same page is listed Derias “Browdey” (16 and over), along with two males (under 16), and two females.  No slaves are listed for this family. This was, obviously, Darius Browder. Darius Browder enlisted in Company F. of the 62nd Infantry Regiment of Tennessee, CSA as a Second Lieutenant during the Civil War. 

            A John Browder is later named in the 1805 Roane County, Tennessee tax list, but it is unclear to me which John Browder this may be.

            Also, a Thomas Browder married Jane B. Montague on July 17, 1811 in Powhatan County, Virginia.

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Thomas Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (16-25), and presumed children; two males (under 10), and three females (under 10).  Also listed are two free colored persons (m. under 14), as well as five male slaves and six female slaves. 

            The 1830 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina also lists Thomas Browder (50-59), along with his presumed wife (30-39), as well as one male (20-29), one male (5-9), three females (5-9), and one female (under 5).  He is presumably one and the same as Thomas Browder from Bladen County, North Carolina. 

            The 1840 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina again lists Thomas Browder (60-69), along with his presumed wife (40-49), and presumed children; one male (5-9), one female (15-19), and one female (5-9).  No slaves are listed. 

            The 1830 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina also lists Joshua Browder (15-19), along with his presumed wife (20-29), and one male (under 5), one female (5-9), and one female (under 5).  This would have to be a son of Arthur and Charlotte Browder. 

            The 1840 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina again lists Joshua Browder (40-49), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; one male (10-14), two males (5-9), one male (under 5), and two females (10-14).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1850 census of Williamsburg district, South Carolina lists Charlott Browder (79), as residing within the household of Moses Brayboy (45), farmer.  This household also included Moses’ wife, Jane (35), and children, Martha (8), Maria (2), and H. V. (m. 2 months).  One would have to assume that Jane was Charlotte’s daughter, although this is not specified.

            Also listed on the same page in this census is the family of Sarah C. Browder (38), which included her children, Mary E. (16), H. E. (f. 15), L. J. (f. 10), B. R. (m. 3), and P. P. (m. 1). 

            Also, on the same page, is listed Joshua Browder (45 or 48), farmer, along with his presumed wife, Ann (35), and Mary A. (30).  Others, presumably children, listed were James W. (17), G. W. (15), Edwin (13), and Amarintha C. (1).  Also listed within this household are Gadson (23), Emma (18), and Laura S. (1).

            Joshua Browder (b. Williamsburg District, South Carolina), son of Arthur and Charlotte Browder, married Nancy Brayboy (born in 1802) in Williamsburg District, South Carolina in 1820.  Nancy is believed to have been a Native American.

            Other known children of Arthur and Charlotte Browder were Martin (b. 1807), and Isaac J. (b. 1816) and, presumably, Jane.  All children were born in Williamsburg District, South Carolina.

            The 1840 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina lists Martin Browder (30-39), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; two males (5-9), one male (under 5), one female (5-9), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina also lists Isaac Browder (20-29), along with his presumed wife (20-29), and presumed children; one male (under 5), two females (5-9), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            Presumably, another unknown South Carolina Browder had the following children; Arthur (b. 1784), Jeremiah (b. 1789), Charlotte (b. 1771), unknown, and Bessie (b. after 1775).  However, it seems obvious that this family likely also originated in Dinwiddie County, Virginia because a Jeremiah Browder is listed in the 1810 Tax List for that county as owning three slaves. 

            He is also listed in the 1810 census there.  He resided close to Jones, Littleberry, and the two Isham Browders, as well as others. 

            Jeremiah Browder, along with others, is again listed in the 1830 tax list.  Whether this is the same Jeremiah Browder as the one who resided in South Carolina is unclear.  But Jeremiah Browder, like a few others, does seem to have come from nowhere. 

            Another Jeremiah Browder married Susan Clements on January 28, 1790 in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  I believe that this is one and the same as the following Jeremiah Browder.

            The 1810 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Jereh Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (26-44).  No slaves are listed.  It seems logical to assume that this Jereh Browder is a son of Jeremiah Browder, but this is not a certainty.  Interestingly, on the same page is listed the family of Littleberry Browder, John Bolling, and Lucy Booth.

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Jeremiah Browder (45 and over), along with his presumed children; one male (26-44), one male (under 10), one female (26-44), one female (10-15), and one female (under 10).  No slaves are listed. 

            The 1830 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Jeremiah Browder (50-59), along with one male (under 5) and one female (20-29).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Jeremiah Browder (60-69) along with two males (5-9), one female (30-39), and one female (under 5).  Neither Jeremiah nor the older female could read or write.  No slaves are listed. 

            Still other early members of the Browder family who remain unaccounted for include, but are not limited to, the following:

            John Browder (45 and over) is listed in the 1810 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, along with his wife (45 and over), and one male (under 10).  Eight slaves are listed for this family that year.

            The 1820 census of Sussex County, Virginia lists Joshua Browder (45 and over), along with his presumed wife (26-44), and presumed children; two males (under 10), two females (16-25), two females (10-15), and two females (under 10).  Also listed is one male slave and five female slaves. 

Most likely this is the Joshua Browder who was the son of a John Browder (d. c1814), who resided in Dinwiddie County, Virginia, and who also had another son named John, according to the 1788 tax records.  The John Browder in the above 1810 census is one and the same as the one who died about 1814 (Penland Browder, p. 54 & 56).  His son, John, is probably the John Browder who married Elizabeth Dyson on September 29, 1796 in Chesterfield County, Virginia.  John, Jr. died about 1817/18 (Penland Browder, p. 57).  So, he is not listed in the 1820 census.  His second son, John, Jr. was born about 1806 (ibid, p. 58). 

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Williamson Browder (26-44), along with his presumed wife (16-25), and one female (under 10).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia (Petersburg, West Ward) lists Wmson Browder (40-49), along with his presumed wife (40-49), and presumed children; one male (20-29), two females (15-19), and two females (10-14).  No slaves are listed.

            Williamson Browder is also found in the property tax records of Brunswick County, Virginia in 1814 and the Dinwiddie County tax records from 1815-1825 (Penland Browder, p. 57). 

            The 1820 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Dianna Browder [widow of John Browder, Sr.] (45 and over), along with her presumed children; one male (16-25), and one male (10-15).  Also listed is one male slave and two female slaves. 

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, North Carolina (Petersburg West Ward) lists John Browder (30-39), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; one male (10-14), two males (5-9), one male (under 5), and one female (under 5).  One female slave is also listed.

            The 1840 census of Caswell County, North Carolina lists Jno. Browder (40-49), along with his presumed wife (40-49), and presumed children; two males (15-19), one male (5-9), one female (20-29), one female (15-19), two females (10-14), one female (5-9), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Williamsburg District, South Carolina lists John Browder (50-59), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; three males (10-14), one male (5-9), one male (under 5), one female (15-19), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1850 census of McMinn County, Tennessee (Subdivision 23) lists, possibly, the same John Browder (60), along with his wife, Susan (55), and family members; Sarah (22), Mary (19), Craton (16), Artissa (14), Dorcas (12), Robt. (10), William (8), Stephen (6), and Nancy (4).  This census shows that John was born in North Carolina. 

            The 1850 census of McMinn County, Tennessee (Subdivision 23) also lists John’s likely brother, Edmond Browder (66), along with his wife, Lucinda (64).  This census states that Edmond was also born in North Carolina.  Both John and Edmund are likely sons of Edmund Browder of Chatham County, North Carolina. 

            The 1840 census of Sumter, South Carolina lists Jno. J. Browder (30-39), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; one male (10-14), two males (under 5), and two females (10-14).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Thos. Browder (60-69), along with his presumed wife (40-49), as well as one male (15-19), one female (10-14), and two females (5-9).  Also listed are two free colored males as well as one male slave and three female slaves.

            The 1850 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia (Northern District) lists Thomas Browder (72), along with his presumed wife, Amy G. (50), as well as household members; Adelina (17), and Martha E. (13).  This census states that Thomas was born in Virginia. 

            The 1840 census of Kanawha County, Virginia lists Thos. Browder (20-29), along with one female (70-79), one female (40-49), two females (15-19), two females (10-14), and two females (5-9).  No slaves are listed.

            There was also a Thomas Browder who is found in the land records of Bladen County, North Carolina in 1770 (Penland Browder, p. xi).  I have been unable to place him properly within any family grouping.  However, since he is listed in 1770, he would have had to have been born as early as 1749.  Since there is no indication that John and Elizabeth Browder had any children after Joseph (although this is not impossible), I believe he is most likely to have been an unknown child of one of their children (most likely Richard Browder), since he is obviously an early Browder.

            The 1840 census of Dinwiddie County, Virginia lists Jeremiah Browder (60-69), along with two males (5-9), one female (30-39), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Halifax County, Virginia (South District) lists Jessee Browder (40-49), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and presumed children; two males (5-9), one female (10-14), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1850 census of Halifax County, Virginia (South District) lists Jessee Browder (55), Planter, along with his wife, Susan (48), and children; Nancy (19), John N. (18), David A. (16), Susan A. (11), and Martha J. (9).  All family members are listed as having been born in Virginia.

            The 1860 census of Stokes County, North Carolina lists Jesse Browder (63), rock and brick mason, along with his wife, Susan (56), and children; Susan Ann (22), and Nancy (28).  All family members are listed as having been born in Virginia.

            The 1840 census of Halifax County, Virginia (South District) lists Ann Browder (30-39), along with her presumed children; one male (10-14), three females (5-9), and one female (under 5).  No slaves are listed.

            The 1840 census of Sussex County, Virginia lists Jos. D. Browder (20-29), along with his presumed wife (30-39), and slaves; three males and two females.

            The 1850 census of Sussex County, Virginia lists Joshua D. Browder (31), along with his wife, Martha C. (28), and children; Junius C. (7), William T. (6), and Sarah E. (2).  This census shows that Joshua was born in Virginia.

            The 1840 census of Sussex county, Virginia lists Elizabeth Browder [also listed in the 1820 census/widow of John Browder, Jr.] (60-69), along with one male (20-29), and one male (5-9).  Also listed are five male slaves and four female slaves.

            Finally, one thing is also virtually certain; our Arthur Browder (c1715-c1739) was also the very first Browder to be given the name Arthur, as far as is known.  The ancestry of these others named Arthur Browder is unknown, as would be their potential relationship with this Arthur Browder.

            Still, to end the story of Jones Browder, I must reiterate the following, which will help to decisively tie up the previously detailed family connections.  The Dinwiddie County, Virginia Order Book of the Circuit Superior Court (FHL Film#7406) details the settlement of Jones Browder’s estate on May 3, 1836.  It was finally settled on that date by John T. Sydnor, Executor of David Thacker, Administrator of Jones Browder’s estate. 

            On that date, his estate was ordered divided among his first cousins on the paternal side of his family (interesting that no one on his maternal side sued for any of it and also received nothing), and it was divided among them on September 21, 1836.  The details are not necessary to delineate here since I will do so elsewhere.  That which, from his record, does add to our knowledge of this family at the time in question is a fact mentioned in the order, which could easily be overlooked by researchers who may not realize its significance. 

It is noted that David Thacker had also been administrator of the estates of Isham Browder, Augustine Browder, Nancy Fears (formerly Browder), Tabitha Clarke (formerly Browder), Martha Whitmore (formerly Browder), all children of Thomas Isham Browder and Mary Cox, as well as Mary Meador [I corrected this] (formerly Browder) and Richard Browder, heirs of Martha Browder (who married Isham C. Browder), deceased. 

            Wow!!!  After all of the speculation by others, and also by me, over the years past, the very first thing that MUST be acknowledged here, based on the above, is that this one notation proves, with absolutely NO doubt whatsoever, that Martha Browder, who married my Isham C. Browder, WAS a daughter of Richard and Mary Browder!  It names her two certainly still living children, Richard and Mary.  This connection is NO LONGER subject to speculation by anyone!  My Isham C. Browder DID marry his second cousin, Martha Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder. 

            And the very fact that David Thacker administered not only the estate of Jones Browder, but also the estates of Isham Browder (obviously Thomas Isham Browder, since the estate of Isham C. Browder was administered by Clement Whittmore) and his son, Augustine [C.] Browder, as well as all of Augustine’s sisters (except for Mary, who was deceased and had no heirs, for they had also passed away by this date), shows a link between Arthur Browder and Thomas Isham Browder that is shown in no other extant record that I am aware of.  The very fact that they are linked at all, in any way, is significant!  If their relationship had not been proven before, I submit that it has been with this document! (thanks to Judy Bean for posting this document on-line.  I did not find this document until December of 2012!). 

Select Bibliography:

Bell, Landon C. The Old Free State: A Contribution to the History of Lunenburg County and Southside Virginia. in Two Volumes. Volume I. Clearfield, 2011.

Browder, Blanche Penland. Notes On the Browder Family of Tidewater Virginia, 1695-1850, Second Edition, Copyright: Blanche Penland Browder, printed in USA by Blanche Penland Browder.

Posted byjmarkbrowderSeptember 4, 2019October 1, 2019Posted inUncategorizedLeave a comment on The Earliest Isham Browders and Other Related Persons:

Proofs That Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), Who Married Isham C. Browder (1773-1832), was a Daughter of Richard Browder (c1719-c1807) of Dinwiddie County, Virginia and That Isham Browder (c1774-1832) Was Her Cousin:


By: John Mark Browder, BA, MS, MTS, MDiv (updated Jan. 1, 2025)

Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, married her cousin, and my ancestor, Isham C. Browder (1773-1832). This fact is first stated in the addendum to the book, “Genealogy of David Browder, of Virginia and His Descendants”, in which David P. Browder refers to research he had recently obtained through his nephew, the Rev. Robert W. Browder, which Robert Browder, in turn, had obtained from his father, the Rev. George R. Browder, who had, in turn, obtained the information from his father, Robert Browder (b. 1804), son of David Browder and Mary Cousins.  In the addendum to this book by Thomas E. Browder, David states this fact in the following manner:

“One BROWDER [John], an Englishman, married a WELSH WOMAN [Elizabeth], and emigrated to America [as we know, he was actually born here] early in the 18th century, and settled in Virginia, where he lived and died. His name has not been retained in the family. The children of these progenitors of the Browder family were Richard, Joseph, ARTHUR, George and Nancy as far as known. . . . MARTHA BROWDER, daughter of RICHARD and MARY THOMPSON BROWDER, married a cousin ISHAM BROWDER. Their children were Mary and RICHARD so far as is known” (Browder, Thomas, pp. 13-14).

This, as far as I know, is the very first written mention in a book of the marriage of the cousins Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder.  But I still wound up having to prove it, as many wanted to dismiss it.

First, in 1808 Isham Browder and Martha Browder, his wife, gave Power of Attorney to Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia to sell Martha’s interest in about 200 acres of land, located on the Gravelly Run (Rockingham County, North Carolina Deed Book N, p. 339).  While all of this is not directly specified, Richard Browder, Martha’s father, was not a large land owner and probably only owned about 200 acres at the time of his death. So, it is certain that he did not bequeath it all to Martha, but bequeathed each of his living children a certain percentage of divided interest in said land. But, sadly, we don’t have a Will to consult.

It is likely that the Richard Browder mentioned in the Power of Attorney was the son of her brother, David Browder and his wife, Mary Cousins, since Martha’s father had already died about 1807, and that the land in question must have been inherited from her father, Richard Browder, who resided in Dinwiddie County all of his life. It should be understood that there is no reason for Martha to have been named in such a document other than the fact that she had inherited a percentage in said land, and that she was actually the one giving Power of Attorney to sell land that she had inherited. Otherwise, it would have only been necessary for Isham to have been named and she would not need to sign off on it. 

So, this document shows a direct relationship between Richard Browder and his daughter, Martha, and shows that she married her cousin, Isham C. Browder. It clearly shows a connection between Martha Browder and Dinwiddie County, Virginia. The fact that Power of Attorney was given to a member of the Browder family also shows conclusively that Martha was a member of the Browder family. Otherwise, why would she not have elected a member of some other family? This is the only known document that conclusively names Martha Browder directly as the wife of Isham C. Browder, although the division of Jones Browder’s property also indicates it directly. The POA follows from Rockingham County, North Carolina Deed Book N, p. 339 (from a copy by genealogist, Maude Reynolds):

“Browder, Richard from BROWDER, ISHAM & WIFE, Power of Attorney, 200 A of Land: Know all men by these presents that we ISHAM BROWDER and MARTHA BROWDER his WIFE, both of the county of Rockingham and state of N. C., have for good cause to us moving, have nominated, authorized, ordained and appointed, and do by these presents authorize, ordain and appoint our friend Richard Browder of the county of Dinwiddia (sic) and commonwealth of Va., to act, make over convey and confirm a certain piece or parcel of land situate, lying and being in the said county of Dinwiddia (sic) and commonwealth aforesaid and the waters of the Gravelly Run, and contains about 200 acres of land:  And we, the said ISHAM BROWDER and MARTHA BROWDER for ourselves and heirs, creditors and administrators do make or convey and confirm unto the said Richard Browder full power absolute authority to sell and dispose, make over, convey and confirm at the will and pleasure, a sure and good right and title to the aforesaid land and premises as above described, to whomsoever he may think proper, as full and ample in every respect, as we could do were we personally present, to all which matter and things – we the said ISHAM BROWDER and the said MARTHA BROWDER for ourselves our heirs creditors and Administrators firmly by these presents, sealed with our seals and dated this day of ___ in the year 1808.

Signed, sealed and acknowledged in the presence of ISHAM BROWDER (seal) Augustine C. Browder MARTHA BROWDER (seal) Thos. Winston Joseph Mclain St. of N. C. Rockingham Co. May Session 1810: The written power of attorney was duly proved in open court by the oath of Thomas Winston & on motion ordered to be recorded.          R. Galloway, C.”

Notice that, although he was a witness, Augustine C. Browder did not directly inherit any of said land, nor similar property, and did not make a Power of Attorney to convey anything himself. This might have been the case if Isham C. Browder were the inheritor of said property, because his brother, as well as other siblings, would have also inherited similar property from Richard Browder in that case. This shows that Isham C. Browder was not descended from Richard Browder, but that Martha Browder was. She is obviously the one who inherited this property, located in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.

It is interesting to note that, in this document it is stated that the couple believed that they had good reason to move. Obviously, the inference is that they moved from Dinwiddie County, Virginia, or close to that location, and relocated back to Rockingham County, North Carolina. What prompted this move? The answer to this question is unknown, but they probably always intended to. Whatever their reasoning was, they considered it good enough not only to move, but also to elect another trusted family member to deal with Martha’s inheritance so that they did not have to return to do so. What eventually happened to said property is still an open question, since I have located no further documentation concerning it. But, presumably, it was sold, so that Isham and Martha Browder received their part of the proceeds from said sale.

Martha Browder (c1768-c1811) is also mentioned multiple times in the Dinwiddie County, Virginia Order Book of the Circuit Superior Court, which details the settlement of Jones Browder’s estate, although she was already deceased by the time the case went to court and, thus, her children are also named in her stead. Martha is mentioned because she was a daughter of Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, and Jones Browder (c1764-c1826) was her nephew. This becomes absolutely clear from the text. So, if her children were first cousins to Jones Browder (and they were), then the father of Jones Browder had to have been her brother. There is no way around this. And it has been established by documentation that Jones Browder had three brothers, Harrison, Frederick, and Samuel, and that all of them were sons of Arthur Browder (c1742-c1786). That makes Arthur Browder (c1742-c1786) Martha’s brother.

THIS Arthur Browder, clearly a son of Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, is NOT the one who may have married Mary Isham Randolph (c1716-Dec. 1772). He is, instead, believed to have married a daughter of Gabriel and Grace Harrison, although this has not been proven. He did marry Rebecca Jones, whom I think was a second wife. 

The case was finally settled on April 4, 1837 by John T. Sydnor, Executor of David Thacker, who had been Administrator of Jones Browder’s estate. On that date, Jones’ estate was ordered divided among his FIRST COUSINS on the PATERNAL side of his family. It was divided among some of them on September 21, 1836, and also on April 4, 1837. Martha Browder and her heirs (children), Richard and Mary, are named in these documents. Martha Browder, who married Isham C. Browder (son of Isham Browder and Talitha Cox), therefore, again, WAS a daughter of Richard and Mary Browder!

For the children of Martha Browder (daughter of Richard and Mary of Dinwiddie County, Virginia) to have been first cousins on the paternal side of Jones Browder’s family, Martha had to be a sister to Arthur Browder, father of Jones Browder, period. But, they didn’t have to be in order to be named because Martha was a first cousin on the paternal side of the family in question herself. So, SHE is the one actually named as an inheritor. But, since she was already deceased when everything was decided, her children inherited in her stead. This is how it worked in all cases here. 

Again, it is also known, through family tradition, that Isham C. Browder married his cousin, Martha, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia. For this to be possible, Isham C. Browder had to also have been descended from the same Browder line as Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia. We know that his father was Isham Browder, who married Talitha Cox. There is so much documentation for this that there can be absolutely no question of it.

So, it becomes obvious that Isham C. Browder had to have been descended from one of Richard Browder’s brothers through his father, Isham Browder. This Isham Browder (c1732-1784) WAS the son, and unnamed heir, of Arthur Browder (1715-c1738), whether one accepts that this Arthur married Mary Isham Randolph (c1716-Dec. 1772), or not. This becomes obvious because of the preponderance of the evidence.

ALL of the legatees who took part were descendants of John and Elizabeth Browder, and they were called “first cousins” in the court documents. There is no way of getting around this fact. So, obviously, Richard and Mary Browder named one son (most likely their first) Arthur, probably after Richard’s brother, who was already deceased by the time this son was born.

From the document dated April 2, 1834 (Dinwiddie County, Virginia Chancery Order Book 1, 1832-1852, pp. 46-47), the following is copied verbatim (but structurally modified for readability, also adding information in brackets as a help to identify persons named):

“Mary Pentecost, formerly [Mary Thompson] Browder [daughter of Richard and Mary Browder],

John W. P. Pool [Pettypool] and Mary [actually, Nancy A. Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder] his wife, formerly Williams,

Nancy Fraser [daughter of Susanna Browder and John Thacker], formerly Thacker, Joseph Lewis and Elizabeth [daughter of Susanna Browder and George Timmons] his wife, formerly Timmons, George Timmons admr. of Susanna Timmons decd [daughter of Richard and Mary Browder]. and Martha Timmons, heirs of Susanna Timmons decd., formerly Browder.

Elizabeth Pulliam, Tabitha Browder, Jane Browder, Benjamin Browder, Venus [actually Vines] Browder, Jesse Browder, and Jonathan Browder, heirs of Thompson Browder decd [son of Richard and Mary Browder]

John P. Pool, Stephen P. Pool, David P. Pool, Pettipool Massie and Elizabeth his wife, formerly Pool, Peterson P. Pool and Elizabeth P. Pool, heirs of Henry P. Pool decd [who married Anne Browder, daughter of John Browder and Elizabeth Jones. These were children of John William Pettypool and Nancy A. Browder, she being a daughter of Richard and Mary Browder].

Obedience Wilkinson, formerly Browder,

John Browder [son of Lt. Joseph Browder and Susanna Kelly],

Lucy Browder, Nancy Browder,

Susan Payne, Joseph Browder, and Elizabeth Browder [children of Lt. Joseph Browder and Susanna Kelly]

Plts against John T. Sydnor exor of David Thacker [son of Susanna Browder and John Thacker] decd who was admr of Jones Browder decd

ISHAM [C.] BROWDER, Augustine Browder, Nancy Fear[s], formerly Browder, Talitha Clarke, formerly Browder and Martha Whitmore [Whittemore], formerly Browder, heirs of ISHAM BROWDER decd [Capt. Isham Browder who married Talitha Cox]

Mary Meadows (sic) [actually, Meador], formerly Browder and RICHARD BROWDER, heirs of MARTHA BROWDER, decd [daughter of Richard and Mary Browder, who married Isham C. Browder].

Mary A. Low, formerly Browder, [relationship uncertain]

Littleberry Browder [son of Richard and Mary Browder],

Anthony Sydnor and Mary C. [daughter of David Browder and Mary Pegram] his wife, formerly Browder, Richard Browder [son of David Browder and Mary Cousins], Robert Browder [son of David Browder and Mary Cousins], William Browder [son of David Browder and Mary Cousins], John Moore and Nancy [daughter of David Browder and Mary Cousins] his wife, formerly Browder, Caleb Bell and Jane [daughter of David Browder and Mary Cousins] his wife, formerly Browder, Robert Hutchings and Almedia [daughter of David Browder and Mary Cousins] his wife, formerly Browder, heirs of David Browder decd,

John P. Pool, Coleman Gordon and Nancy his wife, formerly Pool heirs of Perkinson P. Pool [son of Nancy A. Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder],

David Hampton and Elizabeth his wife, formerly Browder, Sarah Barnes formerly Browder, Anica Kennedy formerly Browder & Rhoda Browder [children of Lt. Joseph Browder, Sr. and Susanna “Sukey” Kelly] Defts.

This day this cause came on to be . . . heard on the bill answer of John T. Sydnor exor of David Thacker decd and exhibits, the other defendants who are out of the county and against whom the plaintiffs appear to have proceeded in the mode proscribed by law against absent defendants still failing to appear and answer, on the motion of the plaintiffs by counsel the Court doth take their bill for confession as to those defendants and was agreed by council on consideration whereof the court doth order that the defendant John T. Sydnor exor of David Thacker decd do render an amount of his Testators administrations of the estate of Jones Browder decd before a commissioner . . . is directed to . . . and settle the same and make report thereof to the Court with any matters specially stated deemed pertinent by himself, or which may be required by the parties to be so stated, and that the commissioner advertize once a week for two months in some newspaper printed in the city of Richmond and in the Cty of Washington for all persons who may claim to be distributes of Jones Browder decd in the maternal line to appear before him and prove their relationship to the decedent on or before the first day of September next or they will be excluded from all share of the estate.

Just this document alone proves (1) that Isham Browder, who married Talitha Cox, was considered a FIRST COUSIN ON THE PATERNAL SIDE and (2) that Martha Browder, daughter of Richard and Mary Browder was also considered a FIRST COUSIN ON THE PATERNAL SIDE. The document names Isham Browder (c1732-1784) directly, along with his descendants “ISHAM [C.] BROWDER, Augustine [C.] Browder, Nancy Fear[s], formerly Browder, Talitha Clarke, formerly Browder and Martha Whitmore, formerly Browder”. Then, separately, it also names Martha Browder directly, “Mary Meadows (sic) [actually, Meador], formerly Browder and RICHARD BROWDER, heirs of MARTHA BROWDER decd”.

To name both separately conclusively proves that they were both cousins on the paternal side of the family, which means that they were both descended from John and Elizabeth Browder, just like all the other plaintiffs were. This proves that Martha was indeed a daughter of Richard and Mary Browder and also conclusively proves that Isham Browder (c1732-1784) was the son of one of the brothers of Richard. The ONLY one of Richard’s brothers who died early enough for Richard and Mary to have taken in his son and brought him up (another family tradition), was ARTHUR. Thus, Isham Browder (c1732-1784) WAS the son of Arthur Browder (1715-c1738) mentioned in the Bristol Parish Vestry Book as his heir, period. Case closed.

Posted byjmarkbrowderSeptember 3, 2019October 4, 2025Posted inUncategorized14 Comments on Proofs That Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), Who Married Isham C. Browder (1773-1832), was a Daughter of Richard Browder (c1719-c1807) of Dinwiddie County, Virginia and That Isham Browder (c1774-1832) Was Her Cousin:

Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder of Rockingham County, North Carolina


By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (corrected and updated Dec. 25-27, 2024)

Isham “Coleman” Browder was born in1773 in Guilford County, North Carolina, and died sometime early in 1832 in Rockingham County, North Carolina, aged about 57 years, almost fifty years after his own father’s death.  He never moved, but Rockingham County was made from the northern part of Guilford County where Isham resided for most of his life.  

I refer to him with the second name of “Coleman” because, as will be shown, his middle initial was “C.”, and it is the likeliest name to begin with that letter that his middle initial could signify, since it is a family name, and “Claiborne” was his brother’s middle name and “Cox” was the middle name of one of his sisters.  Also, he had several relatives from the Cox family with the second name of Coleman. In addition, he had a grandson, Isham C. Browder, who was obviously named after him and who had a son, Robert Coleman Browder.  I cannot prove that “Coleman” is correct, but it is most likely since Talitha’s mother had been Mary Coleman (c1702-c1794). And there is no other connection to the Coleman family. Thus, I will refer to him in said manner. 

Isham Coleman Browder (1773-1832) is known to have married his cousin, Martha Browder (c1768-c1811), daughter of Richard Browder and Mary Eppes Randolph of Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  They were married in late 1799,  probably in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  We know this because Martha Browder first married (1) Simon Swail on May 7, 1792, at Petersburg, Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  This ceremony was performed by the Rev. John Cameron, an Episcopal minister (Bell, Cumberland Parish Lunenburg County, Virginia 1746-1816, Vestry Book 1746-1816). However, Simon Swail soon died. We know that he was deceased at least by 1799 because the Alexandria County, District of Columbia (Arlington County, Virginia) Marriage Bonds Book, 1801-1822, and Minister’s Returns, pp. 128-29 contains a will abstract of Simon Swail which reads:

Will of Simon Swail

Recorded: June 3, 1799 Will Bk. 1, p. 281

Heirs: wife Martha; my bro. Vallentin’s son Vall; my sister Betsey and her Simon named for me.

Presented by – Jas. Campbell, Eliz. McDonald.

Thus, this union is obvious from the records, and is also directly stated in the letter written by David P. Browder on November 27, 1901.  David, it should be remembered, was a great-grandson of Richard Browder and Mary. 

Mary “Thompson” (Mary Eppes Randolph) was also a descendant of the Isham family line, in addition to a Randolph family line.  And one should also remember that Isham Coleman Browder had a sister named after Martha’s mother, Mary Thompson Browder (d. 1793), indicating a very close family connection. 

This record demonstrates that Simon Swail soon died, and the couple had been childless.  Therefore, Martha would have inherited the property of Simon Swail, if any, upon his death.  And, in fact, we know that he owned some property because on Jan. 5, 1796, Isaac Lowe deeded 157 acres of land on Little Troublesome and Hogan’s Creeks in Rockingham County, North Carolina to Simon Swail of Petersburg, Virginia. So, it seems obvious that Simon and Martha Swail had intended to move to that area. But Simon soon died and after his death Martha remarried to Isham Coleman Browder. 

Isaac Lowe, by the way, did not marry Mary A. Browder (taken from the court records of the estate of Jones Browder) as some suggest. He married Catherine, whose maiden name is not known, but who outlived him. 

Martha Browder was born in Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  She lived up to about 1811, for certain, probably dying due to complications from child-birth.  Isham is found in the 1820 census of Rockingham County, but, mysteriously, Isham does not appear in the 1830 census, although he was certainly living as late as 1832, according to court records.  One has to think that he was residing with someone else, but I have still not been able to locate him in the 1830 census record. 

It is known that Isham C. Browder was in Prince George County, Virginia, between 1789-90.  He is listed as residing with William Cole in the 1790 Virginia Tax List, which listed all males aged 16 and older within a household.  His mother and siblings may well have also been residing there at that time, but they were not listed.  They would not have been listed because females were not listed at all in those tax records unless they were heads of households, and anyone under the age of 16 would not have been listed at all.  

Again, Isham C. Browder probably remained in Virginia for a few years to be educated, and while there married his cousin, Martha, following the death of her first husband, Simon Swail.  It seems obvious that Talitha Browder and her family, including Isham C. Browder, resided very near the Richard Browder family of Dinwiddie County, Virginia during this time, within the household of William Cole.  So, Isham certainly would have known his cousin, Richard, son of David Browder.  Regardless, by 1800 Isham and Martha Browder had returned to Rockingham County, North Carolina, as shown in the 1800 census and the 1808 Power of Attorney (discussed later).  Talitha, as well her other children, would have returned by about that same time, all having returned no later than 1800. 

I believe that Augustine Claiborne Browder also spent time in Virginia for educational purposes and may have continued to reside with William Cole after other family members returned to North Carolina, but would have returned soon after they did.  I have found several Booth families in Dinwiddie County, Virginia listed in censuses, etc. which could have been families from which Betsy Booth, Augustine’s wife, came.  If so, then the two brothers both married women from their “ancestral homeland” of Virginia. 

During the August 1800 session of the Rockingham County, North Carolina County Court, Robert Chapman was given leave (allowed a license for the year) to keep the tavern at “Browder’s Crossroads”.  This is the first mention in the records of the tavern located at Browder’s Crossroads.  But it would by no means be the last. 

Interestingly, the very first mention of Isham C. Browder in any record comes from Rockingham County, North Carolina Court Minutes.  During the February 1803 session, Isham Browder was given leave to keep the tavern located at Browder’s Crossroads.  Obviously, this tavern had already existed prior to this point (as shown in the records), but who started it is unclear from this record. 

Still, that is not the first mention in said records of Browder’s Crossroads, for during the February 1800 session of said Court, Edward Newham was appointed as overseer of the road from John Linder’s to “Browder’s Crossroads”.  Later, during the August 1800 session, Robert Chapman was again given leave to keep the tavern at Browder’s Crossroads.

Because of these records, we see that Browder’s Crossroads had been established which accommodated people traveling to and through that area by means of a tavern or, as it was called in the day, an “ordinary”. How much of this had been established by Capt. Isham and Talitha Browder is uncertain. But it seems obvious that they had at least started everything, with Isham Coleman Browder and Robert Chapman, at least, continuing the trend. The records seem to suggest that Augustine had no real interest in the tavern while Isham did. So, the two brothers concentrated on different efforts, Augustine placing more emphasis upon being a Jurist like his father and Isham placing more emphasis upon tavern keeping as well as law enforcement, as we will see. 

To continue with the records, during the May 1802 session, John Smith was appointed as overseer of the road from H. Mill’s to the crossroads, where Chapman’s “Ordinary” (tavern) was located (so, this time it was called “Chapman’s” in the record).  But, during the February 1803 session, Isham Browder was given leave to keep the tavern at the crossroads, with security of 250 pounds from John Wardlow.  Thus, it seems as if the tavern actually sort-of switched hands between the two men, even though it was likely started by a member of the Browder family and was located at Browder’s Crossroads. 

This Robert Chapman (b. C1772, Essex County, Virginia) was a son of Reuben Chapman and Ann Reynolds. He was a relative of Talitha Cox-Browder, Isham’s mother, and also possibly a relative of Elizabeth “Betsy” Booth-Browder, wife of Augustine Claiborne Browder. Robert Chapman married Elizabeth “Bettie” Sutton. 

Now, Isham C. Browder acquired much of the land his father had been granted (at least some 908 acres of it, in all).  According to the land deeds, Isham managed to acquire about 1,308 acres of land in total over the course of his lifetime because he deeded that amount to others.  But, obviously, all of this land did not come from his father.  And, from 1804 to 1807, Isham C. Browder acquired even more land than this in his own right, which was also located on Wolf Island Creek. 

On February 28, 1804, Isham C. Browder sold 70 acres of his land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to Clement Whittemore, Jr. (RCDB I, p. 91).  This deed was witnessed by A[braham]. Philips (the man educated by Thomas Isham Browder), along with J. Ladd. 

Interestingly, during the May 1804 session of the Court, Isham Browder and Thomas Winston were selected to function as slave patrollers in William Bethell’s district.  This is the first indication that Isham served in a law enforcement capacity.  Then, during the August 1804 session, Aaron Cantrell was appointed as overseer of the road from Cantrell’s Meeting House to “Browder’s place”.  Later, during the November 1804 session, William Wheeler was appointed as overseer from Morehead’s Tanyard to “Browder’s Road”.  Also, in November of 1804, Alex Alexerdice was appointed as overseer of the road from “Browder’s Fork” (the same as Browder’s Crossroads) to the Danville Road (now US 29 Business).  And, in November of 1804, Charles Wright was appointed as overseer of the road from Browder’s Fork to Hugh Williams’ old place. 

As will be shown, we know from the records that Isham C. Browder later served as a constable. He also worked in some capacity in the legal field because he was referred to as “Esquire” in later records. When, exactly, he began either is a question, but it would seem that the previous records show that he started at least as early as 1804. 

On September 14, 1805, Isham C. Browder deeded one acre of that land, located on the south side of Wolf Island Creek, to the church trustee, Isaiah Hancock, and the members of the Baptist Society at Wolf Island, for ten shillings.  Witnesses to this deed were Aaron Cantrell and John H. Taylor (RCDB M, p. 33). 

Cantrell’s Meeting House, formed in 1777, had been situated there for some time prior to this transaction, having been started by Isaac Cantrell, who had moved on to South Carolina.  The deed was made so that the new church could officially be established, having its own land, which would include the cemetery already started there, the oldest grave there being known to have been for Talitha Cloud-Cantrell (1729-1768). 

It can be surmised from this that Isham and Martha Browder were possibly members of the Baptist Church at Wolf Island, where his own mother, Talitha, was certainly a member by 1827, according to a list of members for that year.  The present-day church and cemetery are located at Reidsville, North Carolina, at the intersection of Ida Wilson Rd. and Wolf Island Rd. (formerly, Browder’s Road).  It is today called Wolf Island Primitive Baptist Church.  From RCDB M, p. 33 (from a copy made by genealogist Maude Reynolds):

BROWDER, ISHAM To Hancock, Isaiah 1805: This indenture made this 14th day of Sept. in the year of our Lord, one thousand eight hundred and five, between ISHAM BROWDER in the Co. of Rockingham and State of N. Carolina, of the one part, and Isaiah Hancock and members of the Baptist Society at Wolf Island, in conjunction in the same County and state aforesaid, of the other part; – witnesseth that the said ISHAM BROWDER for and in consideration to him of the sum of ten shillings, to him in hand paid at and before the signing of these presents the receipt thereof, he doth hereby acknowledge and hereof exonerate, acquit and discharge, hath given, granted, bargained sold aliened, enfeoffed and confirmed, and do by these presents to give, grant, bargain, sell & confirm unto the sd’ Isaiah Hancock and said members, his and their heirs and assigns, one acre of land, situate, lying and being on the south side of Wolf Island Creek in the county aforesaid: Beginning at a post ISHAM BROWDER’S line, running east 8 poles to a post oak saplin, thence north with ISHAM BROWDER’S line 20 poles to a hickory saplin, thence west 8 poles to a white oak, thence south 20 poles to the first station, and contains 1 acre of land, the same more or less. Together, with all and singular the rights and profits, woods, ways, waters hereditaments and appurtenances to the said land belonging or appertaining to hold to the sd’ Isaiah Hancock, and members, his and their heirs and assigns forever, and the s’d. ISHAM BROWDER and his heirs the said tenement and premises and every part thereof against him and his heirs and against all and every other person or  persons, whatsoever, laying lawful right or claim thereunto, to have and to hold to the said Isaiah Hancock, his heirs and assigns forever do warrant and defend the same. In witness whereof ISHAM BROWDER set my hand and seal, this day and year first above written.

Test: – Aaron Cantrill   ISHAM BROWDER (seal)  John H. Taylor.

So, Isham C. Browder deeded one acre of land to this church body which would have been where the building and the cemetery were located already.  The earliest extant gravestone in this cemetery is dated 1817.  There are, in addition, known to probably be at least 100 unmarked graves in this cemetery.  Many of these date to earlier than 1817, and could include Browders.  In fact, once again, it is probable that Thomas Isham, Talitha, Martha, Isham C., and Augustine Claiborne Browder (along with any earlier children Isham and Talitha may have had) are all buried there, along with related members of the Harris family, in unmarked graves.  It seems more than obvious that the church was located on Cantrell land, which was borderingIsham’s property, and that Isham sold the one acre to this church because he wanted the part of the cemetery where his father had been buried to be included in the church property. Also, from RCDB K, p. 21:  

This indenture made the 28th day of February in the Year One Thousand eight hundred and five Between Aquilla Wilson Late of the County of Rockingham in the State of North Carolina of the one part and Lewis W. Whittmore in the Same County and State aforesaid of the other part WITNESSETH that the Said Aquilla Wilson for and in consideration to him of the sum of Three hundred and forty six dollars in hand paid, the Receipt whereof the Said Aquilla Wilson do hereby acknowledge and hereof exonerate aquit and discharge, Hath given granted bargained sold aliened enfeoffed embossed and  confirmed and do by These presents give grant bargain and Sell and confirm unto the Said Lewis W. Whittmore one hundred and Sixty nine acres of Land situate lying and being on the Waters of the Lickfork of Hogan’s Creek in the County aforesaid. Beginning at a corner of William Bethells land following his line to the other corner of Bethell’s land Thence South to John Johnstons line . . . it contains one hundred Sixty nine Acres of Land. To Witness whereof To the Said Lewis W. Whittmore his Heirs and assigns forever do warrant and defend by these presents In Witness whereof I the Said William Bethell Jnr. as Attorney in Fact for the Said Aquilla Wilson hath hereunto set my hand and Affixed my Seal in the Day and Year just above written. Signed Sealed and Delivered William Bethell (seal) In the presents of for W. Bethell Aquilla Wilson

ISHAM BROWDER Attorney in Fact

This document shows that Isham C. Browder witnessed said deed, standing in for William Bethell, Jr., who was Aquilla Wilson’s attorney; Isham Browder also being named “Attorney in Fact” because he was Wilson’s Power of Attorney. 

During the November session of 1805, mention is made of the road from John Linder’s to “Browder’s old tavern”.  So, it seems that, if Isham C. Browder did not start said tavern, perhaps Talitha, or even Thomas Isham Browder, did.  Or this document could be referring to an older tavern no longer in use by 1805 because it seems unlikely that the one still in operation would have been referred to as “old”. 

During the August 1806 session, Robert “Chapen” was given leave to keep this tavern at “Browder’s Crossroads” once again. 

In 1807 Isham C. Browder was deeded 100 acres of land.  On March 6, 1807, Isham Browder sold 41 acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to George Courts.  George paid Isham $82.00 for said land.  This land was bounded by land already owned by George Courts, as well as the land of Samuel Smith and Clement Whittemore.  Witnesses to the deed were A[braham]. Philips and Aaron Cantrill.  This deed was proven in open court and recorded during the May Session of 1807 (RCDB M, p. 244). 

Also on the same date, Isham Browder sold 127 acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to Samuel Smith (RCDB M, p. 247).  A[braham]. Philips and Aaron Cantrell also witnessed this second deed, which was proven during the May Session of 1807.

During the May 1808 session of the County Court, William Lawson was appointed as overseer of the road leading from William Bethell’s house to Isham Browder’s.  During the November 1808 session of the County Court, Alex Alexedire was appointed as overseer of the road leading from the forks of “Browder’s Road” to Wolf Island Meeting House (church).  Also, Vincent Wheeler was appointed as overseer of the road from Morehead’s Shop to the forks of Browder’s Road. 

And, as indicated previously, in 1808 Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder, his wife, gave Power of Attorney to Richard Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia to sell Martha’s interest in about 200 acres of land, located on the Gravelly Run (RCDB N, p. 339).  While all of this is not directly specified, Richard Browder, Martha’s father, was not a large landowner and probably only owned about 200 acres at the time of his death.  So, it is certain that he did not bequeath it all to Martha, but bequeathed each of his living children a certain percentage of divided interest in said land.  But, sadly, we don’t have a Will to consult.

It is likely that the Richard Browder mentioned in the Power of Attorney was the son of her brother, David Browder and his wife, Mary Cousins and that the land in question must have been inherited from her father, Richard Browder, who resided in Dinwiddie County all of his life.  It should be understood that there is no reason for Martha to have been named in such a document other than the fact that she had inherited a percentage in said land, and that she was actually the one giving Power of Attorney to sell land that she had inherited.  Otherwise, it would have only been necessary for Isham to have been named. 

So, this document shows a direct relationship between Richard Browder and his daughter, Martha, and shows that she married her cousin, Isham C. Browder.  It clearly shows a connection between Martha Browder and Dinwiddie County, Virginia.  The fact that Power of Attorney was given to a member of the Browder family also shows conclusively that Martha was a member of the Browder family.  Otherwise, why would she not have elected a member of some other family?  This is the only known document that conclusively names Martha Browder as the wife of Isham C. Browder, although the division of Jones Browder’s property also indicates it directly.  From RCDB N, p. 339 (from a copy by genealogist, Maude Reynolds):

Browder, Richard from BROWDER, ISHAM & WIFE, Power of Attorney, 200 A of Land: Know all men by these presents that we ISHAM BROWDER and MARTHA BROWDER his WIFE, both of the county of Rockingham and state of N. C., have for good cause to us moving, have nominated, authorized, ordained and appointed, and do by these presents authorize, ordain and appoint our friend Richard Browder of the county of Dinwiddia and commonwealth of Va., to act, make over convey and confirm a certain piece or parcel of land situate, lying and being in the said county of Dinwiddia and commonwealth aforesaid and the waters of the Gravelly Run, and contains about 200 acres of land:  And we, the said ISHAM BROWDER and MARTHA BROWDER for ourselves and heirs, creditors and administrators do make or convey and confirm unto the said Richard Browder full power absolute authority to sell and dispose, make over, convey and confirm at the will and pleasure, a sure and good right and title to the aforesaid land and premises as above described, to whomsoever he may think proper, as full and ample in every respect, as we could do were we personally present, to all which  matter and things – we the said ISHAM BROWDER and the said MARTHA BROWDER for ourselves our heirs creditors and Administrators firmly by these presents, sealed with our seals and dated this day of ___ in the year 1808. Signed, sealed and acknowledged in the presence of ISHAM BROWDER (seal) Augustine C. Browder MARTHA BROWDER (seal) Thos. Winston Joseph Mclain St. of N. C. Rockingham Co. May Session 1810: The written power of attorney was duly proved in open court by the oath of Thomas Winston & on motion ordered to be recorded.      R. Galloway, C.

Notice that, although he was a witness, Augustine C. Browder did not directly inherit any of said land, nor similar property, and did not make a Power of Attorney to convey anything himself.  This might have been the case if Isham C.
