Browder were the inheritor of said property, because his brother, as well as other siblings, would have also inherited similar property from Richard Browder in that case.  This shows that Isham C. Browder was not descended from Richard Browder, but that Martha Browder was.  She is obviously the one who inherited this property, located in Dinwiddie County, Virginia. 

It is interesting to note that, in this document it is stated that the couple believed that they had good reason to move.  Obviously, the inference is that they moved from Dinwiddie County, Virginia, or close to that location, and relocated back to Rockingham County, North Carolina at some point, obviously prior to 1800.  What prompted this move?  The answer to this question is unknown, but they probably always intended to.  Whatever their reasoning was, they considered it good enough not only to move, but also to elect another trusted family member to deal with her inheritance so that they did not have to return to do so.   What eventually happened to said property is still an open question since I have located no further documentation concerning it.  But, presumably, it was sold, so that Isham and Martha Browder received their part of the proceeds from said sale.

Later, during the February 1809 session of the Court, George Courts was appointed as overseer of the road from William Bethell’s to Isham Browder’s.  And John Ellington was appointed as overseer of the road from “Browder’s Fork” to Richard C. Hall’s.  Also, during the May 1809 session of the Court, William Lawson was appointed as overseer of the road leading from William Bethell’s to Isham Browder’s.  Finally, in August of 1809, the Court utilized Isham Browder as one of its jurors during that current court session. 

During the May 1810 session of the Court, Abel Hudson was appointed overseer of the road from Morehead’s Shop to “the forks of Browder’s Road”.  Also, William Lawson was appointed overseer of Iron Works Road from Caswell’s line (the Caswell County line) to Isham Browder’s.  

During the February 1811 session of the Court, Charles Wright was appointed overseer of the road from the forks of Browder’s Road to Wolf Island Meeting House. 

Then, on June 4, 1812, Isham Browder sold 51 acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to Clement Whittemore, Jr.  Isham signed this document, as he often (but not always) did, rather than only providing a mark.  This deed was witnessed by A[braham]. Philips, Sterling Whittemore, and Pleasant Ellington.  It was proven during the May Session of 1816 (RCDB Q, p. 124). 

Also, during the February 1812 session of the Court, “Pideus” Wright was appointed as overseer of the road from Browder’s forks to Richard C. Hall’s. 

During the August 1813 session of the County Court, John Miller was appointed overseer of the road from Browder’s to Richard C. Hall’s.  During the February 1814 session of the Court, the Sheriff was instructed to pay John Harris (Jr.), Isham Browder, and John Hancock ten shillings for slave patrols in Capt. ? district.  And during the August 1814 session, the Court used Isham Browder as one of its jurors during that current session. 

During the February 1815 session of the Court, Philip Briserdine was appointed overseer of the road from Col. Bethell’s to Browder’s Crossroads, with help from George Courts, William Lawson, R. H. Courts, and Samuel Smith, Sr.  And, during the August 1815 session of the Court, Isham Browder was again used as a juror, along with others. 

The 1815 Rockingham County tax list shows that Isham C. Browder had (or claimed) 743 acres of land, valued at $1,486.00, at that time.  Isham Browder also received another 51 acres back from Clement Whittemore, Jr. in 1816 (RCDB Q, p. 124).  And, in 1817, Isham Browder sold two slaves to Francis Bell (RCDB Q, p. 431). 

During the February 1816 session of the Court, Abraham Whittemore was appointed overseer of the road leading from William Lawson’s lower plantation on Wolf Island Creek to Isham Browder’s, with the help of Elizabeth Browder, George Courts, William Lawson, and others. 

On February 24, 1818, Isham C. Browder purchased 22 more acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, from Richard Micheaux.  This deed was witnessed by Abraham Philips, and proven during the February Session of 1818 (RCDB R, p. 165).  And, on December 6, 1824, Isham Browder sold 350 acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to William J. Meador (RCDB Y, p. 175), which was Isham’s way of beginning to divide his land between his two heirs.   Obviously, for such a transaction to have taken place, Talitha Browder would have had to have given her blessing and Isham’s siblings would have understood that it was his to do with as he chose.  From RCDB Y, p. 175 (from a copy made by genealogist, Maude Reynolds – name Meador corrected from “Meadows”):

BROWDER, ISHAM To Meador, William Deed 350 a Wolf Is. Ck. 1824: This indenture made this 6th day of Dec. in the year of our Lord, 1824, between ISHAM BROWDER of the county of Rockingham & St. of N. Car., of the one part and Wm. Meador of the same Co. & St. aforesaid. Witnesseth that the said BROWDER for & in consideration of the sum of $400.00 to him in hand paid by the said Wm. Meador, the said BROWDER doth bargain, sell & convey and by these presents doth sell one certain tract or parcel of land situate, lying & being in said county & on the waters of Wolf Island Creek: Beginning on a post oak thence north 82 degrees East 308 poles to a post oak, thence south 80 poles to a peach tree in De Lap’s field, thence west 136 poles to a post oak De Lap’s corner thence south 226 poles to a black oak, thence west 170 poles to a black gum saplin thence north 260 poles to the first station, containing 350 acres of land, more or less, to have & to hold to the said Wm. Meador, his heirs & assigns forever: the aforesaid tract of land together with appurtenances thereunto belonging or in any wise appertaining to the only proper use & behest (?) of said Meador, his heirs & assigns forever; and I the said ISHAM BROWDER do warrant & defend the right title & interest of said Land  against any claim or claims whatsoever that may hereafter come against the said land unto the day and date first written. Signed, sealed & delivered in presents of us

Thos. S. Sharp Saml. Barnes ISHAM BROWDER (seal) St. of N. Car. Rockingham Co. N. C. Dec. Ses. 1824: The execution of the within deed from ISHAM BROWDER to Wm. Meador was duly proved in open court by the oath of said Barnes and on motion ordered to be rec Robt. Galloway CC.

Land deeds from Isham C. Browder to his son, Richard Browder, begin after this.  On December 6, 1824, Isham Browder sold Richard Browder 320 acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, which adjoined property of Levin Harris (RCDB Y, p. 175-76).  We know that Richard Browder was Isham’s son since he states so in this document.  This document, along with others, proves that Isham Richard Browder, who later moved to McNairy County, Tennessee, was Isham C. Browder’s son.   From RCDB Y, p. 175-76 (from a copy made by genealogist, Maude Reynolds): 

RICHARD BROWDER From ISHAM BROWDER deed 320 a. Wolf Is. Ck.: This indenture made this 6th day of Dec. in the year of our Lord 1824 between ISHAM BROWDER of the Co. of Rockingham & St. of N. Car. of the one part & RICHARD BROWDER of the same Co. & state aforesaid of the other part; – witnesseth: that the said ISHAM BROWDER for the natural love & affection he hath for his son RICHARD BROWDER & also for the further sum of $1.00 to him in hand paid by the said RICHARD BROWDER, his son, he hath, this day bargained, sold & conveyed and as by these presents, give grant, bargain, sell, alien & confirm unto the said RICHARD BROWDER his heirs & assigns, a part of the tract of land whereon I now live, situate on the waters of Wolf Island Crk. & bounded as follows (to wit): beginning at a post oak in the original line south through the survey 265 poles to a black gum saplin in LEVIN HARRIS’S line thence with his line west 106 poles to a hickory thence south 80 poles to a black oak thence west 20 poles to a post oak thence north 203 poles to a white oak thence west 44 poles, thence north 80 degrees west 62 poles, thence south 83 degrees west 66 poles, thence north 55 degrees west 22 poles thence down the various meanders of the creek to a small beach on the side of the hill thence north 82 degrees East 200 poles to the first station containing 320 acres of land, be the same more or less, to have & to hold the aforesaid tract of land or parcel of land together with all the appurtenances to the only proper use of the said RICHARD BROWDER, his heirs & assigns forever, and I, the said ISHAM BROWDER do warrant & defend the right title of said land against any claim or claims whatsoever that may hereafter come against said land unto the said RICHARD BROWDER his heirs & assigns forever: in witness hereof I have hereunto set my hand & affixed my seal, this 6th day of Dec.  in the year A.D. 1824. signed, sealed & delivered in presence of                                   his

Thos. S. Sharp   ISHAM x BROWDER         Samuel Barnes             

        mark

St. of N. C. Rockingham Co. Dec. Ses. 1824: Executed and on motion ordered recorded.        Robt. Galloway C.C.

On January 26, 1826 Isham Browder deeded 168 more acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to Richard Browder, his son.  From RCDB Z, p. 304 (from a copy made by genealogist, Maude Reynolds):

BROWDER, ISHAM To BROWDER, RICHARD Deed 168 A. Wolf Is. Ck. 1826: This indenture made this 26th day of Jan. in the year of our Lord, 1826, between ISHAM BROWDER of the Co. of Rockingham & state of N. Car., of the one part & RICHARD BROWDER of the same Co. & State of the other part; witnesseth: that the said ISHAM BROWDER for, and in consideration of natural love & affection and the further consideration of the sum of $50.00 to him the said ISHAM in hand paid by the said RICHARD at & before the signing & sealing of these presents the receipt whereof is hereby acknowledged & the said ISHAM hath bargained, sold & delivered & doth by these presents bargain sell & deliver unto the said RICHARD BROWDER his heirs & assigns forever, the following  tract of land, to wit; – Beginning at a white oak, running thence north 30 poles to a white oak, thence west 18 poles to a post oak thence north 136 poles to a black oak corner, thence west crossing a branch 190 poles to a black oak, thence north crossing a branch 94 poles to a black oak thence east 56 poles to a black oak thence east 152 poles to the 1st station lying in the Co. of Rockingham & on the waters of Wolf Island Ck., containing 168 acres of land, be the same more or less with all the privileges and appurtenances thereunto belonging or in any wise appertaining to have & to hold the same to the said RICHARD BROWDER his heirs & assigns forever, and, I, the said ISHAM BROWDER do, for myself my heirs, executors, administrators & assigns covenant & agree to warrant and defend the right title & intrest in and to the aforesaid tract of land to the said RICHARD BROWDER, his heirs & assigns forever free & clean from any claim or any claims by through or under me & against the claim or claims of all other persons – in witness whereof I have hereunto set my hand & seal, the day and date above written. signed, sealed & delivered in presence of           ISHAM BROWDER (seal)

             John Forrest.  

St. of N. Car. Rockingham Co. Feb. Ses. 1826. The execution of the written deed from ISHAM BROWDER to RICHARD BROWDER was duly proven in open court by the oath of John Forrest & on motion ordered to be recorded.

Robt. Galloway C.C.C.

The above deed obviously references the 168 acres received by Capt. Isham Browder from the State of North Carolina on November 8, 1784.  Finally, on January 26, 1826, Isham Browder sold 168 more acres of land, located on Wolf Island Creek, to his son, Richard Browder (RCDB Z, p. 310).   This last tract of land is the one that Richard Browder then immediately sold to Clement Whittemore, Jr. (ibid). 

The initial 168 acre tract is the one that Isham C. Browder actually resided on at Browder’s Crossroads, Isham likely finally selling it and the latter 168 acre tract both on the same date to Richard Browder due to failing health. Richard was then expected to take over, in effect. 

The following map shows the final division of the lands by Isham C. Browder (obviously he had surveys done that we don’t have any record of anymore in order to do this). On this map, outlined in red, is the property Isham C. Browder deeded to his son-in-law, William J. Meador. Outlined in Green are the properties that Isham C. Browder deeded to his son, Richard Browder. Also, on this map, the properties are placed on Wolf Island Creek as best as is possible, and it shows several roads, including Browder Road, culminating in Browder’s Crossroads at the southern end close to Wolf Island Meeting House. 

Now, moving back in time just a little, this family is not shown in the 1790 census of Rockingham County, North Carolina.  However, before 1800, according to the census of Salisbury, Rockingham County, for that year, “Isam” and Martha Browder had already had their first child, a daughter.  The child is listed as being under ten years of age.  We know that her name was Mary Thompson “Polly” Browder, named after Isham’s sister, who had been murdered by Native Americans in 1793.  Mary Thompson Browder, daughter of Isham C. and Martha Browder, would have been born in 1800.  This family also owned one slave in 1800.  They resided close to Talitha Browder, and also close to Clem. Whittemore, William Bethell, and John Dillon. 

By 1810, according to the Rockingham County census of that year, Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder had two sons, as well as one daughter.  Their sons are listed as being between the ages of 10-15 and under 10, respectively.  The daughter is listed in the next to youngest age range (10-15). 

We do know from extant records that one of their sons, my ancestor, was named Richard.  Richard Browder was born about 1801 (late 1800 or early 1801), so he would have been the first son.  

In 1810, according to the already mentioned census, the Isham C. Browder family still resided close to Talitha Browder, who resided with one daughter (Martha) and 12 slaves at the old home place.  They also resided close to the family of Isham’s brother, Augustine, and close to the family of John Sanders (who was in the oldest category of males for that census), and the family of Zephaniah Sanders (same age category), as well as the families of Abraham Whittemore, William Bethell, William Lawson, William Sanders, and Walter Martin.

The 1820 census of Rockingham County, North Carolina lists Isham Browder (45 and over), along with his daughter (Mary Thompson, 26-45) as residing close to the households of his mother, Talitha Browder, and his son, Richard Browder.  Also listed within the same household is one male, aged between 16 and 26 (presumably Richard Browder’s brother).  Isham is also listed as owning two male slaves that year.  Martha, wife of Isham C. Browder, is not listed.  So, sadly, it becomes obvious that she was deceased by this date in time. 

Richard Browder, their son, is listed separately in the 1820 census as head of household.  We also know that Richard’s presumed brother reached maturity since he is listed as still residing with Isham Browder in 1820.  According to this census, Isham Browder could also read and write, as we already know (but, he still sometimes signed only with a mark). 

Elizabeth Browder, widow of Augustine Claiborne Browder, and her family, at that time resided close to the old home place where Talitha Browder and other family members resided, in the home she had already been residing in for several years. It is obvious that they resided on the 640 acre tract that Talitha Browder had purchased from John Browder. We know this partly because this tract was not among those that Isham C. Browder sold or divided between his two heirs. So, it was obviously understood, regardless of what Capt. Isham Browder’s 1779 will stated, that this land was to go to Augustine and his heirs. 

Interestingly, during the November 1825 session of the County Court, the case of the State vs. Isham Browder and Polly Newman came up.  It is not at all clear what this case was about, but either he or they was/were found guilty as charged.  Isham was fined $50.00, and Polly was fined $10.00, with John Forrest to pay her fine for her. 

Now, again, it is known that Isham C. Browder served as a Rockingham County Constable during the years 1829, 1830 and 1831 (Perdue), as shown by the records.  Whether he served in this capacity prior to these dates cannot be ascertained from extant records, but it seems obvious that he did.  It is interesting to note here that future U.S. President, Andrew Jackson studied law in Guilford County, North Carolina, and was also appointed as a Constable there in 1789 (Stoesen, p. 9).  Could the Browder family have known him personally and professionally during this time?  It is entirely possible.  Richard Browder himself certainly knew him at a later date, I think.  From p. 125, Rockingham County Minute Docket, Spring Term 1807 to Spring Term 1826:

October 27, 1817: ISHAM BROWDER on Jury duty.  The Honorable Henry Sewell, Judge, presiding.  

Also, during the August 1830 session of the County Court, William Smith was appointed overseer for the road from Nathan Wright’s to Isham Browder’s, with help from Jos. Newman and others.  In 1832, Caleb Harris (son of John Harris, Jr.) was appointed overseer of the road from Nathan Wright’s to Isham Browder’s, with help from Reuben Reed, Jas. Newman, and William Denton.  A quote from genealogist, Maude Reynolds, in a letter to my great-uncle, Herman E. Browder, states:

In the Minute Book, 1813-1819, I find on page 13, an order by the court to have the county surveyor, survey certain acreages – one such tract of 155 acres – adjoining the lands of ISHAM BROWDER, on Jacob’s Crk. (This would indicate that he lived near there; Jacob’s Crk. Flows into Dan River, several miles west of Wentworth; Alexander Martin’s home place was at the mouth of Jacob’s Creek & Dan River.  There is a large house just this side of Jacob’s Creek, that has intrigued me.  I’ve tried to find who originally built it without success – but still hope to get information on it).

Further, about the above-mentioned property, the following information from RCDB A, p. 58 (originally copied by genealogist, Maude Reynolds/excerpt):

St. of N. Car. Grant 500 A, head of Rocky branch Ck. Guilford Co. To Jesse Browder: For consideration of 50 shillings for every 100 acres.  Tract of land containing 400 acres at the head of Rocky branch & little Troublesome Cks. Guilford Co. a description follows – lies along Benjamin Granger’s line, also Andrew Martin’s line. . . . Alexander Martin Gov. at time of transaction Nov. 8th 1784.

So, the above-mentioned property, originally deeded to Jesse Browder, was bounded by property of Isham Browder, all of which was located close to the residence of Alexander Martin.  Alexander Martin represented North Carolina during the Constitutional Convention of the United States in 1776.  He was born in 1740 in Hunterdon County, New Jersey, and was the son of Hugh and Jane Martin. 

It should be remembered here that Edmund J. Randolph was a Representative during the Constitutional Convention of the United States, from Virginia, in 1776.  So, Randolph was representative from Virginia, and Martin was representative from North Carolina.  And Alexander Martin later became Governor of North Carolina. 

It has previously been thought by some researchers that Isham C. Browder’s Will was dated 1833 (RCWB B, p. 84).  However, having requested, and received, the above dated Will myself from the North Carolina State archives, it has become obvious to me that the Will in question is, instead, that of Capt. Isham Browder, which was dated 1779 and only recorded in 1833.  So, evidently, Isham C. Browder left no extant Will. 

After Isham C. Browder died in early (by April) 1832, during the November 1833 session of the County Court, Clement Whittemore, Jr. was ordered to give see as administrator of Isham Browder’s estate.  Also, Richard Browder and William J. Meador had already received some perishable property, which they split among themselves.  This property was received by them on April 17, 1832. 

So, Clement Whittemore, Jr. served as administrator of Isham C. Browder’s estate, just as he had also been one of the administrators of Talitha Browder’s, estate (although Abner Whittemore had originally been named as administrator of Isham C. Browder’s estate).  This document, found in Rockingham County Inventory Book A, 1829-1833 (from copy done by genealogist, Maude Reynolds) reads verbatim as follows:

List of perishable property coming into the hands of Wm. J. Meadour & RICHARD BROWDER, son of ISHAM BROWDER, dcsd.  2 head of horses, 2 colts, 2 cows, 9 yearlings, 2 calves, 4 sows, 10 goats (?), 9 young pigs, 1 desk & bookcase, 1 corner cupboard, other pieces of furniture…., etc., bushels of wheat, oats, corn, etc., 5 gallons vinegar, 317 lbs bacon: 40 lbs dry beef, half bushel salt.      RICHARD BROWDER      William J. Meador

The above inventory sworn to in open court Feb. 24, 1835

           Test. A. Holderby, C. C.

It is obvious from the list of property here that, by this generation, our Browder ancestors were no longer in the tobacco business.  This is in contrast to the Isham Browder (1762-1830), the American Revolutionary War soldier.  He was very much in the tobacco business.  It seems, then, that the Isham C. Browder and Martha Browder family made their living strictly by farming, and through the profits of various land transactions (and, probably also through horse breeding and racing).  This in addition to whatever legal fees Isham may have charged for his services and what little a constable of that day might have been paid. 

Only Richard and William J. Meador received any perishable property following the death of his father, Isham.  This brings about a question.  Why indeed had Richard’s brother received no perishable property upon the death of his father, Isham?  It seems obvious that he was deceased by this time.  

William J. Meador married Richard’s older sister, (1) Mary Thompson Browder (1800-c1844).  In Rockingham County Marriage Bonds Book [RCMBB]   ?, p. 172, can be found a record of the marriage between “Polly” Browder and William J. Meador on December 24, 1823 (Ingmire, p. 46).  Clement Whittemore, Jr. stood as bondsman, and the ceremony was witnessed by Robert Galloway, Jr.  This is the main reason we can conclude that she is the one listed in the 1820 census, rather than her mother.  She was still residing within the household of her father, Isham C. Browder, in 1820, since she had not yet married. 

By 1837 William J. Meador himself had sold 229 acres of land to Luke Palmer.  Thereafter, he and his wife, Mary, moved to Itawamba County, Mississippi. 

But the documents relating to Richard Browder, himself, do not cease here, although they add little to our knowledge.  These documents pertain to the estate of Capt. Isham Browder following Talitha’s death.  Still, I have listed them here because of the dates.   From Rockingham County Inventory Book 1833-1836 we glean the following, copied verbatim by genealogist, Maude Reynolds:

Page 123 – Return of Clement Whittmore, admin., with the will annexed of ISHAM BROWDER, dcsd., of the hire of the negroes of said estate during the period he has hired them out – For the year 1829:  Joseph Young – 4 negroes $120.00; 1831 – 6 negroes $344.92; 1833 – 6 negroes $340.42. Page 124 – For the year 1830 – Clement Whittmore took Jasper and Sarah for their food & clothing.  RICHARD BROWDER for the same year – kept old Jenny the same way.

In fact, the records show several persons hiring the slaves from the estate of Talitha Browder year by year, including Richard Browder himself.  Please understand that at this time no one had found the 1779 will of Capt. Isham Browder, so the 1778 will was the one being annexed and followed, whatever it said.  Everything was being done according to whatever the 1778 will stipulated.  Such records continue through pages 124 and 125.  

On pages 227-28 the following notation is made:

Elizabeth Browder widow of Augustin C. Browder Decd. had possession of Jim from the death of Talitha Browder until the day of the foregoing sale & is therefore liable for the time of him. 

Isham Browder had possession of Bill & Charles since the death of his mother until his own death, and his Executors have had possession, or have hired them out since, until the day of sale aforesaid, and hire ought to be due for their services. Several of the negroes of the estate were hired out by Talitha Browder for a year and Bonds taken – she died during the year, a part of these Bonds belong to the estate.

Also whatever hire may be due, if any, for the Negroes above mentioned in Georgia. Also whatever Interest may be due upon the monies arising from the several hirings and services.

Sworn in open Court this 24th Feby. 1835.

Test: J. Holderby C. C.

Further, on Nov. 25, 1834, the sale of all of the slaves from the estate of Capt. Isham Browder was completed by the administrator, Clement Whittemore, Jr.  Several persons purchased slaves, including Nathaniel Roach, Milton A. Browder, Robert Martin, William Meador (so we know that he and his wife still resided in Rockingham County), Edmund Martin, Augustine B. Fears (even though he was residing in Georgia, as shown in the documents), Matthew Mills, James M. Scales, and even Richard Browder (who purchased a girl named Mary). This, by the way, would have been Richard’s only known (or necessary since he could hire them prior to this) purchase of a slave prior to moving to McNairy County, Tennessee. An example from said return follows: 

Page 146 – Additional inventory of ISHAM BROWDER estate, returned by admnr. May 26th 1835, one negro man named Charles sold to Milton A. Browder. $666.00. Page 223 – Return of Clement Whittmore, admnr. November 25, 1834.

In addition, it is noted that a man named Charles had been in the possession of Richard Browder, but hired out to Edmund Martin to work in the gold mines in Burke, but that Charles had broken his arm while working in said mines.  For this reason, he remained unsold.  It is also noted that several slaves were already in the possession of Talitha Clark and her husband, Isaac, in Georgia as well as one in the possession of Augustine B. Fears.  All of these were ordered to be sold by Augustine B. Fears.  

If all of this seems a bit mixed up or confusing, so does the court case.  The case concerning the Will of Thomas Isham Browder, which plagued Isham C. Browder’s final years, was one of the major probate court cases in early laws of civil proceedings in the United States.  Documentation from these proceedings follows verbatim:

State of North Carolina  Rockingham County   To the worshipful the Justices of the Court of Pleas & Quarter Sessions for Rockingham County:  The petition of Clement Whittmore & Martha his wife Elizabeth Browder, administrator of Augustus Browder deceased also of Milton A. Browder Isham Browder Junr Nancy Browder Augustus Browder Elizabeth Browder which said Augustus and Elizabeth are minors prefer this their petition by their Mother & next friend the aforesaid Elizabeth Browder, also of Nathaniel Roach and Tabitha (sic) his wife.       Against  ISHAM BROWDER JUNR William Meadour administrators of the Estate of Nancy Thrasher decd., also against Isaac Clark and Talitha his wife and Augustine Fears-   Humbly sheweth that ISHAM BROWDER late of the County of Rockingham departed this life in the spring of 1785 leaving a widow named TALITHA BROWDER and the following children, to wit, ISHAM BROWDER Augustus Browder Mary Browder Nancy Washington Browder Talitha Browder and Martha Browder, that the said Mary Browder intermarried with Richard Thrasher and they both died in the year 1792 leaving an only child named Nancy Thrasher who had since died and administration of her estate hath been granted to William Meadour by this worshipful Court.  That the said Nancy Washington Browder intermarried with Cloud Thrasher who having died leaving two children both of whom died minors & without issue.  She intermarried with William Fears and the said Nancy and William are both dead she having died first leaving an only legitimate child named Augustus Fears who is an infant.  That the said Talitha Browder first married Isaac Thrasher by whom she had Three children now living.  He having died she intermarried with Isaac Clark.  That the said Augustus Browder hath long since died intestate and administration of his estate was granted by this Worshipful court to your petitioner Elizabeth Browder his widow; and he left the following children to wit, your petitioners Milton A. Browder Talitha Browder intermarried with, Isham Nathaniel Roach Browder Junr. Nancy Browder Augustus Browder, & Elizabeth Browder – And your petitioner Martha hath intermarried with your  petitioner Clement Whittmore – Your Petitioners show that the said ISHAM BROWDER having died as before mentioned his WIDOW took possession of his estate claiming to hold the same for life under a paper writing which she alledged was the last Will and Testament of her Said Husband bearing date 27th Sept. 1778 or thereabouts, but which was never proved as such so far forth as your petitioners can ascertain.  That she continued to hold and dispose of the said property until her death which happened a short time ago, having all that time in her possession or custody another paper writing duly executed by the said ISHAM BROWDER as his last Will & Testament on or about the 27th day of Sept. 1779 and which after his death she found among his valuable papers and effects and carefully concealed the same from your petitioners who although they had understood during her life time that such paper writing had been found after the said ISHAM’S death, never saw the same nor knew the contents thereof nor whether the same had been kept or destroyed until the month of April last when the same was discovered among the papers of the said ISHAM which had been kept and preserved by his said widow.  Your petitioners shew that they have annexed to this their petition the said paper writing and that the same is the last Will and Testament of the said ISHAM BROWDER decd every part thereof being written in his own proper hand writing and duly signed by him – and your petitioners pray that the same may be admitted to probate in this Worshipful Court and that process may issue to the said ISHAM BROWDER JUNR. and William Meadour administrators as aforesaid, that publication be made in some news paper designated by your Worships for the said Isaac Clark and Talitha his wife and the said Augustus Fears who resides out of this state to appear and be made parties defendants to the petition and to show cause if any they have why the said paper writing should not be admitted to probate in this court as the last Will of ISHAM BROWDER deceased – And as the said Augustus Fears is a minor your petitioners pray that your Worships will appoint a guardian pending this petition to defend his interest therein – Your Petitioners pray for such other and further relief as their case may require and that the process to be issued in this case may be returnable to the next Court of Pleas and quarter Sessions to be held for Rockingham County on the fourth Monday of August next                         J. M. Morhead Atty. 31st July 1830

Isham C. Browder answered the foregoing Petition thus: 

State of North Carolina Rockingham County  The answer of ISHAM BROWDER JR to the Petition of Clement Whittmore his Wife Martha & others against Him & others.  The Defendant states that his FATHER died in or about the time mentioned in the Petition, but he is of opinion that he died earlier and that he left him missing the children as stated in the Petition.  This defendant for the further answer saith that he was an infant of tender years at the time of his FATHER making his Will and was only ten or eleven years old  at the time of his FATHER’S death, but he states expressly that he always understood from his MOTHER & his brother & sisters and that thereafter their arrival at full age that the Will of his Father of the 21st September 1778 was his Will & that the same had been duly sworn in Guilford County Court of which County this then constituted a part and that his MOTHER TALITHA BROWDER had qualified as Executrix thereto and acted under the same.  He further states that his brother and sisters were all of full age for a long time before his MOTHER’S death and that he never heard of the pretended Will of his Father having date 27th September 1779 until some time since the death of his MOTHER.  He states that he is informed that the pretended Will of 1779 has endorsements on it made in the hand writing of his brother Augustine Browder purporting to have been made in the year 1804.  He further states that at that time Augustine was twenty one years of age and lived until about the year 1814 that he was the youngest child of his FATHER except Martha Whittmore & that she was more than twenty one years of age when she intermarried with Clement Whittmore and they have been married more than twenty years.  This Deft showeth that his brother and sisters were of full age and under no legal disability for nearly thirty years before the death of his MOTHER which was on the 3rd day of July 1828   He again states that he has uniformly understood from his childhood both from his MOTHER Brother & sisters that his FATHER’S Will had been duly and regularly proven & that his MOTHER qualified as Executrix to the same & he verily believes that fact to be so & if it should not appear from the records of Guilford County Court, it has been owing to the negligence or misconduct of the Clerk in not making the proper entries on the records of said probate & qualification of the Executrix.  He further shows that said Will of 21st September 1778 was filed among the proven Wills of Guilford County shortly after the death of his Father where it has remained ever since.  That opposite the name of Charles Gilly one of the subscribing witnesses is a mark made by the Clerk of Guilford & which was his usual mark when a witness had proven a deed or Will & he further shows that there is this further endorsement on the Will made by the Clerk at the time as he believed En Testified.  He therefore submits to the Court whether after so long a time, when the Clerk of the Court who made the entries is dead & the four subscribing witnesses to the Will are dead the negligence or misconduct of the Clerk in not making the proper entries on his docket is to be the cause of wholly changing his Father’s estate & rendering void all the acts of his Executors.  He prays to be dismissed from said Petition with his costs.            Thos. Settle Atty for Deft. ISHAM BROWDER maketh oath that all singular the facts stated in this his answer which are the facts as of his own knowledge and true & that stated as not of his own knowledge he believes to be true

 Sworn and Subscribed to             ISHAM BROWDER before me this 4th day of Feby 1831.    Wilson S. Heir JP

All that seems to be certain about the purported Will of 1778 is that it has apparently not survived to this day, and that no one, other than Isham, seemed to have been particularly interested in it during the lawsuit period.  It seems reasonable to believe, however, that the “lost Will” would have changed nothing between family members in any case.  The court determined that all real estate was to be more or less equally divided among all of the children of Isham and Talitha Browder. 

But, obviously, Isham C. Browder had indeed seen this Will and it is the one that had earlier been annexed and followed.  Capt. Isham Browder surely had meant for this Will to have been replaced by the 1779 Will in any case.  The fact that Isham Browder never wrote another Will to include his remaining children more directly, by name, is sad.  But it changed nothing.  The change was brought about due to the fact that family members initiated the lawsuit.  If they had not done that, it seems to me that the 1778 Will of Isham Browder would have been followed, because it never would have wound up in court.  This is what Talitha Browder had obviously intended – to keep everything intact and bequeathed according to the Will of her late husband. 

Sometimes, one can actually solve a mystery by noting what is not said, just as much as what is said.  It is my belief, even after all of the previous speculation as to what may have caused the death of Capt. Isham Browder, that the greatest likelihood is that he simply died of some unfortunate accident.  This must have been something which caused him to become missing and, perhaps, eventually found, deceased.  This has become obvious to me because, otherwise, his eldest son would surely have known the exact date of his death.  Since he did not, it is painfully obvious that Isham Browder died, perhaps, while out hunting, or during bad weather, or that he encountered someone who did harm to him, and his body was found at some later date.  Whatever it was, it was sudden.  So, no one really knew exactly when he died, or what happened to him.  This would explain a great deal concerning conflicting information in extant records.  In any event, it is still certain that he died in late 1784 or early 1785 (most likely, late 1784).  To continue with the court case: 

Elizabeth Browder & others   Petition for probate of the last will and testament of ISHAM BROWDER the oldest ISHAM BROWDER JNR & others A paper writing filed in the office and mark A purporting to be the last will and testament of ISHAM BROWDER the oldest, was …. offered for probate by Clement Whittmore & his wife Martha—Elizabeth Browder wife of Augustine Browder Decd—also Milton A Browder Isham Browder the youngest—Nancy Browder, Augustine Browder, Elizabeth Browder the younger, the last two of them are minors & ….  This …….. friend Elizabeth Browder Snr & Nathaniel Roach & Talitha his wife, which the probate of which…. was offered by ISHAM BROWDER SNR., William Meadour administrator of Nancy Thrasher Dcd Isaac Clark & Talitha his wife & Augustine Fears a minor who … said probate of his guardian  ……… William Meadour In this cause it is made …. to the ….. of the Court… to a firm order of the Court as to all the …  Defendants & that all the other defendants have been duly named with process Whereupon it is ordered by the Court that a judge be …. & empowered to this petition …. is the last will & testament of said ISHAM BROWDER the Oldest  In pursuance of an order from the Honorable Supr. Court of Law for the county of Rockingham and a commission to us directed from The Clerk of the said Supr. Court of law we Jno. J. Wright and James Miller Two acting Justices of said county caused Abram Philips to appear before us at The Tavern House of Jno. J. Wright in Witness on Monday The 28 day of  November 1831 both Plaintiffs and Defendant being present  The said Abram Philips being duly sworn on the holy Evangelist of the Almighty deposeth and saith that he went to School to ISHAM BROWDER decd. and he was and is well acquainted with his hand writing and that he has had in his possession ever since considerable of his hand writing up to this Time and that he says that The paper writing herewith enclosed marked A and purporting to be the last will and Testament of the said ISHAM BROWDER decd. is the proper hand writing of The said ISHAM BROWDER and every part Thereof to the best of his knowledge and belief and that the Signature and Codicil or addition Thereto he believes is in his proper hand writing and the said ISHAM BROWDER lived of the two of and died upon Wolf Island not far from Delass Shop where his widow for many years Resided in This County which was then a part of the County of Guilford & further this deponent saith not.

          A[braham]. Philips State of North Carolina

Rockingham County  We John J. Wright & James Miller Two acting Justices of the peace for said county do hereby certify that the Foregoing deposition of Abram Philips was subscribed & sworn to . . . at  the time and place Mentioned in the foregoing caption which  deposition is to be  Read as Evidence in a suit now pending in the supr court of law to be held for the county of Rockingham Wherein Elizabeth Browder & others are Plaintiffs and ISHAM BROWDER & others are defendants given and in our seals this 28th Nov. 1831

         Jno. J. Wright (seal)      Ja. Miller (seal)

One can only see the foregoing documentation from the North Carolina State Archives as a surreal situation of at least as great a mystery as anything else involving Isham Browder.  The reason is that this Abraham Philips, having known Isham Browder well, and having outlived both Isham Browder and his wife, Talitha, by this time, stated that he not only has had in his possession several of Isham’s papers (presumably school papers), but still possessed said papers some 46 years after Isham’s death!  Beyond that, what became of all of these papers – not only the ones in the possession of Abraham Philips, but also those which Talitha Browder had in her possession at the time of her death?  Obviously, Capt. Isham Browder left much in the form of important documents, which we do not have access to today. 

It should also be noted that, not only do these foregoing documents essentially prove that Capt. Isham Browder (1) died in mid to late 1784 (Isham C. Browder’s testimony), but also show that he (2) taught school or was a tutor, and that he (3) died at or close to his home in, then, Guilford County, probably on the 569 acre plot since the eastern edge of that was close to DeLap’s shop.  Still, apparently, no one knew (a) when Isham died, or (b) about the contents of the two wills, other than, perhaps, Isham C. Browder, since Talitha was deceased.  It becomes obvious that, in order to prevent hard feelings among family members while she lived, Talitha simply allowed everyone to believe whatever they wished as long as she lived. 

Finally, two proof texts, also from the North Carolina State Archives, that show Isham Browder, Jr.’s middle initial as “C.” are provided.  And on the following pages, the details of the court decision, in which Isham’s name is repeatedly spelled incorrectly (which he did not actually do when writing his own Will) as “Isom”, are presented. 

State of North Carolina Rockingham County   Pleas before the worshipful Court of Please and quarter sessions begun and held for the County of Rockingham at the Court House in Wentworth on the 4 Monday of May 1831   Be it remembered that … On the 4 Monday of August in the year 1830 Elizabeth Browder & others filed their Petition against ISOM BROWDER & others which Petition is herewith sent together with the will of ISOM BROWDER the Eldest appended thereto as part of this record copies of the said citation sent to ISOM BROWDER JNR. Wm. Meadour & the … returned a copy was delivered to the said ISOM BROWDER & William Meadour it was therefore ordered by the court that publication be made in the Raleigh Register giving notice to the other Defendants to appear and answer to the said petition at February session of our said Court 1831 Clement Whittmore One of the Defendants filed his answer which is herewith sent as part of this record the Cause was therefore by the court continued with the 4 Monday of May in the year 1831 at which time the parties by their attorneys appeared before our said Court and the following orders were made  Elizabeth Browder & others v ISOM BROWDER JR. & others Petition for probate of the last will and Testament of ISOM BROWDER the Eldest  It appearing to the … of the Court that due advertisement has been made agreeable to the former Order of this Court as to all the absent Defendants & that all the Other Defendants have been duly served with process  A paper writing being filed in this office & marked A purporting to be the last will & testament of ISOM BROWDER the Eldest was therefore offered for probate by Clement Whittmore and his wife Martha, Elizabeth Browder admr. of Augustin Browder decd.  Also Milton A. Browder Isom Browder the youngest Nancy Browder Augustin Browder Elizabeth Browder the younger the two last of whom are minors and prefer this paper by their next friend Elizabeth Browder Sen. and Natl. Roach his wife Tabitha (sic) the probate of which paper writing was offered by ISOM BROWDER JR. Wm. Meadour adm. of Nancy Thrasher decd. Isaac Clark & his wife Talitha, Augustin Fears a minor who offers said probate by his Grant … Wm. Meadour  Wherefore it is ordered by the count court that a Jury be sworn and confirmed to try whether the said paper writing marked A is the last will & Testament of said ISOM BROWDER the Eldest  Wherefore the following good and Lawful men to wit “Jas [?] Isaac Cummins Isaac Wilson Thomas Martin Wm. Taylor James McCallum Geo Delwart James Walker Danl. E. Garret Billy Martin Jno. Lemons James Young” who being duly confirmed & sworn a Jury to  speak the truth upon their oaths of the matters written   Contained on the same day & year the Jury then & there being empowered & charged as aforesaid for their record & say they … the paper writing offered for probate & marked A & in the following words & figures-In the name of God amen I ISOM (sic) BROWDER of Guilford County State of North Carolina being of sound & perfect  memory & knowing that it is ordained for all men once to die and having pleased God to bless me with a few worldly goods do give and dispose of them in manner & form following Item  I leave to my beloved wife TALITHA BROWDER during her natural life or widowhood the land & plantation whereon I now live together with all the household furniture whatsoever & the following horses Bloom, Lockit Kiddy Gratis & Dolsey all my stock of cattle sheep & Hogs and eight negroes towit Will Cezar Jenny Milly Andrew Peter Betty and Daniel and their increase and after her decease or widowhood I direct that all my personal estate not hereafter devised and its increase to be equally divided amongst and between my living issue Item  I give and bequeath unto my son ISOM BROWDER the land whereon I now live containing by estimation twelve hundred and Eighty acres likewise my English horse Jack the bachelor only deducting one third part of the earnings of the said horse to my wife for keeping the said horse in order and maintaining him and collecting the money arising from the said horse covering which said money I desire may be put to interest under bond with sufficient security so that my said son may receive the same at his arriving at the age of Twenty one years to him his heirs and assigns forever.  I likewise direct that my wife do school and have my children educated out of the profits of my Estate I direct that my Executor sell on six months credit my waggon and such of my Team as may not have been heretofore given and my tract of land purchased of Thomas Brandon containing by estimation one  hundred and fifty acres and that all my just debts be paid from the monies arising from such sale and the residue and such moneys that may be due to me meaning outstanding debts & which may remain from the above said sale after my just debts is paid I do give to my wife TALITHA BROWDER Lastly I appoint my beloved wife Executrix and my friends Abraham Womack John Robertson and William Bethell Executors of this my last will in witness whereof I have hereunto set my hand & seal this 27th day of September 1779.  signed & sealed in presence of  ISHAM BROWDER (seal)

I give to my daughter Talitha Cox Browder my gray horse purchased of John Bolinger the earnings of said horse from couring I desire that my executrix & Executors do maintain the said horse out of the crops made by my negros as it may be a clear profit to her and heirs forever  Is the  Last  Will and Testament of ISOM BROWDER the eldest From which judgment the Deft prays an appeal to the next superior court of Law to be holden for the county aforesaid on the 5th  Monday after the 4th in September next and along with them  Samuel Holson and John O’Aurial their … for prosecuting the same which being granted by the court the said Samuel Holson & John O’Aurial enter into hand accordingly which … is Witnesseth transmitted as part of this record. Declared to be the last will and testament of ISHAM BROWDER, dcsd. only executed to pass his personal estate, but not duly executed to pass his real estate and that the said ISHAM BROWDER, dcsd. did thereby bequeath his personal estate but did not thereby devise his real estate, and it is adjudged by the court that the said paper writing marked A- is the last will & testament of the said ISHAM BROWDER, dcsd. duly executed to pass his personal estate but not his real estate and it is further adjudged by the court that said title is duly proven to pass said ISHAM BROWDER’S personal estate but not his real estate.  It is ordered by the court that a writ of provedendo issue to the Justices of the county court to admit the foregoing will to record. St of N Car. Superior Court of Law, Rockingham Co. Fall term 1833. 

What exactly the meaning of this final court decision is, does not seem to be quite obvious, at least to an intelligent mind.  Like any other Will, the Will of Capt. Isham Browder appears to pass everything to future generations effectively.  It is, in fact, quite specific even if he did acquire more land after it was written. 

First, he bequeathed his entire estate, including the home and plantation, furniture, etc, plus five horses, all stock and all slaves as well as their children, to Talitha, as long as she lived, or as long as she remained a widow. 

Then, after her death, or the end of her widowhood, he bequeathed that his personal estate “not otherwise devised and its increase” to be equally divided among his living descendants.  So, everything not specifically mentioned in his Will concerning his personal estate, thereafter, was to be equally divided among all of his living children. 

Second, his eldest son, Isham, was to receive the home and plantation, containing at that time about 1,280 acres at that time, plus his English horse and the profits from said horse, except for maintenance costs.  Isham was to receive said profits at the age of 21 and, obviously, was to receive the rest of the property at the death of his mother, or at the end of her widowhood.  At the very least, if read correctly, it stipulated that Isham would receive the two plots, then thought to total 1,280 acres.  That Talitha herself obviously changed things with her purchase, along with Augustine as an infant, of the 640 acre plot from John Browder, demonstrates that she believed that she had the right to adjust things as she saw fit.  It also demonstrates that Isham was definitely deceased by the time she made this purchase. 

Third, all of Isham’s children were to be educated utilizing the profits from his estate.  Fourth, Isham’s wagon and team were to be sold, along with a tract of land of about 150 acres, in order to pay all of his just debts.  Once these debts were paid, Talitha was to receive the balance of the profits of this sale herself. 

Finally, he bequeathed his daughter, Talitha, one gray horse and the profits from said horse entirely clear of any debt or penalties. 

So, the bulk of the estate was to remain with Talitha until it was specified to go to Isham C. Browder, after the just debts of Capt. Isham Browder were paid.  He bequeathed his first daughter a horse.  Everything else concerning his personal estate not specifically mentioned, was to be divided among all living children, including Isham and Talitha, equally.  What might have actually been left, if anything, of his personal estate to be divided after Talitha died and Isham received his share, is uncertain.  But evidently, it wasn’t much, if anything, or relatives surely would not have sued for some of his real estate.  One can plausibly argue  that Isham had intended for all of his real estate to go to his son, Isham, even though he only specified the 1,280 acres he was thought to have at the time of the writing of the will.  Otherwise, surely he might have stipulated that at least some real estate be divided between all of his heirs.  But he made no such stipulation.  So, the fact that he (and Talitha) acquired more land after this will was written is irrelevant.  But that is not how the court saw it. 

Regardless, the following are copies of the page covers that show Isham’s middle initial as “C.”. 

Still, once again, everything was apparently done according to Isham’s Will as long as Talitha lived.  Certainly, some of the children felt essentially left out, without adequate inheritance.  But there was no conspiracy against them involving Talitha or Isham C. Browder.  She preserved at least one of Isham’s original Wills, after all (and, probably, both).  There was, in fact, no reason for anyone to know what Isham’s will was until she passed away, since she had control of the property as long as she lived and remained a widow anyway.  And for all she knew, the correct Will had been duly recorded as it should have been (and maybe it was, but had become missing).  She had no way of knowing that a backlog had existed concerning the recording of Wills and, perhaps, Isham’s Will had not been recorded, as she rightfully believed it had been. 

From what we now know, it is practically a miracle that Isham’s will was ever known in the first place.  The fact that she preserved it ensured that it would be known and followed.  She was not concealing his Will; she was preserving it.  And she acted accordingly to the best of her ability. 

Still, things did not turn out quite as she had hoped.  Although Isham C. Browder had asked for an appeal, he was never to get that far.  The appeal would have been held during the fall of 1832.  But, instead, Isham died early in 1832.  I do believe that Isham died, probably, due to the stress of the court battles and family squabbles.  That seems obvious. But he had really slighted no one if one looks at all of the land transactions, especially Elizabeth Browder, who apparently is the one who found the 1779 will and initiated the court case.  Again, Isham C. Browder never so much as touched the 640 acre plot that Elizabeth and her children resided upon, so to speak.  It was presumed to be theirs.  He further made other land transactions with the Whittemores with no apparent issues between them.  That he made none with any of his other sisters is simply because they had all moved away to Georgia, and one was already deceased. 

In any case, although it is said that his son, Richard, carried on said appeal, it seems that this appeal changed nothing with reference to the court’s decision.  The court had made its decision.  Capt. Isham Browder had obviously intended for his property to be passed on to his descendants.  Talitha Browder obviously took steps to ensure that it would be, mainly by remaining there when she might instead have left the area to go with her daughters to Georgia.  But following her death, family members sued, and that ensured that the decision would be placed into the hands of others, who decided that Isham’s Will did not effectively pass on said real estate property.  Frankly, it was a travesty for a man who had been an important figure in the county’s history. 

Perhaps the Will of 1778, if it truly existed (and I am certain that it did), was a better Will, and Isham C. Browder was prepared to present it to the court.  Obviously, Isham C. Browder felt that it was a better Will for some reason.  But that never happened, and it was never ordered recorded.  So, again, there is no real mystery concerning the death of Isham C. Browder, in my opinion.  It was caused by the stress of other family members suing him over his father’s estate, a suit which was wholly unnecessary.

State of North Carolina Rockingham County William Meadour & RICHARD BROWDER Administrators of ISHAM BROWDER Decd.  Report to Fall term 1835-  In obedience to the Order of the Honl. Court of Equity for the Court of Rockingham made at Spring term 1835 in the Case above in the Case above stated, I worshipfully submit the following report; I find that ISHAM BROWDER (the Elder) died in the year 1784 or 1785 having personally made & published his will bearing date the 27th September 1779, that at the date of said Will he had living, the following children to wit, Polly, ISHAM, Nancy Washington & Talitha, and that after the date of said Will & before his death he had two other Children born to wit Augustine & Patsey – having the said six children him surviving – and or I answered agreeable to the intentions of the Testatum the said ISHAM BROWDER (the Elder) as … in his said Will, each one of his or her … entitled to one sixth part of the money arising from the Estate in the Hand of the Defendant Clement Whittmore arising from the  … & Sale of the Slaves of the said Testator;  I find that Polly intermarried with Richard Thrasher & that they were both killed by the Indians about the year 1792, leaving an only child Nancy, who grew mature & died childless and Intestate about the year 1807 and that William Meadour one of the Complaintants administer on his Estate*  I find that ISHAM BROWDER (the younger) died in the year 1832 and that William Meadour & RICHARD BROWDER administered on his Estate—I find that Nancy Washington, intermarried with Cloud Thrasher who having died by whom she had two children, and after the death of said Cloud Thrasher both of said children died   Infants, and the said Nancy intermarried with William Fears by whom she had one of the Complaintants Augustine Fears, and she then died leaving her said Husband William Fears him surviving, who hath also since died having other children him surviving.  That Augustine Fears the Complaintant administered on the Estate of the said Cloud Thrasher … on the estate of the said William Fears and Nancy Fears – it is respectfully submitted to the Honl. Court … to say in right of which of his Intestates the said Augustine Fears shall …of said Nancy-I find that Talitha Whittmore with Isaac Thrasher & whom she had three children … having died the said Talitha intermarried with the Complaintant Isaac Clark and that Augustine Fears has administered on the Estate ….

Now, aside from the obvious mistakes in the above document, some facts need to be belatedly stated here.  According to the extant records, Thomas Henderson served as the first Clerk of Court for Guilford County, North Carolina.  He later became the first Clerk of Court for Rockingham County at the court’s first session, held in February of 1786, after the county of Guilford was divided.  After this division, Thomas Searcy was elected the first Clerk of Court for Guilford County on February 21, 1786.  John Hamilton became the third Clerk of Court for Guilford County, serving from 1790 through 1816.  Afterward, John Hanner became the Clerk, during which time A. E. Hanner served as Deputy Clerk.  In 1833 W. W.  Woodburn was elected, but only served for a short time, after which A. E. Hanner became Clerk, having been elected during the May term of 1836.  He served until the November term of 1837.

It appears that no wills were recorded until John Hanner became Clerk of Court in 1816 and that he divided out the accumulation of wills alphabetically and then arranged the wills under each letter chronologically and then started two books – one for the old wills and one for the current wills. . . .  There are 418 wills in Book A, so it must have taken quite a while to record this backlog.  How many had been lost??? (Stossen, p. 1).

Knowing this, it would indeed seem that the first three Clerks were certainly negligent in failing to record Wills properly, as Isham C. Browder had suggested (something, again, which would likely have been unknown to Talitha Browder).  And even when an effort was finally made, some Wills were never recorded in the will books at all, but, instead, remained in boxes for a long time or became lost.  We are indeed fortunate that the 1779 Will of Thomas Isham Browder survived at all.  And it survived only because Talitha Browder kept it.  She didn’t hide important papers; she preserved them.  

So, the 1778 Will might have been recorded, sometime by or after 1816, and that is obviously the Will that had been annexed and utilized by Richard Browder and William J. Meador.  And that would have been the Will that Isham C. Browder would have presented to the Court, had he lived long enough to do so.  Augustine would have known about this will too, and Isham stated that Augustine had written on it, or added to it.  But, it seems we will never really know, since it appears to no longer be extant.  That the 1779 will would obviously have superceded the 1778 will makes perfect sense.  But if the 1778 will had stipulated that Isham C. Browder was to receive all of Capt. Isham Browder’s real estate, regardless of acreage (because he only had about 150 acres at that time), then that might have allowed for a different decision to have been made by the court. 

One other thing is certain; not very long following the death of Talitha Browder, the descendants of Isham and Talitha finally began to go their separate ways.  The main groupings, the descendants of Isham C. Browder, and the descendants of Augustine Claiborne Browder, went to Tennessee and Georgia respectively (some having already done so).  The Whittemores remained in Rockingham County.  All others had already migrated to Georgia.  It is likely that they never again made any effort to contact one another, as there was much ill feeling now between the family groupings over the lawsuit concerning the Will of Thomas Isham Browder.  This would, perhaps, be the saddest part of our family’s legacy. 

The known children of Isham C. and Martha Browder were Mary Thompson “Polly” (1800-c1844), Isham Richard (c1801-1885) and an unknown male who did not live past young adulthood (b. c1804). 

Mary Thompson “Polly” Browder (1800-c1844) married William J. Meador on December 24, 1823 (Rockingham County Marriage Bonds Book [RCMBB] ?, p. 172/Ingmire, p. 46).  Clement Whittemore, Jr. stood as bondsman, and the ceremony was witnessed by Robert Galloway, Jr. 

Again, Isham C. Browder sold several tracts of land to both William J. Meador, and his son, Richard Browder, thus dividing his land between them.  However, Richard received the bulk of Isham’s land in this way, as he was deeded more of it by his father.  

By 1837 William J. Meador himself had sold 229 acres of land to Luke Palmer.  Thereafter he and his wife, Mary, moved to Itawamba County, Mississippi.  It is obvious that they had moved prior to 1840, since they are not found in that census of Rockingham County.  Where they were in 1830 is not known as they are found in no census.  But the census taker of Rockingham County obviously missed several families that year for some reason, so they were presumably still there and probably taking care of her aging father, Isham C. Browder, who is also not listed in any census for that year. 

By 1860 the family is known to have moved on to Cherokee County, Texas, but without Mary, because she died in Itawamba County, Mississippi about 1844.  William J. Meador married (2) Narcissa Allen on January 16, 1845 in Itawamba County, Mississippi.

The 1850 census of Itawamba County, Mississippi (District 7) lists William J. Meador (50), farmer, along with his wife, Narcissa (37) and children, Thomas (23), Martha A. (19), Mary F. (17), William (15), Elizabeth (11), Zachariah (4), Jesse (2, m), and Lila (1, f).  The family’s real estate value in 1850 was $1,300.  William is listed as having been born in Virginia.  Narcissa is listed as having been born in Tennessee.  Thomas, Martha, Mary, and William are listed as having been born in North Carolina.  These would have been children of William Meador and Mary Thompson Browder.  Thus, the known children of William and Mary Meador were Thomas (b. c1827), Martha A. (b. c1831), Mary F. (b. c1833), and William (b. c1835).  The rest of the children are listed as having been born in Mississippi.  Elizabeth (b. c1839) would also have had to have been a daughter of William and Mary Meador.  So, Mary would have died sometime between 1840 and 1844.  The rest of these listed children would have been those of William and Narcissa.  No other members of the Browder family, or any other known family members, are listed as residing close to this family in 1850.

The 1860 census of Cherokee County, Texas (Beat 8) also lists Wm. J. Medor (60), along with his wife, Narcissa (48), and son, Monroe (8), as well as Hannah O’Neil (21), and Wm. J. Webb (8).  This census confirms that William Meador was born in Virginia and that Narcissa was born in Tennessee.  All other household members were born in Texas.

So, the known children of William and Narcissa Meador would have been Zachariah (b. c1846), Lila (b. c1848), Jesse (b. 1850), and Monroe (b. c1852). 

Select Bibliography:

Bell, Landon C. Cumberland Parish Lunenburg County, Virginia 1746-1816, Vestry Book 1746-1816; transcribed by: Thomas Walter Duda. Lunenburg County Virginia Marriages Registers of Births,

Marriages and Deaths by the Rev. John Cameron. Baltimore: Genealogical Publishing Company, Inc., 1974, pp. 299-320.

Bell, Landon C. The Old Free State A Contribution to the History of Lunenburg County and Southside Virginia, Vol. II. Richmond, Va.: The William Byrd Press, Inc. Printers. Copyright 1927 by Landon C. Bell.

Browder, Blanche Penland. Notes On the Browder Family of Tidewater Virginia, 1695-1850, Second Edition, Copyright: Blanche Penland Browder, printed in USA by Blanche Penland Browder.

Ingmire, Frances T. Rockingham County North Carolina Marriage Records, 1785-1868. Copyright Frances Terry Ingmire, St. Louis, MO, 1984.

Perdue, Michael. “Directory of Officials of Rockingham County, North Carolina 1786-1991”, Journal of Rockingham County History and Genealogy, December 1991.

Stoesen, Alexander R. Guilford County, A Brief History. The North Carolina Division of Archives and History, 1993; second printing, 2000.
